# GUIDE TO THE ECCLESIASTICAL TYPIKON, <a id="typikon-of-alexander-mikita-mukachevo-uzhhorod-1901"></a>

### which
### for the spiritually profitable use of youth
### was composed and augmented by:
## Dr. ALEXANDER MIKITA,
### Canon-Custodian of the Cathedral Church of Mukachevo.

### With the approval of the Eparchial Government
### published by:
## THE ECCLESIASTICAL-LITERARY CIRCLE
## OF THE SEMINARY OF UNGVÁR (UZHHOROD).

### SECOND EDITION.

**UNGVÁR (UZHHOROD)**  
**BOOK-PRINTING PRESS OF THE SOCIETY OF ST. BASIL THE GREAT**  
**1901**

# PREFACE TO THE READER

Just as man consists of soul and of body, so too any ecclesiastical service and the entire rite consists of two parts: of spirit and of body—that is, of the spiritual element (*elementum spirituale* [spiritual element]) and of the material element (*elementum materiale* [material element]).

The spiritual element in ecclesiastical rites is internal and invisible, and it consists in this: what theological essence or symbolic significance one or another service possesses. The material element, on the other hand, is external and visible, and it consists in how one or another service ought to be performed decorously in church in accordance with its theological meaning.

Both elements stand in the closest union and in the greatest alliance between themselves, for they are the component parts of one and the same rite, such that the one sustains, fosters, and perfects the other, and the one cannot even exist in its fullness without the other. For the material element is the principal condition for the spiritual element, since the spiritual element receives from the material its subsistence, worth, and import, while the material element without the spiritual would be nothing other than an empty, dead mechanism.

And although in ecclesiastical services both elements are equally important, nevertheless in this booklet we shall not occupy ourselves with the symbolic meanings of individual holy rites—for all this is taught to youth in Pastoral Theology or in Liturgics—but here we shall speak only of how those services ought to be performed externally according to ecclesiastical prescriptions; that is, here we shall occupy ourselves solely with the material element of our rites.

This material element, these ritual prescriptions, are compiled in our public ecclesiastical books. Our ecclesiastical books are an evident witness to how great, how magnificent, how marvelous is the material element of our

=== LEAF p6 ===
[Book Page VI]

ecclesiastical rites. Yet not only are they witnesses, but what is more, they are themselves the rite-bearing books.

Now, since among us there are multiple ecclesiastical books, and many rites are compiled not in one, but in several books, therefore there must be a single guide to the rite-bearing books, there must be a single director for the ecclesiastical services.

This guide, or better to say, this director, is: the **Typikon**.

For through the Typikon a key is given to youth for the use of our ecclesiastical books, and at the same time there is presented therein an anthology of ritual prescriptions.

To possess this key, to know these prescriptions, and according to their direction to perform all services in unity and uniformity (*in unitate et uniformitate* [in unity and uniformity]) is a conscientious and binding duty for all those to whom the celebration of divine services is entrusted, or will be entrusted. Furthermore, to speak of these prescriptions as of little consequence is great frivolity; for the purpose of the rite and of ritual prescriptions is the glorification of God and the salvation of men; and in a matter of such great importance there can be no discussion of insignificant things. Already in the Old Testament God revealed this: *“Cursed is every man that continues not in all the words of this law, to do them”* (Deut. 27:26 LXX [V Moses 27:26]).

However, since such a guiding and systematic booklet, in which this material element of our holy rites might be found briefly set down, has hitherto not been found among us either in the theological seminary or in the cantors’ and teachers’ preparatory institute (*praeparandia*), therefore I ventured to write such a work for the spiritually profitable use of our youth.

My aim was likewise none other than through this booklet to be of assistance to our alumni, our future! For up to this time, as is well known, for the most part they learned the holy rites solely from the oral instruction of professors; and this oral instruction—without any textbook—was very arduous for them. But besides this, in the sense of the Latin proverb: *“Verba volant, scripta manent”* [Spoken words fly away, written words remain], with many it also happened that in practical life they forgot these ritual instructions, or mistakenly adapted and distorted them into something else, so that by not keeping the ecclesiastical prescriptions before their eyes they testified to the truth of these words: *“Graeca per Ausoniae fines sine lege vagantur”* [Greek rites wander without law throughout the borders of Ausonia].

=== LEAF p7 ===
[Book Page VII]

Therefore, esteemed reader, in these remarks you have learned my aim and intention, lest you should think that through this booklet I desire to uproot the antiquity and antiquities (*antiquitates* [antiquities]) of our Church and introduce novelty and novelties (*novitates* [novelties]) according to my own opinion—oh no!—this intention is far from me. I keep in mind the words of the Universal Chief Hierarch of blessed memory, Pope Pius IX, who in the Bull *“Omnem sollicitudinem”* (May 3, 1874)[^1] expresses himself thus: *“Nemini prorsus, hac Sancta Sede inconsulta, fas esse in re liturgica vel leviores innovationes peragere”* [It is absolutely unlawful for anyone, without consulting this Holy See, to carry out even rather slight innovations in liturgical matters]; and further: *“Nec ullius momenti est, quod ad fucum faciendum adjicitur, nimirum hujusmodi liturgicas innovationes proponi, ut orientalis ritus expurgetur et ad nativam integritatem restituatur”* [Nor is it of any weight whatsoever what is added to create a pretext—namely, that liturgical innovations of this kind are proposed so that the Eastern rite may be purified and restored to its native integrity]. And I will sincerely say that I desire only what others also ought to desire, namely: *“Ut tollatur abusus, et maneat usus”* [That abuse may be removed, and customary usage may remain].

In conclusion, I recall also that in the compilation of this booklet I made use of various works, which I shall indicate in their proper place.

I sought much in them, but found not everything; and I found much, but recorded not everything; and what I have recorded, I recognize that for the most part I recorded imperfectly and with shortcomings. For the questions which I treat in this booklet are among us hitherto for the most part of such a kind that everyone will speak of them in different ways; and therefore do not wonder at me, esteemed reader, if I say that among us the Typikon is hitherto such a discipline in which: *“Nemo sibi satis”* [No one is sufficient unto himself].

May Divine Grace likewise prosper this booklet and its second edition, so that it may be of benefit to our youth and that through it all our need and deficiencies in ritual matters may be diminished in a short time. For in this manner the defects of this booklet will also be diminished, and I hope that already its third edition will be more complete, for the readers of this booklet as true lovers of the rite will lovingly remind me of my mistakes.

Commending myself to our Protectress and Intercessor, the Queen of Heaven, the all-pure, most blessed, and immaculately conceived Most Holy Mother of God and Ever-Virgin Mary, I remain

In Ungvár (Uzhhorod), on the feast of St. Panteleimon the Healer, 1900.

**THE AUTHOR.**

[Blank Leaf]

## SECTION II
**§ 137.** On Liturgical Celebrations — 173

### CHAPTER I
**§ 138.** On the Divine Liturgies in General — 174

#### MEMBER I
#### Order of the Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom
**§ 139.** (a) On the Proskomide — 175  
**§ 140.** (b) Order of the Liturgy of the Catechumens — 182  
**§ 141.** (c) Order of the Liturgy of the Faithful — 187

#### MEMBER II
#### On the Liturgy of St. Basil the Great

# INTRODUCTION

## § 1. Concept, Principles, and Division of the Typikon

Since the worship of God is not only internal, but must also be manifested externally, holy Mother Church hath therefore established and appointed external services for the worship of God. A service is nothing other: *quam veritatis religioso-theoreticae practica professio* [than the practical profession of religious-theoretical truth], that is, the practical confession of theoretical supernatural truth. For example, Baptism is the confession of redemption and justification.

The services that are prescribed by the Church for Sundays and weekdays, as well as for feast days and ordinary days, are compiled in their completeness in the ecclesiastical books; they are divided into private and public; they consist of multiple parts; and these parts consist of diverse liturgical actions, symbols, and chants.

Public services are divided into 3 parts: (α) Into pre-liturgical (*ante-liturgicae*), that is, those preceding the Divine Liturgy, and these are: Vespers, Compline, the Midnight Office, Matins, the Hours, and Typika (*Obidnytsia*)[^3]; (β) Into liturgical (*ceremoniae liturgicae*), concerning the three Liturgies, namely: of St. John Chrysostom, of St. Basil the Great, and of St. Gregory the Dialogist; (γ) And into services following the Divine Liturgy, and these are of the Holy Mysteries, Ecclesiastical Sanctifications, and Blessings (*ceremoniae Sacramentorum et Sacramentalium*).

The pastor must celebrate the holy services: (α) Intelligibly, that is, with distinct pronunciation and with a decent voice; (β) In godly fear, that is, with an interior spirit for the profit of the people; (γ) In unity with other pastors and with ecclesiastical ordinances; therefore the Council of Trent states: “If anyone should say that the received and approved rites of the Church

=== LEAF p26 ===
[Book Page 2]

may be omitted by any pastor at his own pleasure without sin, or changed into other new ones, let him be anathema.”[^4] (δ) Uniformly, that is, without any diminution or addition, and at that very time which hath been appointed by the eparchial authority.

The systematic collection of all church services constitutes a rite (*complexus ordinatus omnium ceremoniarum est ritus*). The Typikon (*in Greek Τυπικὸν, from the word τύπος = forma, praescriptio, regula* [form, prescription, rule]): (α) Objectively is the collection of all those prescriptions, that is, rules, according to which the sacred services must be celebrated (*est complexus regularum, juxta quas ceremoniae administrari possunt ac debent* [is the collection of rules according to which ceremonies can and ought to be administered]); (β) Subjectively is the science which anyone acquires for himself if he learns those prescriptions, that is, the rules. Those prescriptions are taken either: (α) from the church books; or (β) from the decrees of the Congregation of Sacred Rites (*Congregatio SS. Rituum*); or (γ) from customary law (*consuetudo*).

Just as services are divided into 3 parts, so also prescriptions are divided into 3 parts, namely: (α) into prescriptions concerning pre-liturgical services; (β) into prescriptions concerning liturgical services; and (γ) into prescriptions concerning services of the Holy Mysteries and other Ecclesiastical Sanctifications and Blessings.

In accordance with this, we also divide our Typikon first into 2 principal parts: and namely, in Part I we shall speak concerning services in general, and in Part II concerning services in particular. This Part II we furthermore divide into 3 sections: and namely, in Section I we shall speak concerning pre-liturgical services; in Section II concerning liturgical services; and in Section III concerning services of the Holy Mysteries and other sacred actions.

# PART I. ON SERVICES IN GENERAL

Divine services are celebrated according to feasts or according to ordinary church days from the church books, and consist of diverse parts: therefore in this part we shall speak:

=== LEAF p27 ===
[Book Page 3]

1) concerning the Menologion; 2) concerning the church books; and 3) concerning the technical terms by which the diverse psalms, hymns, and ecclesiastical chants are designated.

## CHAPTER I. § 2. On the Ecclesiastical Menologion in General

Just as in the Old Testament the people of Israel had a twofold year: (α) the civil year, which began with the month of Tishri in autumn; and (β) the ecclesiastical year, which began with the month of Nisan in spring: so also in the New Testament the Christian people have a twofold year: (α) the civil year, which begins on the first day of January; and (β) the ecclesiastical year, which begins on the 1st day of September.

The ecclesiastical year consists of: (α) Days dedicated to the worship of God, and specifically to the worship of the most important acts of God concerning the redemption of the human race, that is, Feasts of the Lord; (β) Days dedicated to the veneration of the Most Holy Virgin Mary, that is, Marian Feasts; (γ) Days dedicated to the veneration of the Saints, that is, Feasts of the Saints. But since every greater feast is already announced beforehand by holy Church, and holy Mother Church is occupied with it for a considerable time even after its celebration, therefore: (δ) the ecclesiastical year consists also of Forefeasts and Afterfeasts; (ε) Furthermore, it consists of days which holy Mother Church hath established for the repentance and sanctification of the human race, that is, fasting days; (ζ) And finally, it consists of days on which mankind entreats God for those who have “fallen asleep in faith and in the hope of resurrection,” that is, days for the departed.

The focal point of the entire ecclesiastical year is the Resurrection of Christ, even as the Resurrection of Christ is the seal of the redemption of the human race. “And if Christ be not risen,” saith the Apostle, “then is our preaching vain, and your faith is also vain” (1 Cor. 15:14). And therefore His memorial is celebrated every week on Sunday, and all other feasts receive their brightness and warmth from this feast; in a word: what the sun is among the stars, that the Feast of Pascha is among the other feasts.

=== LEAF p28 ===
[Book Page 4]

The sequential order, that is, the arrangement of the church year according to diverse feasts, is called the “Menologion” (*Misiatsoslov*), in Greek: “*Heortologion*” [Ἑορтоλόγιον].

Furthermore, feasts are variously divided,[^5] namely:

1. In regard to time (*ratione temporis*): (α) into those which do not fall in the Menologion at the same time or on that same day, but their celebration depends upon Pascha, which falls on one of the Sundays between the 22nd day of March and the 25th day of April, and therefore these are called movable feasts; (β) into those which fall in each year in the Menologion on that same day and on that same date of the month, and therefore are called immovable feasts.
2. In regard to exterior sanctification (*ratione exterioris sanctificationis*), they are divided: (α) into holy days of obligation (*festa fori*), on which the pastor is bound to celebrate the Divine Liturgy for the people (*praeceptum missam pro populo applicare*), and the people are bound to hear the Divine Liturgy (*praeceptum missam audire*); (β) into feast days of devotion (*festa chori*), on which the pastor is bound to celebrate the Divine Liturgy for the people, but the people nevertheless are not bound to hear the Divine Liturgy; (γ) into ordinary days, on which neither the pastor is bound to celebrate the Divine Liturgy for the people, nor are the people bound to hear the Divine Liturgy.
3. In regard to the object celebrated (*ratione objecti celebrati*), they are divided: (α) into Feasts of the Lord (*cultus latriae* [worship of adoration]); (β) into Marian Feasts (*cultus hyperdouliae* [veneration of highest honour]); and (γ) into Feasts of the Saints (*cultus douliae* [veneration of honour]).
4. In regard to the liturgical rite (*ratione ritus liturgici*), into: (α) great; (β) middle; and (γ) lesser feasts. — Great feasts have Great Vespers with 10 or 8 stichera with an entrance, and sometimes with a Litiya; Matins with a Polyeleos, Gospel, and the Great Doxology. Middle feasts have Vespers with 8 stichera with an entrance, but without a Litiya; and Matins with a Polyeleos, Gospel, and the Great Doxology. Lesser feasts have Vespers with 6 stichera, but without an entrance; at Matins there is no Polyeleos, the canon is on 6, but they have the Great Doxology, and therefore are called “doxology feasts” (*doxologoumena* [δοξоλογούμενα]).

=== LEAF p29 ===
[Book Page 5]

Besides feast days there are also non-festal days, on which we celebrate the memory of lesser Saints having 3 stichera at Vespers and a canon on 4 at Matins.

## § 3. On the Menologion of Movable Feasts

Pascha is the focal point of the movable feasts. It can fall from March 22 unto April 25, that is, on one of these 35 days. — The “Paschalia”[^6] is the unfailing index of the day of the Pascha of Christ and of all festal days which depend upon Pascha. — In the Paschalia, the greatest importance is held by the boundary key (*kliuch hranychnyi*) or boundary letter. The boundary keys are letters of the alphabet which show on which day and month, in what year, the Pascha of Christ will fall. — Thus the earliest day of Pascha, March 22, hath the key A, March 23 hath B, and so forth in order. The last and latest day of Pascha, April 25, hath the key Ѿ (*Ot*).

Together with the boundary key there is also found the Dominical letter (*vrutsilito*, or Dominical letters, Sunday letter), and they are seven in number. These are alphabetical letters taken as numbers to designate the 7 days of the week: A, V, H, D, E, S, Z [А, В, Г, Д, Е, Ѕ, З].

The boundary key begins with the Nativity of Christ and governs throughout the entire year. And therefore, if we wish to know when in any given year the movable seasons fall, it is necessary to know beforehand what the boundary key is in that year; and knowing the key, everything that we wish to know will be unlocked by that key, if we only look in the tables at what stands opposite the governing key. The Dominical letter (*vrutsilito*), however, doth not begin together with the boundary key; it begins with the 1st day of March and governs unto the last day of February of the following year. — Therefore, the Sunday days in January and February of that second year stand in the Menologia always under the Dominical letter of the preceding year. The Menologion of movable feasts consists of: 1) the Forefeast of Pascha itself; 2) the Feast of Pascha; 3) the Afterfeast of Pascha; and 4) the 32 weeks after the Descent of the Holy Spirit, which are called “ordinary.”

I. To the Forefeast of Pascha belong: (α) The Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee, with the following week called “fast-free” (*pospolytsia*, *zahalnytsia*, *hyrtsia*),

=== LEAF p30 ===
[Book Page 6]

that is, when there is no fasting either on Wednesday or on Friday; (β) The Sunday of the Prodigal Son; (γ) Meatfare Saturday, which is a Saturday for the departed; (δ) Meatfare Sunday (or Sunday of the Second Coming of Jesus Christ); (ε) Cheesefare Saturday, on which is celebrated the memory of all our Venerable and God-bearing Fathers who shone forth in asceticism (*in ascesi*). On that day is celebrated the canon of St. Theodore the Studite, in which Odes 1–6 glorify those holy Fathers who were ascetics, Ode 7 the holy Women, and Odes 8 and 9 the holy Pastors of the Church; (ϛ) Cheesefare Sunday, or the Sunday of the Expulsion of the First-Created Adam from the Paradise of Delight; (η) The First Saturday of the Forty Days (*Chetyredesiatnytsia*), when we celebrate the memory of the miracle of the kolyva wrought by the holy Great-Martyr Theodore the Tyro, who died in the year 306. This miracle occurred in the year 362 in Constantinople, in the time of Julian the Apostate, who wished to turn all Christians back unto paganism, and that in such wise that he commanded that all provisions found in the marketplace should be secretly sprinkled with the blood of pagan sacrifices. But St. Theodore appeared by night unto Eudoxius, Bishop of this city, and told him that he should forbid them to buy that, and commanded that wheat, that is, grain boiled in pots, called “*kollyva*” [κολλύβα], should be given unto the needy; (ϑ) The First Sunday of the Fast, when is also celebrated the memory of the triumph of Orthodoxy over the Iconoclasts, therefore it is also called the “Sunday of Orthodoxy”; (ι) The Second Saturday of the Forty Days, and for the departed; (κ) The Second Sunday of the Holy Great Fast. The Synod of Zamość (in the year 1720) forbade the celebration on that day of the memory of Gregory Palamas, the heretic[^7]; (λ) The Third Saturday of the Holy Fast, for the departed; (μ) The Third Sunday of the Forty Days, which is called “Veneration of the Precious Cross” (*kyriake tes stauroproskyneseos* [κυριακὴ τῆς σταυροпροσκυνήσεως]); (ν) The Fourth Saturday of the Holy Fast, for the departed; (ξ) The Fourth Sunday of the Fast, in which we celebrate the memory of our Venerable Father John of the Ladder; (ο) The Fifth Thursday of the Holy Forty Days, on which day at Matins (with us, on the evening of the 7th hour) the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete is sung; (ρ) The Fifth Saturday of the Holy Fast, with the Akathist to the Most Holy Virgin; (π) The Fifth Sunday of the Holy Fast, in which is celebrated the memory of our Venerable Mother Mary of Egypt; (σ) The Sixth Saturday...

---

=== LEAF p31 ===
[Book Page 7]

...of the Holy Fast, or of Lazarus; (τ) The Sixth Sunday of the Holy Fast, or Palm Sunday (*“kyriake ton baion”* [κυριακὴ τῶν βαΐων] or *“heorte baiophoros”* [ἑορτὴ βαϊοφόρος], *festum Palmarum*, from the Greek word *“baia”* [βαΐα]—boughs of palm, olive, or willow); (υ) Great Week, with Great Thursday, Friday, and Saturday.

II. The Sunday of Pascha (from the Hebrew word *“pesach”* = *transitus* [passing over]), with the canon of St. John Damascene. Among Christians there is a twofold Pascha: (α) Pascha *“staurosimon”* [σταυρώσιμον, of the Cross], when Jesus Christ passed over from life unto death, that is, Great Friday; and (β) Pascha *“anastasimon”* [ἀναστάσιμον, of the Resurrection], when He passed over from death unto life, that is, the Bright Resurrection. This entire week is called Bright, but at the same time it is fast-free (*pospolyta*).

III. To the Afterfeast of Pascha belong: (α) The First Sunday, that is, Thomas Sunday or the Sunday of Antipascha (*anti pascha* [ἀντὶ πάσχα]), that is, in place of Pascha, because on that Sunday the Feast of Pascha is repeated for the first time. For that reason that Sunday is also called the “Sunday of Renewal,” or the “New Sunday”; (β) The Second Sunday, that is, of the holy Myrrh-Bearing Women and of holy Joseph of Arimathea with Nicodemus; (γ) The Third Sunday, that is, of the Paralytic; (δ) The Wednesday of Mid-Pentecost (that is, the 25th day from Easter Sunday, in Greek: *“he mesopentekoste”* [ἡ μεσοπεντηκοστή]). This feast is observed for 8 days; (ε) The Fourth Sunday, that is, of the Samaritan Woman; (ϛ) The Fifth Sunday, that is, of the Blind Man; (η) The Wednesday of the sixth week, on which is performed the Apodosis of Pascha; (ϑ) The Thursday of the sixth week after Pascha, that is, the Ascension of Jesus Christ, with the canon of St. John Damascene; (ι) The Sixth Sunday, that is, of the Holy and God-bearing Fathers of the First Ecumenical Council in Nicaea; (κ) The Saturday before the Descent of the Holy Spirit, which is for the departed; (λ) The Sunday of Pentecost, that is, the Descent of the Holy Spirit, with the canon of St. Cosmas. After the Divine Liturgy, at Vespers the kneeling prayers are concluded; (μ) The Monday of the Holy Spirit and the Feast of the Holy Trinity. The service of the Feast of Pentecost is celebrated throughout the entire following week, which is fast-free (*pospolyta*); and on Saturday the Apodosis of that feast takes place.

IV. There are 32 Sundays after the Descent of the Holy Spirit,[^8] of which: (α) The first is the Sunday of All Saints. On this Sunday

=== LEAF p32 ===
[Book Page 8]

...the *Octoechos* begins with Tone 8 and the fast unto the Feast of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul, which, however, is not always of equal length, for it can last at the least 7 days, and at the most 6 weeks; (β) The Thursday of the second week is in Galicia the Feast of the Holy Eucharist; (γ) The Second Sunday after the Descent of the Holy Spirit: the Gospel for this day is “On the manifestation of Jesus Christ and the calling unto Apostleship of Peter, Andrew, James, and John”; (δ) The Friday of the third week is in Galicia the Feast of the “Compassion of the Most Holy Virgin at the Passion of Jesus Christ”; (ε) In July, on a Sunday near the 16th day of that month, we celebrate the “Memory of the Holy Fathers of the Six Ecumenical Councils,” and that in such wise: if the 16th day falls on a Monday, Tuesday, or Wednesday, then the “Council of the Holy Fathers” is celebrated on the preceding Sunday; but if on a Thursday, Friday, or Saturday, then on the following Sunday; finally, if the 16th day falls on a Sunday, then we celebrate the memory of the holy Fathers on that very same Sunday; (ϛ) In September, around the 14th day, are celebrated the Sunday before the Exaltation of the Precious Cross and the Sunday after the Exaltation of the Precious Cross; (η) In October, around the 11th day, is celebrated: the “Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the Seventh Ecumenical Council held in Nicaea in the year 787”;[^9] (ϑ) In December, on the penultimate Sunday before the Nativity of Christ, is celebrated the “Memory of the Holy Forefathers” (*Dominica Protopatrum*). By “Forefathers” we understand: Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. (Some understand Adam and Eve, but erroneously); (ι) The last Sunday before the Nativity of Christ is called the “Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the Old Testament” (*Dominica SS. Patrum*). By “Holy Fathers” we understand here all the God-fearing men of the Old Testament, beginning

=== LEAF p33 ===
[Book Page 9]

...from Adam even unto holy Joseph, the Betrothed of Mary; (κ) On the first Sunday after the Nativity of Jesus Christ we celebrate the memory of holy Joseph, the Betrothed of Mary, David the King, and James, the Brother of the Lord according to the flesh; (λ) In January we celebrate the Sunday before Theophany and after Theophany. Mention is also made of the “Sunday of the Holy Guardian Angels” and of the “Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the Council of Florence,” but the day of these feasts is not appointed; (μ) The 32nd Sunday after the Descent of the Holy Spirit is “of Zacchaeus.”[^10]

## § 4. On the Menologion of Immovable Feasts

Of the immovable feasts we shall adduce only the most important. The focal point among these feasts is the Nativity according to the flesh of our Lord Jesus Christ, with which many of the immovable feasts stand in close connection. The Menologion of immovable feasts begins with the 1st day of September and ends with the 31st day of August.

I. In September the most notable of the immovable feasts are: (α) On the 1st: The Beginning of the Indiction, that is, of the New Year. The name “Indiction” is the Latin word *indictio*, *initium et finis* [beginning and end]. It is a period consisting of 15 years, which period was divided into three parts (*lustrum*), after which the Roman Emperors decreed a tax upon their subjects in the first days of each five-year period, and collected the same in the first days of each five-year period. The Church begins the new year on this day either because on this day Jesus came into the Jewish synagogue and there began to preach, and having taken the book of the Prophet Isaiah, read from it that place where it is written: “The Spirit of the Lord is upon Me” (Isa. 61:1, *Initium indictionis ab initio

=== LEAF p34 ===
[Book Page 10]

...praedicationis Jesu* [The beginning of the Indiction from the beginning of the preaching of Jesus]). Others, however, consider that the Church begins the new year on that day because, according to tradition, time began on that day (*Initium indictionis a principio temporis* [The beginning of the Indiction from the beginning of time]); (β) On the 6th: “Commemoration of the Miracle Wrought at Colossae by the Archistrategos Michael.” This miracle was that the pagans wished to destroy the church of the holy Archistrategos Michael by means of a river which they directed against the church; but the holy Archangel Michael appeared, and striking the rock with his staff, made a passage for the river, which even now flows through this rock; (γ) On the 8th: The Nativity of the Most Holy Theotokos; (δ) On the 14th: The Exaltation of the Precious Cross. On the 16th: St. Josaphat; (ε) On the 26th: The Falling Asleep of the Holy Apostle and Evangelist John the Theologian; (ϛ) On the 28th: Our Venerable Father Chariton.

II. In the month of October the most notable feasts are the following: (α) On the 1st: The “Protection of the Most Holy Theotokos,” instituted in memory of how St. Andrew the Fool-for-Christ and his disciple Epiphanius, during the All-Night Vigil on October 1 in the year 1309, beheld in the air in Constantinople, in the Church of Blachernae, the Mother of God with Angels, Prophets, and Apostles, praying for the world and covering Christians with her veil. At this time the Saracens had attacked the Greeks and caused them great harm; but when the Greeks learned of this vision, they took courage and after a short time drove away the Saracens;[^11] (β) On the 6th: The holy and glorious Apostle Thomas; (γ) On the 9th: The holy Apostle James, son of Alphaeus; (δ) On the 18th: The holy Evangelist Luke; (ε) On the 23rd: The holy Apostle James, Brother of the Lord according to the flesh; (ϛ) On the 26th: The holy Great-Martyr Demetrius the Myrrh-Gusher. The Myrrh-Gusher (in Greek: *“Myroblytos”* [Μυροβλύτης] = *unguentum effundens* [pouring forth ointment], from the words *“myron”* [μύρον] = *unguentum* and *“blyo”* [βλύω] = *scaturio* [to gush forth]) is so called because from his relics flowed myrrh, that is, oil which was a medicine in certain sicknesses; (η) On the 28th: The holy Martyr Paraskeva, a Ruthenian nun and abbess, who, having left her homeland because of the Tatars, moved to Rome and died there in the year 1239, and was inscribed into the number of the Saints by Gregory X.

III. In the month of November the most notable feast days are the following: (α) On the 1st: The holy Wonderworkers and Unmercenaries Cosmas and Damian. These Saints were physicians who miraculously and not for money, but gratuitously healed people (*“Anargyros”* [Ἀνάργυρος] = unmercenary, *argento carens*, *mercedem non recipiens* [lacking silver, receiving no fee]); (β) On the 8th: The Synaxis of the Holy Archangel Michael, Guardian of Holy Mother Church

=== LEAF p35 ===
[Book Page 11]

...and of the other Bodiless Powers (Synaxis = *“Synaxis”* [Σύναξις], *conventus sacer fidelium solemnitatis causa* [a holy gathering of the faithful for the purpose of a solemnity]); (γ) On the 13th: Our Holy Father John Chrysostom; (δ) On the 14th: The Holy Apostle Philip. From this day begins the fast unto the Nativity of Jesus Christ; (ε) On the 16th: The Holy Apostle Matthew; (ϛ) On the 21st: The Entry (*Praesentatio*) into the Temple of the All-Pure Virgin; (η) On the 30th: The Holy Apostle Andrew the First-Called (*“Protokletos”* [Πρωτόκλητος] = first-called).

IV. In the month of December the most notable feasts are the following: (α) On the 4th: The Holy Great-Martyr Barbara; (β) On the 5th: St. Sabbas; (γ) On the 6th: St. Nicholas, Bishop of Myra in Lycia; (δ) On the 9th: The Immaculate Conception (*Immaculata Conceptio*) of the Most Holy Virgin Mary; (ε) On the 13th: The Holy Martyrs Eustratios, Auxentios, Eugenios, Mardarios, and Orestes; (ϛ) On the 25th: The Nativity of Jesus Christ; (η) On the 26th: The Synaxis of the Most Holy Virgin; (ϑ) On the 27th: St. Stephen, the First Martyr and Archdeacon.

V. In January the most notable feasts are: (α) On the 1st: The Circumcision of Jesus Christ (*Circumcisio Domini*) and St. Basil the Great; (β) On the 6th: Theophany (*Theophania*); (γ) On the 7th: The Synaxis of St. John the Baptist; (δ) On the 11th: St. Theodosius, the Initiator of Common Life (*Coenobiarcha*); (ε) On the 16th: The Veneration of the Precious Chains (*catena*) of the Holy Apostle Peter; (ϛ) On the 17th: St. Anthony the Great (*Eremita*); (η) On the 20th: St. Euthymius the Great; (ϑ) On the 25th: St. Gregory the Theologian; (ι) On the 27th: The Translation of the Relics of St. John Chrysostom; (κ) On the 30th: The Three Holy Hierarchs: St. Basil the Great, St. Gregory the Theologian, and St. John Chrysostom.

VI. In February the most notable feasts are: (α) On the 2nd: The Meeting (*Occursus*) of our Lord Jesus Christ with Simeon, at the same time both the Presentation of Jesus in the Temple (*Praesentatio Domini*) and the Purification of the Blessed Virgin Mary (*Purificatio Beatae Mariae Virginis*); (β) On the 24th: The First (in Jerusalem) and Second (in Emesa) Finding of the Head of St. John the Baptist.

VII. In March these are the most notable feasts: (α) On the 9th: The 40 Martyrs; (β) On the 25th: The Annunciation (*Annuntiatio B. M. Virginis*), at the same time also the Feast of the Incarnation of our Lord Jesus Christ (*Conceptio D. N. J. Christi*).

VIII. In April the most notable feasts are: (α) On the 23rd: St. George the Great-Martyr; (β) On the 25th: St. Mark the Evangelist; (γ) On the 30th: The Holy Apostle James, brother of St. John.

IX. In May the most notable feasts are: (α) On the 3rd: The Falling Asleep of St. Theodosius of the Caves; (β) On the 8th: St. John the Evangelist;

=== LEAF p36 ===
[Book Page 12]

(γ) On the 9th: The Translation of the Relics of St. Nicholas from Myra to the city of Bari; (δ) On the 10th: The Holy Apostle Simon; (ε) On the 21st: St. Constantine and St. Helen; (ϛ) On the 25th: The Third Finding of the Head of St. John the Baptist (*Comanae in Cappadocia*).

X. In June the most notable feasts are: (α) On the 11th: The Holy Apostle Bartholomew; (β) On the 19th: The Holy Apostle Jude, Thaddaeus; (γ) On the 24th: The Nativity of St. John the Baptist; (δ) On the 29th: The Holy Apostles Peter and Paul; (ε) On the 30th: The Synaxis of the Holy 12 Apostles.

XI. In July the most notable feasts are: (α) On the 2nd: The Deposition of the Precious Robe of our Most Holy Lady the Theotokos in Blachernae (*Depositio vel translatio pretiosae vestis B. M. Virginis in Blachernis*); (β) On the 5th: Our Venerable Father St. Athanasius of Athos; (γ) On the 10th: St. Anthony of the Caves; on the 15th: St. Vladimir; (δ) On the 20th: The Holy Prophet Elijah; on the 24th: St. Boris and St. Gleb; (ε) On the 25th: The Dormition of St. Anna; (ϛ) On the 27th: St. Panteleimon.

XII. In August: (α) On the 1st: The Procession of the Precious Wood of the Precious and Life-Giving Cross, and the memory of the Holy 7 Maccabean Martyrs. With this day begins the fast unto the Dormition of the Most Holy Virgin; (β) On the 6th: The Transfiguration (*Transfiguratio*) of our Lord Jesus Christ; (γ) On the 8th: St. Stephen, King of Hungary; (δ) On the 9th: The Holy Apostle Matthias; (ε) On the 15th: The Dormition of our All-Pure Lady the Theotokos (*Obdormitio et Assumptio B. M. Virginis*); (ϛ) On the 16th: The Translation from Edessa to Constantinople of the Icon Made without Hands (*divinitus fabricata imago*) of our Lord Jesus Christ; (η) On the 29th: The Beheading (*Decollatio*) of the Head of St. John the Baptist; (ϑ) On the 31st: The Deposition of the Precious Sash of the Most Holy Theotokos (*Depositio Zonae S.Smae Deiparae*).

## § 5. Which Are the Feasts of the Lord?

The Feasts of the Lord are the following: 1. The Conception, that is, the Incarnation of our Lord Jesus Christ.[^12] 2. The Nativity of Jesus Christ. 3. The Circumcision of Jesus Christ. 4. The Presentation in the Temple or Meeting (*“Hypapante”* [Ὑπαπαντή] = *occursus*) of our Lord Jesus Christ with Simeon. 5. The Baptism of Jesus Christ. 6. The Transfiguration of Jesus Christ. 7. The Entry of Jesus Christ into Jerusalem (*Dies Palmarum*). 8. The Death of Jesus Christ, that is, the Pascha of Death. 9. The Resurrection of Jesus Christ,

=== LEAF p37 ===
[Book Page 13]

that is, the Joyful Pascha. 10. The Ascension of Jesus Christ. 11. The Descent of the Holy Spirit. 12. The Monday of the Holy Spirit, when at the same time the Feast of the Holy Trinity is also celebrated. 13. The Procession of the Precious Wood of the Precious and Life-Giving Cross. 14. The Exaltation of the Precious Cross. 15. The Translation from Edessa to the City of Constantine of the Icon Made without Hands of Jesus Christ.

## § 6. Which Are the Marian Feasts?

The Marian Feasts are the following: 1. The Immaculate Conception of the Most Holy Virgin. 2. The Nativity of the Most Holy Virgin. 3. The Entry into the Temple of the Most Holy Virgin. 4. The Annunciation of the Most Holy Virgin. 5. The Purification of the Most Holy Virgin.[^13] 6. The Synaxis of the Most Holy Virgin. 7. The Dormition of the Most Holy Theotokos. 8. The Protection of the Most Holy Virgin. 9. The Deposition of the Precious Robe of the Most Holy Virgin. 10. The Deposition of the Precious Sash of the Most Holy Virgin.

Note: The feasts of the Annunciation and the Meeting are of a twofold nature: they are both Feasts of the Lord and Marian Feasts; nevertheless, the Annunciation is reckoned among the Marian Feasts, and the Meeting among the Feasts of the Lord.

## § 7. Which Are the Most Notable Feasts of the Saints?

Among the Saints are holy men and holy women. (α) Among men are the following choirs: Angels, Prophets, Apostles, Doctors, Hierarchs, Martyrs, Hieromartyrs, Venerables, Venerable-Martyrs, Confessors, Hieroconfessors, Venerable-Confessors. — (β) Among women: Women Martyrs, Venerable Women and Venerable-Women-Martyrs, Venerable and Righteous Virgins.

Among their feasts the most notable are the following: 1. The Synaxis of the Holy Archangel Michael and of the other Bodiless Powers. 2. The feasts of St. John the Baptist, namely: Conception, Nativity, Beheading, Synaxis, First, Second, and Third Finding of the Head of the Honorable John the Baptist. 3. The Holy Prophet Elijah. 4. The feasts of the Holy Apostles, especially the feasts of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul.

=== LEAF p38 ===
[Book Page 14]

5. The feasts of the Great Hierarchs, especially St. Nicholas and the Three Hierarchs. 6. The Holy Martyrs: Stephen, Demetrius, and George. 7. The feasts of our Venerable Fathers, especially St. Anthony and St. Theodosius. 8. The Holy Unmercenaries: Cosmas and Damian. 9. The feasts of holy women: St. Anna, St. Helen, St. Paraskeva, etc.

## § 8. Which Are the Decretal Feasts (Festa Fori) in the Eparchy of Mukachevo?

In the Eparchy of Mukachevo the decretal feasts are: 1. The Nativity of the Most Holy Theotokos. 2. The Exaltation of the Precious Cross. 3. The Protection of the Most Holy Virgin. 4. The Synaxis of St. Michael. 5. The Entry into the Temple of the Most Holy Virgin. 6. The Day of St. Nicholas. 7. The Nativity of Jesus Christ. 8. The Synaxis of the Most Holy Theotokos. 9. The Feast of St. Stephen the Archdeacon. 10. The Circumcision. 11. Theophany. 12. The Three Hierarchs. 13. The Meeting. 14. The Annunciation. 15. Holy and Great Friday. 16. The Sunday, Monday, and Tuesday of Pascha. 17. St. George. 18. The Ascension of Jesus Christ. 19. The Sunday and Monday of the Descent of the Holy Spirit. 20. The Feast of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul. 21. The Nativity of St. John the Baptist. 22. The Feast of the Holy Prophet Elijah. 23. The Transfiguration of Jesus Christ. 24. The Feast of St. Stephen, King of Hungary. 25. The Dormition of the Most Holy Theotokos. 26. The Beheading of the Honorable Head of John the Baptist.[^14]

## § 9. Which Are the Choral or Church Feasts (Festa Chori)?

According to the direction[^15] of the late Bishop of the city of Nagyvárad [Veliko-Varadin], Papp-Szilágyi, the following were at one time church feasts: 1. The Falling Asleep of St. John the Apostle and Evangelist Luke. 4. The Day of the Holy Apostle James, Brother of the Lord. 5. The Day of the Holy Great-Martyr Demetrius. 6. The Immaculate Conception of the Most Holy Virgin. 7. The Day of St. John Chrysostom. 8. The Day of the Holy Apostle Philip. 9.

=== LEAF p39 ===
[Book Page 15]

The Day of the Holy Apostle and Evangelist Matthew. 10. The Day of the Holy Martyr Catherine. 11. The Day of the Holy Apostle Andrew. 12. The Holy 14,000 Infant Martyrs Slain for Christ by Herod. 13. The Day of St. Anthony the Great. 14. The Day of St. Athanasius and Cyril. 15. The Day of St. Euthymius the Great. 16. The Translation of the Relics of St. John Chrysostom. 17. The Day of St. Theodore Stratelates.[^16] 18. The Day of St. Theodore the Tyro.[^17] 19. The First and Second Finding of the Honorable Head of St. John the Baptist. 20. The Day of the Holy 40 Martyrs. 21. The Day of the Holy Evangelist Mark. 22. The Day of the Holy Apostle James, Brother of St. John.[^18] 23. The Day of St. Athanasius.[^19] 24. The Day of St. John the Apostle and Evangelist.[^20] 25. The Day of St. Constantine and St. Helen. 26. The Third Finding of the Head of St. John the Baptist. 27. The Synaxis of the Holy 12 Apostles. 28. The Day of St. Mary Magdalene.[^21] 29. The Dormition of St. Anna. 30. The Day of the Holy Martyr Panteleimon. 31. The Day of the Holy Apostle Matthias.[^22][^23] But now the days of choral feasts depend upon eparchial determinations.

## § 10. Which Are the Great Feasts?

Great feasts are divided into 3 classes: (α) To the first belongs only Pascha, which is the “feast of feasts”; (β) To the second belong the 12 feasts: Nativity of the Theotokos, Entry, Annunciation, Dormition of the Most Holy Virgin, Nativity of Jesus Christ, Meeting, Theophany, Transfiguration, Entry into Jerusalem, Ascension, Descent of the Holy Spirit, and Exaltation of the Precious Cross; (γ) To the third class belong: The Circumcision of the Lord, Nativity and Beheading of John the Baptist, and the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul.

=== LEAF p40 ===
[Book Page 16]

Great feasts are designated in the Menologion by an enclosed cross 🞣, and the titles are usually printed in red letters. They are Feasts of the Lord and Marian Feasts. All these feasts have Great Vespers with a Litiya, and Matins with a Polyeleos, Gospel, and Great Doxology; besides this they have a Forefeast and Afterfeast, that is, the divine service of those feasts is not limited to the very day of the feast, but extends unto preceding and following days. For the most part, great feasts have one day of Forefeast each; only the Feast of the Nativity of Christ hath 5 days, and the Feast of Theophany 4 days. The Circumcision and the other feasts of the 3rd class do not have a Forefeast.

The Afterfeast likewise is not always the same, but is at times longer, at times shorter. — Thus the Afterfeast consists of one day after the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos; of four days after the Nativity of the Theotokos and the Entry into the Temple of the Theotokos; or of 6 days after the Nativity of Christ; or of 7 days after the Exaltation of the Precious Cross and after the Transfiguration of the Lord; or of 8 days after Theophany and after the Dormition of the Most Holy Theotokos. The Feast of the Circumcision and the other feasts of the 3rd class do not have an Afterfeast.

The last day of an Afterfeast is called the “Apodosis of the feast,” because on that day the celebration of the feast itself is for the most part taken. All Apodoses are “doxology” feasts.

## § 11. Which Are the Middle Feasts?

Middle feasts are designated in church books by the sign of a small cross: †, and they have at Vespers from the Kathisma “Blessed is the man” (*Блаженъ мужъ*) only the 1st antiphon, at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) stichera on 6 or on 8, a great entrance, and 3 paremias. At Matins they have a Polyeleos, Gospel, a canon on 8, and the Great Doxology.

These middle feasts are divided into two parts.

I. Among them are such feasts as are at the same time also decretal feasts, such as: 1. The Protection of the Most Holy Theotokos. 2. The Feast of the Holy Archangel Michael. 3. The Day of St. Nicholas. 4. The Feast of the Three Hierarchs. 5. The Feast of St. George. 8. The Feast of the Holy Prophet Elijah. 9. In Hungary: St. Stephen, Equal-to-the-Apostles, King of Hungary.

---

=== LEAF p41 ===
[Book Page 17]

II. There are also such feasts as are not decretal and are merely called “polyeleos feasts,” and that because at Matins they have a “Polyeleos.”  
Those feasts are:  
1. In the month of September: (α) On the 16th: St. Josaphat; (β) On the 26th: The Falling Asleep of the Holy Apostle and Evangelist John; (γ) On the 28th: Our Venerable Father and Confessor Chariton.  
2. In October: (α) On the 6th: The Holy Apostle Thomas; (β) On the 9th: The Holy Apostle James, son of Alphaeus; (γ) On the 18th: The Holy Apostle and Evangelist Luke; (δ) On the 26th: The Holy Great-Martyr Demetrius the Myrrh-Gusher; (ε) On the 28th: St. Paraskeva.  
3. In November: (α) On the 13th: St. John Chrysostom, Archbishop of Constantinople; (β) On the 14th: The Holy Apostle Philip; (γ) On the 16th: The Holy Apostle and Evangelist Matthew; (δ) On the 30th: The Holy Apostle Andrew the First-Called.  
4. In December: (α) On the 5th: Our Venerable Father Sabbas; (β) On the 9th: The Immaculate Conception of the Most Holy Virgin; (γ) On the 13th: The Holy Martyrs Eustratios, Auxentios, Eugenios, Mardarios, and Orestes.  
5. In January: (α) On the 11th: Our Venerable Father Theodosius; (β) On the 17th: Our Venerable Father St. Anthony the Great; (γ) On the 20th: Our Venerable Father Euthymius the Great; (δ) On the 25th: Our Holy Father Gregory the Theologian, Archbishop of Constantinople; (ε) On the 27th: The Translation of the Relics of our Holy Father John Chrysostom.  
6. In February: On the 24th: The First and Second Finding of the Head of St. John the Baptist.  
7. In March: On the 9th: The Holy 40 Martyrs.  
8. In April: (α) On the 25th: The Holy Apostle and Evangelist Mark; (β) On the 30th: The Holy Apostle James, brother of St. John the Evangelist.  
9. In May: (α) On the 3rd: The Dormition of our Venerable Father St. Theodosius, Hegumen of the Monastery of the Caves; (β) On the 8th: The Holy Apostle and Evangelist John; (γ) On the 9th: The Translation of the Honorable Relics of St. Nicholas the Wonderworker; (δ) On the 10th: The Holy Apostle Simon the Zealot; (ε) On the 21st: St. Constantine and St. Helen; (ϛ) On the 25th: The Third Finding of the Honorable Head of St. John the Baptist.  
10. In June: (α) On the 11th: The Holy Apostles Bartholomew and Barnabas; (β) On the 19th: The Holy Apostle Jude, Brother of the Lord.  
11. In July: (α) On the 2nd: The Deposition of the Precious Robe of the Most Holy Theotokos; (β) On the 5th: St. Cyril and St. Methodius, Enlighteners of the Slavs, and our Venerable Father Athanasius of Athos; (γ) On the 10th: The Memory of St. Anthony of the Caves; (δ) On the 15th: St. Vladimir;

=== LEAF p42 ===
[Book Page 18]

(ε) On the 24th: St. Boris and St. Gleb; (ϛ) On the 25th: The Dormition of St. Anna; (η) On the 27th: St. Panteleimon.  
12. In August: (α) On the 9th: The Holy Apostle Matthias; (β) On the 31st: The Deposition of the Sash of the Most Holy Virgin.

## § 12. Which Are the Lesser Feasts?

Lesser feasts are of a twofold kind:

I. There are such feasts as have at Vespers the customary kathisma, at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) stichera on 6 without an entrance, at Matins there is no Polyeleos, but a canon on 6 and the Great Doxology, that is, the hymn “Glory to God in the highest” (*Слава во вышнихъ Богу*) is sung and concludes with the “Trisagion,” as at Great Matins. — These feasts are designated by three dots with a semicircle on the left side ⸪), and they are called “doxology feasts” or “Saints with the Great Doxology.”

These are: 1. September 1: The Beginning of the Indiction; 2. Sept. 12: The Apodosis of the Nativity of the Most Holy Virgin; 3. Sept. 13: The Commemoration of the Renewal [Dedication] of the Church of our Lord Jesus Christ;[^24] 4. Sept. 23: The Conception of St. John the Baptist; 5. Sept. 21: The Apodosis of the Exaltation of the Precious Cross; 6. Oct. 23: St. James, Brother of the Lord; 7. Nov. 25: The Apodosis of the Entry; 8. Dec. 26: The Synaxis of the Most Holy Virgin;[^25] 9. Dec. 31: The Apodosis of the Nativity; 10. Jan. 7: The Synaxis of St. John the Baptist; 11. Jan. 14: The Apodosis of Theophany; 12. Feb. 9: The Apodosis of the Meeting; 13. Cheesefare Saturday, which is of All Venerable Fathers; 14. March 26: The Apodosis of the Annunciation; 15. Saturday of the Akathist; 16. Lazarus Saturday; 17. Great Saturday; 18. Mid-Pentecost Wednesday; 19. The Apodosis of Mid-Pentecost; 20. The Apodosis of Pascha (on Wednesday before Ascension); 21. The Apodosis of Ascension (on Friday before the Descent of the Holy Spirit); 22. Monday of the Holy Spirit;[^26] 23. The Apodosis of the Descent of the Holy Spirit (on Saturday before the Sunday of All Saints); 24. June 30: The Synaxis of the Holy 12 Apostles; 25. Aug. 1: The Procession of the Precious Wood of the Honorable and Life-Giving Cross; 26. Aug. 13: The Apodosis of the Transfiguration; 27. Aug. 16:

=== LEAF p43 ===
[Book Page 19]

The Translation from Edessa to Constantinople of the Icon Made without Hands; 28. Aug. 23: The Apodosis of the Dormition.[^27]

II. But among the lesser feasts there are also such feasts as are designated in the Horologion by three dots with a semicircle on the right side, that is, by the sign ⸫), and they have at Vespers at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) stichera on 6 without an entrance, at Matins there is no Polyeleos, the canon likewise is on 6, and the Small Doxology, that is, the hymn “Glory to God in the highest” (*Слава во вышнихъ Богу*) is only read without the “Trisagion.” These lesser feasts are called “feasts or Saints on six,” or “six-fold” [*shesterichni*].

Of these feasts there are more; among them the most notable are: 1. Sept. 5: The Holy Prophet Zachariah, father of St. John the Baptist; 2. Sept. 6: Commemoration of the Miracle Wrought at Colossae; 3. Sept. 9: Holy Joachim and Anna; 4. Sept. 24: The Holy Protomartyr Thecla. She was received into the faith of Christ by the Holy Apostle Paul; 5. Sept. 29: Our Venerable Father Cyriacus the Anchorite. Cyriacus was a Corinthian by birth, living in the time of Emperor Theodosius, and as a monk lived for 109 years; 6. Sept. 30: St. Gregory of Greater Armenia. By order of his kinsman Tiridates, King of Armenia, he was cast into captivity, where he remained for 15 years; 7. Oct. 2: The Holy Hieromartyr Cyprian. He suffered as bishop in the time of Emperor Decius in the year 247; 8. Oct. 3: The Holy Hieromartyr Dionysius the Areopagite. He received the Christian faith from the Holy Apostle Paul, and as bishop died a martyr's death in the year 98; 9. Oct. 7: The Holy Martyrs Sergius and Bacchus, who suffered under Emperor Maximian in the year 288; 10. Oct. 20: The Holy Great-Martyr Artemius, who suffered in the time of Emperor Julian the Apostate in the year 344; 11. Oct. 21: Our Venerable Father Hilarion († 325); 12. Nov. 1: St. Cosmas and St. Damian; 13. Nov. 6: Holy Father Paul, Confessor of Constantinople, who lived in the time of Constantius; 14. Nov. 17: St. Gregory, Bishop of Neo-Caesarea; 15. Nov. 28: St. Stephen the New; 16. Dec. 12: Our Venerable Father Spyridon the Wonderworker, Bishop of Trimythous; 17. Jan. 16: The Veneration of the Precious Chains of the Holy Apostle Peter; 18. Jan. 18: St. Athanasius; 19. Jan. 28: St. Ephrem; 20. Jan. 29: St. Ignatius the God-Bearer; 21. Jan. 30: St. Cyrus and John; 22. Feb. 17: The Holy Great-Martyr Theodore

=== LEAF p44 ===
[Book Page 20]

the Tyro; 23. April 1: Venerable Mary of Egypt; 24. May 7: Commemoration of the Sign of the Precious Cross which Appeared in the Heavens in Jerusalem; 25. May 24: Venerable Symeon; 26. June 8: The Holy Great-Martyr Theodore Stratelates, in memory of the translation of his relics from Heraclea to Euchaita; 27. June 9: St. Cyril, Archbishop of Alexandria; 28. June 28: The Translation of the Relics of the Holy Wonderworkers and Unmercenaries Cyrus and John; 29. July 1: The Holy Unmercenaries Cosmas and Damian; 30. July 8: The Holy Martyr Procopius; 31. July 24: St. Christina; 32. Aug. 2: The Translation of the Relics of St. Stephen the Archdeacon and Protomartyr.

Finally, the remaining Saints[^28] have no sign in the church books, and at Vespers at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) they have only 3 stichera, and at Matins a canon on 4 with the Small Doxology. The days of such Saints are not festal, and therefore they are also called “non-festal Saints.”

Note: The day of St. Stephen the Archdeacon on December 27, although concerning liturgical celebration it is without Great Vespers and without Great Matins, that is, it hath not even the Great Doxology, nevertheless concerning outward observance it is a decretal feast.

## § 13. Direction of Feasts Prescribed for the Eparchy of Mukachevo

His Excellency Julius Firczák, Bishop of Mukachevo, under number 4227:1897 given for the cathedral church and for his eparchy concerning feasts, hath prescribed the following Typikon:

I. Great and decretal feasts, namely: (α) Of the 1st division is Pascha, sorrowful and joyful, that is, Great Friday and the Bright Resurrection; (β) Of the 2nd division are these 12: Nativity of the Theotokos, Entry, Annunciation, Nativity of Christ, Meeting, Theophany [Baptism],

=== LEAF p45 ===
[Book Page 21]

Transfiguration, Entry into Jerusalem, Ascension, Descent of the Holy Spirit, Dormition of the Most Holy Virgin, and Exaltation of the Precious Cross; on all these we celebrate a Litiya; (γ) Of the 3rd division are: The Circumcision of the Lord, Protection of the Most Holy Virgin, Nativity and Beheading of the Head of John the Baptist, the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul, Holy Father Nicholas, and the feast of the church patron. On the Circumcision we celebrate a Litiya only if it falls on a Sunday. On the Protection of the Most Holy Virgin, by the decree of His Excellency our Bishop Julius, given under number 1557:1898, we therefore celebrate a Litiya because this day is the feast of the Patroness of our Eparchy of Mukachevo. On the Nativity and on the Beheading of the Honorable Head of St. John the Baptist it is proper to celebrate a Litiya, but we do not celebrate a Litiya, except where there is the patronal feast of a church. On the day of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul we always celebrate a Litiya, likewise also on the day of Holy Father Nicholas, for Holy Father Nicholas is the Patron of our Greek-Ruthenian Church; besides these, we celebrate a Litiya also on the patronal feast of a church.

II. Middle feasts are divided into decretal, and into choral, that is, polyeleos feasts:

(a) Middle and decretal feasts are: 1. Nov. 8: St. Michael the Archangel and the other Bodiless Powers; 2. Dec. 27: St. Stephen the Protomartyr and Archdeacon. On the Feast of St. Stephen the Protomartyr, by the decree of His Excellency our Bishop, given under number 1740:1898, at Vespers we make an entrance and read 3 paremias, at Matins we sing the Polyeleos, Exaltation [Velichaniye], Gospel, and Great Doxology, that is, we supplement his service as for a Solemn Saint from the General Menaion [Obshchina]; 3. Jan. 30: The Three Hierarchs; 4. April 23: St. George the Great-Martyr; 5. July 20: The Holy Prophet Elijah; 6. Aug. 7: St. Stephen, Equal-to-the-Apostles, King of Hungary, whose service, by the decree of His Excellency our Bishop Julius, given under number 3212:1897, we take with an entrance, Polyeleos, Gospel, and Great Doxology from the General Menaion to a single Apostle. It is our own proper feast.

(b) Polyeleos feasts are the following: 1. Jan. 11: St. Theodosius the Great; 2. Jan. 17: St. Anthony the Great; 3. Jan. 20: St. Euthymius the Great; 4. Jan. 25: St. Gregory the Theologian; 5. Jan. 27: The Translation of the Relics of St. John Chrysostom; 6. Feb. 24: The First and Second Finding of the Honorable Head of St. John the Baptist; 7. March 9: The 40 Martyrs; 8. April 25: St. Mark the Evangelist; 9. April 30: The Holy Apostle James, son of Zebedee; 10. May 8: St. John the Evangelist; 11. May 10: The Holy Apostle Simon; 12. May 21: St. Constantine and St.

=== LEAF p46 ===
[Book Page 22]

Helen; 13. May 25: The Third Finding of the Head of St. John the Baptist; 14. June 11: The Holy Apostles Bartholomew and Barnabas; 15. June 19: The Holy Apostle Jude; 16. The Apodosis of the Descent of the Holy Spirit on Saturday before the Sunday of All Saints; 23. Aug. 13: The Apodosis of the Transfiguration; 24. Note 1: The following days: 1. May 3: The Dormition of St. Theodosius of the Caves; 2. May 9: The Translation of the Relics of St. Nicholas; 3. July 10: St. Anthony of the Caves; 4. === LEAF p47 ===
[Book Page 23]

church only commemorative days without a Polyeleos and without the Great Doxology. (See Eparchial Ordinariate number 3212:1897.)

Note 2: On July 5: for St. Cyril and St. Methodius we take from the Common Service to Two and Many Hierarchs. (See *Molitvoslov*, Ungvár (Uzhhorod), 1895, p. 817.)

Note 3: On September 28: the day of St. Chariton, and on December 13: the day of the Holy Martyrs Eustratios, Auxentios, Eugenios, Mardarios, and Orestes, by the decree of His Excellency our Bishop Julius, given under number 4227:1897, are in our cathedral church and in our eparchy merely doxology feasts.

Note 4: The Galician-Greek Church hath also proper feasts (*festa propria*), and these are the following: 1. The Feast of the Most Holy Eucharist, which is celebrated on the Thursday after the Sunday of All Saints. It is a decretal feast; 2. The Feast of the Compassion of the Most Holy Theotokos, which is performed on the Friday of the second week after the Sunday of All Saints; 3. May 2: The Translation of the Relics of St. Boris and St. Gleb; 4. May 3: The Dormition of St. Theodosius of the Caves; 5. May 10: The Translation of the Relics of St. Nicholas; 6. July 10: St. Anthony of the Caves; 7. July 15: St. Vladimir; 8. July 24: St. Gleb and St. Boris; 9. Sept. 16: St. Josaphat; 10. Oct. 1: The Protection of the Most Holy Virgin (it is decretal; among the Romanians it is not a feast); 11. Oct. 23: St. Ignatius; 12. Oct. 28: St. Paraskeva the Martyr. (Cf. Nilles, *Calend. Utr. Eccl.*, Vol. I, p. 20.) — Of these feasts, among us the “Protection of the Most Holy Virgin” is a decretal feast. The remaining Saints are also inscribed in our *Molitvoslovy* (except St. Josaphat), and we celebrate their memory, but nevertheless their services in our cathedral church are not performed (except where they have the patronal feast of a church). The Feast of the Holy Eucharist and of the Compassion are not feasts among us.

## § 13. What Must Be Noted Concerning Sunday, Weekday, Fasting, Fast-Free, and Requiem Days?

Sunday is dedicated to the Resurrection of Christ; of all Sundays there are 52. They are reckoned either after the Resurrection, 1st, 2nd, etc., or after the Descent of the Holy Spirit, 1st, 2nd, etc., or 1st, 2nd, etc., of the Fast.

=== LEAF p48 ===
[Book Page 24]

Monday is dedicated to the Angels; Tuesday to St. John the Baptist; Wednesday and Friday to the Precious Cross; Thursday to the Apostles and St. Nicholas; Saturday to All Saints and the Departed.

On Wednesday and on Friday there is a fast; on Saturday and Sunday there is no fast even in the Great Fast itself, but only abstinence, with the exception of Great Saturday.[^29] For thus saith Apostolic Canon 66: *“Si quis clericorum deprehendatur die Dominico jejunans aut Sabbato, praeter unum solum (nempe Sabbatum Sanctum) deponatur, si vero laicus segregetur”* [“If any cleric be found fasting on the Lord’s day or on the Sabbath, except only one (namely Holy Saturday), let him be deposed, but if a layman, let him be segregated”].[^30] For Saturday is the day of creation, but Sunday is the day of redemption.

Fasts are: 1. Before the Nativity of Jesus Christ, from November 15 unto December 24 inclusive; 2. Before the Resurrection of Christ, from Cheesefare Sunday unto the Resurrection; 3. Of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul, from the Sunday of All Saints unto the feast of Peter and Paul; 4. Before the Dormition of the Most Holy Virgin Theotokos, from the 1st day of August unto the 14th; 5. On the day of the Exaltation of the Precious Cross; 6. On the day of the Beheading of the Honorable Head of St. John the Baptist.

Free or fast-free (*pospolyta*) days are: 1. The week after the Nativity of Christ unto Theophany; 2. The week of the Publican and the Pharisee; 3. Bright Week; 4. The week after the Descent of the Holy Spirit.[^31]

Requiem days: 1. The Saturday of Meatfare Sunday; 2. The Saturday before the Descent of the Holy Spirit (these days are *per excellentiam* All Souls’ Saturdays [*zadushni suboty*]); 3. The 2nd, 3rd, and 4th Saturdays of the Great Fast.

=== LEAF p49 ===
[Book Page 25]

# CHAPTER II

## § 14. On Church Books in General

Church books are either public or private, that is, intended either for public or for private use. — Public books are those which are approved for public use by the Church herself; private books are smaller books taken from one or more public books for the benefit of the clergy or the faithful, and they are approved only by one or another bishop.

It is not permitted to use unapproved books or books approved by schismatics in church; so commands Benedict XIV of blessed memory, who in his constitution *“Etsi pastoralis”* speaks thus: *“Quia in ecclesiasticis Graecorum libris nonnulli errores irrepsere, quibus primaevam Ecclesiae Graecae puritatem fraude potissimum ac dolo schismaticorum corruptam esse orthodoxi dolent, Ordinariorum locorum vigilantiam excitamus, ut nonnisi probatorum librorum usum admittant”* [“Because into the ecclesiastical books of the Greeks several errors have crept, whereby the orthodox grieve that the pristine purity of the Greek Church hath been corrupted chiefly by the fraud and deceit of schismatics, We arouse the vigilance of the local Ordinaries, that they admit the use of none but approved books”].[^32]

## § 15. Which of the Church Books Are Public?

1. The Horologion (in Greek: *Horologion* [Ὡроλόγιον], from the word: *hora* [ὥра] = *hora* [hour]), which is so called because the services contained therein had to be completed daily according to appointed hours (*per horas*), according to the words of the Psalmist: “Seven times a day have I praised Thee for the judgements of Thy righteousness” (Ps. 118:164 [LXX]). It contains in itself: (α) Morning prayers for lay people; (β) The service of the Midnight Office, daily, Saturday, and Sunday; (γ) The Order of Matins, both Sunday and daily; (δ) The Hours with the Typika [*Obidnytsia*]; (ε) The Order of Vespers with Litiya; (ϛ) The service of Compline, small and great, with the Paraklesis to the Most Holy Virgin; (η) Various services, both Sunday and for the departed, prayers before Communion and after Communion, besides this also prayers for those going to sleep;

=== LEAF p50 ===
[Book Page 26]

(ϑ) Sunday Troparia with Theotokia, Kontakia and Hypakoai, Prokeimena and Communion Hymns of the eight Sunday tones; likewise the daily Troparia with Kontakia and other chants of the entire week; (ι) Dismissal Theotokia in 8 tones and for all days of the week, sung after the Troparia of the Saints throughout the whole year at Vespers and at Matins at “The Lord is God” (*Бо́гъ Госпо́дь*) and again at the conclusion; (κ) The Menologion for the entire year according to movable[^33] and immovable feasts, in which the Troparion, Kontakion, Prokeimenon, and Communion Hymn of the feast or of the Saint, and at great feasts also the Antiphons with the Zadostoiniki [Hymns in place of *It is truly meet*], are found; (λ) Finally, there are the Indiction, Paschalion, and other rubrical directions.

2. The Psalter (in Greek: *Psalterion* [Ψαλτήριον], from the word: *psallein* [ψάλλειν] = *cantare comitante musica* [to sing to musical accompaniment]), which contains in itself: (α) The 150 Psalms of the Old Testament. These Psalms are divided into 20 kathismata, each kathisma containing 3 antiphons [staseis], and after the antiphons are Troparia and prayers; (β) The Nine Canticles of Moses; (γ) The Exaltations [Velichaniya] sung on Feasts of the Lord, Marian Feasts, and for Great Saints,[^34] to which are also added general refrains [*prypivy*] to various Saints, and then Bulgarian refrains prescribed for movable Feasts of the Lord, Marian Feasts, and immovable Great Saints; (δ) And the canon for the departure of the soul. Besides this there is also a direction how the Psalter is to be read throughout the entire year.

3. The Triodion (in Greek: *Triodion* [Τριῴδιον], from the words: *treis ode* [τρεῖς ᾠδή] = *tres odae*, *liber trium odarum* [three odes, book of three odes]). It is called “Triodion” because the canon of daily Matins in the Great Fast for the most part consists not of 9, but only of three odes. Besides this, on a certain day the canon hath only two odes, and such is called a “Diodion” [*Dvopesnets*], and yet on another four, and such is called a “Tetraodion” [*Chetyrepesnets*].[^35] The Triodion contains in itself services both Sunday and weekday, namely from the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee even unto Great Saturday; certain newer Ruthenian editions, however, have it only up to Lazarus Saturday. For Sunday services it hath: (α) Vespers stichera; (β) At Matins the stichera sung after Psalm 50 in place of “Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ,” namely at “Glory” (*Сла́ва*): “Open unto me the doors of repentance,” at “Now and ever”: “Guide me into the paths of salvation”; (γ) It hath one ca- 

=== LEAF p51 ===
[Book Page 27]

non.[^36] (δ) The exaposteilarion sung after the 9th ode. (ε) Two stichera at the Praises and at “Glory.” For weekday services it hath: (α) At Vespers three stichera and two readings, and stichera at the aposticha. (β) Small Matins with three sessional hymns and with three odes of the canon. (γ) The Triadicon exaposteilarion with “Glory,” which is read only after the 9th ode. (δ) At the end it hath a small Typikon. It must be noted that this book in the Greek-Ruthenian Church is also called the “Lenten Triodion.”

---


4. The Pentecostarion, that is, the book (in Greek: *Pentecostarion* [Πεντηκοστάριον], from the word: *pentekonta* [πεντήκοντα] = *quinquaginta* [fifty]). It contains in itself all services from the Sunday of Pascha (in Ruthenian editions from Lazarus Saturday) unto the Sunday of All Saints. And that: (α) Services of Passion Week; (β) Services of Bright Week; (γ) Services from Thomas Sunday [Antipascha] unto the Sunday of All Saints. For Sunday divine services: Vespers with Sunday stichera and with stichera at the aposticha; Matins with sessional hymns, the Sunday canon with an exaposteilarion, and stichera at the Praises. For daily divine services: regular Vespers on 3 stichera, Matins with two kathismata and sessional hymns, the Sunday canon, and stichera at the aposticha.

Note: It is necessary to note that because in Galicia the services of Passion Week have been taken from the Triodion and placed beforehand in the Pentecostarion, therefore both books are collectively called the “Triodion,” dividing them into the (α) Lenten and (β) Flowery Triodion.

5. The Octoechos (in Greek: *Octoechos* [Ὀκτώηχος], from the words: *octo* [ὀκτώ] and *echos* [ἦχος] = *octo* et *sonus* [eight and tone/sound]). It begins on the Sunday of All Saints in the evening and is used alone until the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee, and together with the Triodion until Lazarus Saturday. It contains in itself: Sunday and weekday services according to the 8 tones, and that in each tone: (α) The Sunday service, namely: stichera of Small and Great Vespers, both at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) and at the aposticha; furthermore, the Theotokion canon of Compline, the Trinity canon of the Midnight Office, sessional hymns, the hypakoe, the morning graduals [stepenny], a threefold Matins canon: of the Resurrection, Cross-Resurrection, and Theotokion, Sunday stichera at the Praises, and the Beatitudes at the Liturgy; (β) The Monday service: Vespers with stichera, the Theotokion canon of Compline, Matins with 3 sessional hymns and with

=== LEAF p52 ===
[Book Page 28]

two canons, namely: of Repentance and to the Angels, stichera at the aposticha, and the Beatitudes; (γ) The Tuesday service: It contains in itself the stichera of Vespers, the Theotokion canon of Compline, and Matins with sessional hymns and with two canons, namely: of Repentance and to the Forerunner; (δ) The Wednesday service: It contains in itself: evening stichera, the Theotokion canon of Compline, and Matins with sessional hymns and with two canons, namely: of the Theotokos and of the Cross; (ε) The Thursday service: It contains in itself: evening stichera, the Theotokion canon of Compline, and Matins with sessional hymns and with two canons: to the Holy Apostles and to St. Nicholas; (ϛ) The Friday service, that is, stichera of the Cross and Stavrotheotokia at Vespers, the canon of the Theotokos at Compline, at Matins after the sessional hymns the canon: of the Cross and of the Theotokos; (η) The Saturday service: It contains in itself: evening stichera, the Theotokion canon of Compline, and Matins with sessional hymns and with two canons: to All Saints and for the Departed. At the Praises (which the other days do not have) martyr stichera, and at the aposticha stichera for the departed. Besides these, there are found here: (ϑ) The Triadica of Gregory of Sinai used on Sunday at the Midnight Office after the Theotokion canon; (ι) Troparia after the Evlogitaria [Amomos / Psalm 118]: “The angelic council,” etc.; (κ) The 11 Matins Gospels with 11 Sunday exaposteilaria and with 11 Gospel stichera; (λ) Daily exaposteilaria sung throughout the entire week, and daily Triadica for the Holy Forty Days; (μ) A small Typikon on how the Octoechos must be used with the Menaion.

6. The Heirmologion [Chant-Book] (in Greek: *Heirmologion* [Ἑιρμολόγιον], from the word: *heirmon* [εἱρμὸν] = *melodia vel series* [melody or chain/series]). It is so called because it contains in musical notes various church chants, especially from the Octoechos, but besides this also taken from the Menaion, from the Triodion, and from other books, which in church must be sung and not read, and according to which many other hymns are also sung. It is likewise for the most part an abridged Octoechos, but since it also hath hymns taken from other books which are set here to musical notes, therefore in this sense it is an excerpt from all the principal books. Most particularly it contains in itself: The Heirmologisation of the Octoechos, which is divided according to the 8 tones, that is, melodies. In each tone there is set in notes: (α) How the stichera at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) are to be sung with the dogmatica and with the doxastica [slavy]; (β) Sunday Troparia with sessional hymns and graduals [stepenny]; (γ) Heirmoi of the canon with exaposteilaria; (δ) Then model hymns [prosomoia] and Bulgarian chants, etc. — With each tone there are also stichera of the great feasts

=== LEAF p53 ===
[Book Page 29]

which at Great Vespers are sung in place of Sunday dogmatica. After the eighth tone there are various hymns and chants taken from other books, such as: the Polyeleos [*Mnohomylostyven*], Exaltations [Velichaniya], the hymn “We who the Cherubim” [Cherubic Hymn], “All of creation rejoices in thee,” chants of the Liturgy of the Presanctified, and the thanksgiving hymn of St. Ambrose: “We praise Thee, O God” [*Te Deum*].

7. The Monthly Books [Menaia] (in Greek: *Menaia biblia* [Μηναῖα βιβλία] = *libri mensuales*, from the word: *men* [μὴν] = month). They consist of 12 books for the 12 months, in which are arranged the services for immovable feasts. Every Saint concerning whose life church history knows anything, and all church events whose memory holy Church celebrates, have their proper services and their morning canons, so that their number reaches up to 800.[^37] Each book contains in itself: (α) If there be a lesser or non-festal Saint: evening stichera, further the morning canon, and Troparia of lesser Saints; (β) If there be a middle Saint, then at Vespers it hath stichera on 6, the rest as for lesser Saints; (γ) If there be a great Saint, then it contains Great Vespers with stichera and with readings, sometimes also a Litiya — and the entire divine service is taken from the Menaion; (δ) It contains in itself the divine service of Marian Feasts; (ε) Of Feasts of the Lord, only the divine service of immovable feasts is found here. At the end of each single book there are: (α) Sunday Theotokia of the Resurrection in 8 tones sung at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) and at the aposticha; (β) Theotokia of the 8 tones which on weekdays, when there is a “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) to the Saint in the Menaion, are taken at “Now and ever”; (γ) Sunday dismissal Theotokia in 8 tones sung after the Sunday Troparia at “Now and ever”; (δ) Dismissal Theotokia for weekdays in 8 tones sung after the Troparia at Vespers, and at Matins at “The Lord is God” (*Бо́гъ Госпо́дь*) and at the conclusion.

8. The Anthologion (in Greek: *Anthologion* [᾿Ανθολόγιον], from the word: *anthos* [ἄνθος] = *flos*, flower; or: *Tryphologion* [Τρυφολόγιον], from the word: *tryphe* [τρύφη] = *deliciae* [delights]). This book is nothing other than an abridged Menaion, which is also called the “Festal Menaion,” and that because it contains in itself all those services of immovable

=== LEAF p54 ===
[Book Page 30]

feasts of the Lord, Marian feasts, and great Saints which are found also in the Menaion. Besides this, the Anthologion contains in itself at the end of the second volume also the common services for middle and lesser Saints according to various ranks [choirs], and therefore this part is called: The General Menaion, that is, the *Obshchina*. This book contains in itself: (a) If there be a Feast of the Lord: (α) Stichera of Small Vespers; (β) Stichera of Great Vespers with readings; (γ) Stichera at the Litiya with stichera at the aposticha; (δ) Three morning sessional hymns; (ε) The sticheron sung after Psalm 50; (ϑ) The Magnification refrain in place of “My soul doth magnify the Lord” (*Вели́читъ дꙋша̀ моя̀ Го́спода...*); (η) An exaposteilarion; (ι) Stichera at the Praises; (κ) Antiphons at the Liturgy; (λ) Stichera of the Afterfeast sung at Vespers; (b) If there be a Marian Feast, then everything is found just as at Feasts of the Lord, except at Matins, for here after the 8th ode there is no magnification refrain; if there be a refrain, it is found in the Psalter; (c) If there be a great Saint, then it is one case if there be a Litiya, and another if there be no Litiya. If there be a Litiya, then it contains in itself: (α) Small Vespers with 4 stichera at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) and with stichera at the aposticha; (β) Great Vespers with stichera at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) and readings, and with stichera at the Litiya and with stichera at the aposticha; Matins with canons, which concludes in regular order. If there be no Litiya, then it contains in itself: (α) Stichera of Great Vespers with readings, and with stichera at the aposticha; (β) Three morning sessional hymns; (γ) The sticheron sung after Psalm 50; (δ) Three canons: the first to the Theotokos, and the second and third to the Saint; (ε) Stichera at the Praises; (d) Finally, if there be a middle or lesser Saint, the service is taken from the General Menaion [*Obshchina*]. In the General Menaion the Saint hath: (α) Three stichera at Vespers; “Glory and now”: a Theotokion or Stavrotheotokion; (β) At Matins he hath a canon, sometimes also an exaposteilarion.

9. The Synaxarion (in Greek: *Synaxaria* [Συναξάρια], that is: *biblia*, from the word: *synago* [συνάγω] = *colligere* [to gather together]). It contains in itself the description [lives] of the Saints. For every day holy Mother Church celebrates the memory of some Saint whose life ought to be read at Matins. The Synaxarion, like the Menaion, hath 12 volumes. Among us there is no Synaxarion, but in place of this book parts of the Synaxarion are inserted in other books, most particularly in the Flower and Lenten Triodion.

10. The Apostol (in Greek: *Apostolos* [᾿Απόστολος] or *Apostolikon biblion* [᾿Αпоστολικὸν βιβλίον]). It contains in itself: (α) The Acts of the Apostles, writ-

=== LEAF p55 ===
[Book Page 31]

ten by the Holy Evangelist Luke; (β) The General [Catholic] Epistles of the Holy Apostles: James, Peter, John, and Jude; (γ) The Epistles of the Holy Apostle Paul to various churches or persons, thus: to the Romans, 2 to the Corinthians, to the Galatians, to the Ephesians, to the Philippians, to the Colossians, 2 to the Thessalonians, 2 to Timothy, to Titus, to Philemon, and to the Hebrews. — The Apostol with respect to ecclesiastical use is divided into pericopes [*pericopae* / *zachala*], of which there are 339. The numbering by pericopes begins from the first chapter of the Acts of the Apostles and proceeds through all the apostolic epistles in the cited order, even unto the end of the Epistle to the Hebrews. In the margin it is indicated when at the beginning of the reading one must begin: “In those days,” when: “Brethren,” when: “Child Titus,” or: “Child Timothy!” — At the end of the book there is found a direction when and how the appointed portions from the epistles are to be read.[^38]

11. The Gospel (in Greek: *Evangelion* [Εὐαγγέλιον], from the words: *eu* and *angelia* [εὖ et ἀγγελία] = *laetus nuntius* [glad tidings]). It contains in itself the life and teaching of Jesus Christ set forth by the four Evangelists. The Gospels are divided into chapters and verses, but this division is scholastic; for ecclesiastical use there is the division into pericopes [*zachala*]; — and thus the Gospel of St. Matthew is divided into 115, of St. Mark into 71, of St. Luke into 114, and of St. John into 67 pericopes. At the end the Gospels have an index [rubric] showing what Gospel ought to be read and when, and that firstly according to movable feasts, beginning from the Sunday of Pascha, then according to immovable feasts, beginning from September 1, and lastly according to various occasions and needs.
