In order that this pericope might be more easily found, there is indicated in the text at the bottom, usually in red letters, that same designation, and therewith are added also the words with which the respective pericope is to begin, which are: “The Lord said,” “The Lord said to His disciples,” “The Lord said to the Jews that came unto Him,” or “The Lord spoke this parable,” or “At that time.” — After these words a red star is printed, directing to the first word of the pericope which must be read, and beside that word there is likewise a star.

12. The Sluzhebnik (in Greek: *Leitourgikon* [Λειτουργικόν], from the word: *leitourgein* [λειτουργεῖν] = *munus publicum obire* [to perform a public duty/office], which word is composed

=== LEAF p56 ===
[Book Page 32]

of *leiton* and *ergon* [λεῖτον et ἔργον] = *publicum* et *munus* [public and duty]). It contains in itself: (α) All things necessary for the Holy Liturgy, namely: Antiphons, Troparia, Prokeimena, Epistles, and Gospels, Exaltations [Velichaniya],[^39] Communion Hymns, and dismissals of Sundays, feasts, and plain movable days, and that: beginning from the Sunday of Pascha unto the Sunday of All Saints, furthermore from the Sunday of All Saints unto the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee, and from thence unto Great Saturday; (β) The Order of the Divine Liturgies: of St. John Chrysostom, of St. Basil the Great, and of St. Gregory the Dialogist, that is, of the Presanctified; (γ) Services of the entire year for immovable feasts, and that: Feasts of the Lord, Marian Feasts, and proper to Saints; (δ) General services for other Saints having no proper services in the Menologion; (ε) Services of various intentions; (ϛ) The Order of Vespers with Litiya, the Order of the Midnight Office, both Sunday and daily, and the Order of Matins; (η) Sunday Troparia, Kontakia, and Theotokia of the eight tones; (ϑ) Daily and general Troparia to the Saints; (ι) The prayer over kolyvo (offering), and “We praise Thee, O God.”

13. The Trebnik [Euchologion] (in Greek: *Euchologion* [Εὐχολόγιον], from the word: *euche* [εὐχὴ] = *praecatio* [prayer]). It is called in the Old Slavonic language “Trebnik” from its principal content, that is, from the Holy Mysteries [Sacraments], which are indispensably necessary [*potrebny*] to men, or also because daily necessities can be satisfied through these services. It contains in itself: (α) The rites of the seven Holy Mysteries. It must be noted that the priest will find also in the Trebnik those rules which he must observe during the administration of the Holy Mysteries; (β) Services of every kind of ecclesiastical Consecrations and Blessings, such as: the Order of Burials, the Order of Parastas, the Order of the Consecration and Blessing of water, church, cemetery, bells, vestments, vessels, objects, fruits, herbs, etc.; (γ) Various prayers, such as: that concluded at the beginning of the Holy Forty Days, for the blessing of palms, of meats, over the sick, in time of fair weather and drought, etc.; (δ) The Order of the installation of a newly appointed pastor, further the rubric on the Divine Liturgy celebrated by a priest for the first time, and then a second time after 50 years; (ε) At the end there is also the profession of faith which a priest, pastor, professor of theology, canon, and bishop must profess.

14. The Pontifical Sluzhebnik [Archieratikon], that is, *Archieratikon* [᾿Αρχιερατικόν], which contains in itself the services celebrated by a Bishop.

=== LEAF p57 ===
[Book Page 33]

15. The Typikon (in Greek: *Typikon* [Τυπικὸν], from the word: *typos* [τύπος] = *forma*, *regula* [form, rule]) is the rubrical order, or guide, for how the divine services of the entire year are to be celebrated from all the above-mentioned books. The Typikon which is used among us[^40] was published for the Eparchy of Przemyśl; it is divided into the following parts: (α) On the ecclesiastical rule in general; (β) On the order of sacred ministrations, that is, how Small and Great Vespers, Compline, the Midnight Office, Matins, the Hours, the Typika [*Obidnytsia*], and the Liturgy are celebrated in general; (γ) Special directions for the most notable immovable feasts in the order of the Menologion, beginning from September 1; (δ) Special directions for movable feasts, Sundays, and weekdays from the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee even unto the Sunday of All Saints; (ε) Directions on the Polyeleos, the ordinary Katavasia, the hymn “My soul doth magnify the Lord,” on the Typika [*Izobrazitelni*] and Antiphons, and on dismissals with supplementary notes; (ϛ) Finally, it contains six pillars [stólpy] with the incipits of the Gospel pillars taken from the Indiction, and a brief Paschalion direction. The Typikon was compiled on the foundation of the church books, to which it frequently refers, and in which explanation must be sought if anything in the Typikon should be stated incompletely or not entirely clearly.

## § 17. Which of the Church Books Are Private?

(α) The Molitvoslov, or better said, the “Priestly Horologion,” for it contains in itself everything that the Horologion contains, but besides this priestly needs are also satisfied here; (β) The Izbornik of needful ecclesiastical rites and services taken from the Horologion, Leitourgikon, Octoechos, Menaion, and Triodion; (γ) The “Sunday Octoechos,” which contains in itself only the Sunday service; (δ) The “Strastnik” [Passion Book], that is, the book containing the Gospels that are read in Passion Week; (ε) The “Hlasopesnets,” containing in itself the most frequently used chants of the Heirmologion and other church hymns; (ϛ) The “Bohohlasnyk” (in Greek: *hymnologion* [ὑμνολόγιον]), containing in itself church hymns in musical notes;[^41] (η) The “Akathistnik,” which contains in itself 20 Akathists.

=== LEAF p58 ===
[Book Page 34]

## § 18. How Are Public Books Divided?

Public books are divided in accordance with church rites into 3 parts, and that into: (α) Choral, (β) Liturgical, and (γ) Euchological books (*libri chorales, liturgici et euchologici*).[^42] Choral books contain in themselves pre-liturgical services, and these are: the Horologion, Psalter, Triodion, Pentecostarion, Octoechos, Menaia (or the Anthologion with the General Menaion), and the Synaxarion. Liturgical books contain the services of the Holy Liturgies, and these are: the Leitourgikon, Apostol, and Gospel. Lastly, euchological books contain in themselves the rites of the Holy Mysteries and other ecclesiastical Consecrations and Blessings; to these belongs: the Trebnik.

## § 19. What Must Be Noted Concerning Church Books?

1. The Psalter is so divided in the services that in each week the entire Psalter is to be read through once, and in the Great Fast in each week (with the exception of Passion Week) twice; — in Passion Week, however, when it is prescribed to read through all 4 Gospels, the entire Psalter is read in the first three days only once, namely all kathismata with the exception of the first three and the 17th, while the 18th kathisma at that time is read three times, namely each day at Vespers. On other days, just as also in Bright Week, the Psalter is omitted.

2. Of the choral books, some are used only for movable feasts or days; such are: the Triodion, Pentecostarion, and Octoechos; others for immovable feasts or days; these are: the Menaion, or Anthologion with the General Menaion; and lastly, certain books both for movable feasts or days and for immovable feasts or days; such are the Horologion and Psalter.

3. Because each day we celebrate together a twofold feast or day, that is, movable and immovable, therefore in a single service those two feasts or days are combined, and in accordance with this, in a single Horologion there are combined from the Sunday of All

=== LEAF p59 ===
[Book Page 35]

Saints unto the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee the Octoechos with the Menaion; in the fast, the Triodion with the Octoechos and with the Menaion; and during the time of the Pentecostarion, the Flower Triodion with the Menaion.

4. The Lenten Triodion is joined with the Octoechos in such wise that: (α) On the days of Cheesefare Week there are taken from the Octoechos at Vespers the stichera at “Lord, I have cried,” and at Matins the sessional hymns after the first stichology, exaposteilaria, and those odes of the canon which are not in the Triodion; (β) On the Sundays of the Great Fast, besides the Triodion, the Octoechos is also taken, with the exception of Palm Sunday; (γ) On the weekdays of the Forty Days there are taken from the Octoechos at Matins the sessional hymns after the 1st stichology, but the canon of the Octoechos is superseded by the Triodion [three odes] and this canon on weekdays is entirely omitted. In a word, concerning the Octoechos we can say this: that the chanting of the Octoechos, regarding weekdays, begins on Monday after the Sunday of All Saints and in regular order concludes on Friday before Meatfare Saturday, and exceptionally ends on Friday before Lazarus Saturday; regarding Sundays, however, it begins on the second Sunday after the Descent of the Holy Spirit and continues unto the 5th Sunday of the Great Fast inclusively. Because parts of the Octoechos are printed at the end of the Menaion, or in the Menaion itself, we can say that in the Great Fast the Octoechos is taken only on Sundays, and on weekdays is omitted. But besides this, the Lenten Triodion is combined also with the Menaion, namely: on all weekdays of the Forty Days there are taken, besides the Triodion, at Vespers from the Menaion 3 or 4 stichera at “Lord, I have cried,” and at Matins the canon of the occurring Saint, which, with slight exceptions indicated in the Triodion, is sung in its entirety.

5. The Octoechos is omitted: (α) From Lazarus Saturday even unto the Sunday of All Saints inclusively, with the exception of certain parts which are taken from the Octoechos and transferred to the Flower Triodion and printed here; (β) From December 20 unto January 14, with the exception of a single Sunday; (γ) On great Feasts of the Lord, on whatever day they fall, even on a Sunday; (δ) On great Marian Feasts, if they fall on a weekday; (if a Marian Feast falls on a Sunday, the Sunday service is taken from the Octoechos, and the feast from the Menaion); (ε) Likewise also during Forefeasts and at the Apodoses (excepting Sundays) of great feasts; (ϛ) And on the feasts of Great Saints (excepting Sundays) the Octoechos is omitted.

6. The Menaion is omitted: (α) On all Sundays of the Lenten Triodion, beginning from the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee unto Palm Sunday

=== LEAF p60 ===
[Book Page 36]

inclusively; (β) On Wednesday and Friday of the 3rd week and on Thursday of the 5th week of the Great Fast; (γ) On Saturdays, namely Meatfare, Cheesefare, of the 1st and 5th weeks, and on Lazarus Saturday; (δ) On all days of Passion Week.

Note: But if on those days there should occur a Great Saint, or the Patron Saint of the church, or the Feast of the Meeting and the Annunciation, their service is taken even on the cited days. Nevertheless, here too there are limitations, thus: the service of the “40 Martyrs” is not taken if those Saints occur on Thursday or Saturday of the fifth week; the service of St. John the Forerunner is not taken on Meatfare Saturday if it falls on February 24; only the service of the Meeting is always taken on its proper day, and the service of the Annunciation is celebrated even in Passion Week; the service of a Great or Patron Saint in Passion Week is taken in the first days, but in the latter days is transferred to Bright Week on Monday.

7. The Flower Triodion is taken together with the Menaion from Thomas Sunday even unto the Sunday of All Saints, and that usually so that at Vespers there are taken at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) 3 stichera from the Menaion, and the canon on 4. Nevertheless, during this time the Menaion is omitted: (α) On all days of Bright Week; (β) On all Sundays of Pentecost; (γ) On the day of Mid-Pentecost and its Apodosis; (δ) On the day of the Apodosis of Pascha; (ε) On the day of Ascension and its Apodosis; (ϛ) On the Saturday before Pentecost; (η) On the feast of the Descent of the Holy Spirit on Sunday and on Monday; (ϑ) On the Sunday of All Saints. Nevertheless, although the services of middle Saints are transferred on these days, those of Great Saints, and especially of Patron Saints of the church, are celebrated on their proper days, even though they should occur on the cited days.

Note: The services of Lesser and Middle Saints which are omitted from Lazarus Saturday unto Thomas Sunday are taken anticipatively [*anticipative*] from Monday of the 2nd week of the Great Fast at Compline.

8. The singing of the eight tones of the Octoechos in the course of 8 weeks is called a “pillar” [*stolp*]. Of pillars throughout one year there are 6; they begin at an appointed time: the 1st pillar begins on the 1st Sunday of the Fast of the Apostles [Peter and Paul]; the 2nd on the days after the Prophet Elijah; the 3rd after the Exaltation of the Precious Cross; the 4th during the Fast of the Nativity of the Lord; the 5th after Theophany; and the 6th during the Holy Great Fast. — If anyone wishes to know on which day of each year each single pillar falls: let him first look up from 

=== LEAF p61 ===
[Book Page 37]

the Indiction the Key-letter [*klyuch hranychnyi*], that is, the boundary letter, and thereafter let him look under this letter in the Paschalion, and there it is accurately stated on what day each pillar begins.

---


## § 20. From Whom Do the Church Books Originate?

In the 1st, 2nd, and 3rd centuries Christians probably had of church books only the Holy Scripture of the Old and New Testaments and the Psalter.

But in the 4th century, when the Christian faith was not only free, but also dominant: in the Church of Christ there probably was already the Horologion [Book of the Hours], the Leitourgikon [Sluzhebnik], and the Euchologion [Trebnik]. Those books were probably compiled by the great Hierarchs of the 4th century, especially by St. Basil the Great and St. John Chrysostom.

In the 5th century lived St. Anatolius († 458), Patriarch of Constantinople, who composed a multitude of stichera,[^43] and St. Romanus the Deacon († 500), the Melodist, who composed a multitude of kontakia, that is, short poetic hymns.

In the 6th century lived St. Sabbas († 532), Archimandrite of the “Great Lavra” on the torrent Cedron near Jerusalem, who composed a multitude of chants and canticles and one church order, which is now known under the name of the “Typikon of St. Sabbas.” — Besides this, he is also held to be the first compiler of the Menaion. He systematically compiled the Menaion according to oral tradition, which he received from his teachers St. Chariton (who lived in the time of Constantine), St. Euthymius († 473), and St. Theoctistus († 467),[^44] and prescribed it with obligation for his monks.

In the 7th century lived St. Sophronius († 638), Patriarch of Jerusalem, to whom they attribute the compilation of the Triodion and the enlargement of the Menaion; furthermore St. Andrew, Archbishop of Crete († 712), compiler especially of the Great Canon of Repentance and the canon for the Wednesday of Mid-Pentecost.

=== LEAF p62 ===
[Book Page 38]

In the 8th century lived St. John Damascene († 760), compiler of the Octoechos and enlarger of the Menaion; and St. Cosmas, at first a monk in the “Great Lavra” of Jerusalem, but later Bishop of Maiuma († 781). He composed, among others, the canon for the Descent of the Holy Spirit.

In the 9th century lived St. Theodore the Studite († 826), Archimandrite of the Monastery of Studium at Constantinople, and his brother Joseph († 845), Bishop of Thessalonica, who composed many canons and canticles. Some also attribute to them the compilation or enlargement of the Triodion.

Finally, in this same 9th century St. Theophanes, Archbishop of Nicaea († 858), and Joseph the Hymnographer, a monk in Constantinople († 883), compiled the services of Selected Saints, that is, they enlarged the Menaion.[^45]

# CHAPTER III.
## On Liturgical Expressions and Terminology in the Church Books
## § 21. On Liturgical Technical Terms in General

By technical terms we call those names by which we designate the psalms and poems, readings, liturgical actions and melodies, hymns, chants and canticles, parts and entire services used in our rite. — Those designations were received by the respective parts of our rite either according to text, or melody, or composition, or usage, or purpose, or according to their meaning. Thus the kontakion received this name from the text, for it is a short poem (*kontakion* [κοντάκιον] == *breviculum*); psalms are called antiphons from their melody; the polyeleos received this name from its composition, for therein the word *eleos* [ἔлеος] == *mercy* is found many times; the refrain [*pripiv*] is so called from its usage, for it is chanted before a troparion; the prokimenon is so named from its purpose, for by it the faithful are reminded of the reading of Holy Scripture; while the parastas is so called according to its meaning (*parastasis* [παράστασις] == *intercessio*), for by it we intercede for the departed.

=== LEAF p63 ===
[Book Page 39]

## § 22. On Liturgical Technical Terms in Particular

1. **Order** [*Chyn*] (in Greek: *diataxis* [διάταξις]) is used as the order or disposition of some divine action (*ordo seu dispositio officii divini*).

2. **Service** [*Sluzhba*] (in Greek: *Akolouthia* [᾿Ακολουθία] == *ipsum officium*) is nothing other than the action itself arranged in an ordered sequence (*ipsum officium ordinatum denotat*).

3. **Psalms** are hymns taken from the Old Testament and are called psalms (in Greek: *psalmos* [ψάλμος]) from the word *psallein* [ψάλλειν], for the Hebrews, when singing them, accompanied them with music. Of the psalms the most renowned are: (α) Psalm 103 (“Bless the Lord, O my soul” (*Благослови душе моя Господа*)), which is called the introductory psalm (in Greek: *prooimiakos* [προοιμιακὸς] == *prooimialis*), for with it the church rule begins; (β) Psalm 140 (“Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*)), called the lucernarium psalm (in Greek: *ho epilychnios* [ὁ ἐπιλύχνιος] == *lucernarius*), and that because at the evening sacrifice of old that psalm was stichologized by the light of that candle which the deacon brought out and placed before the royal doors, or because at Vespers it is stichologized before the lighting of the evening sacrifice; (γ) Psalms 3, 37, 62, 87, 102, and 142 are called the “Six Psalms” [Hexapsalmos] (in Greek: *hexapsalmos* [ἑξάψαλμος], from the words *hex* [ἕξ] and *psalmos* [ψάλμος]); (δ) Psalm 118 (“Blessed are the undefiled”), called the blameless psalm (in Greek: *ho amomos* [ὁ ἄμωμος]), and that because therein is described the blessedness of the blameless man; (ε) Psalm 135 (“O give thanks unto the Lord”), called the polyeleos psalm (in Greek: *ho polyeleos* [ὁ πολυέλεος]), and this name derives from the fact that in those verses of the psalm the word “mercy” is frequently repeated (in Greek: *eleos* [ἔλεος]); (ϛ) Psalms 119–133 are called gradual psalms (in Greek: *anabathmoi* [ἀναβαθμοί] == *psalmi graduales*), and that because in the Psalter before Psalm 119 we find the inscription: “A Song of Ascents.” The Holy Fathers say that the Hebrews sang those psalms when they returned from the Babylonian captivity; others say that they sang them during prayer pilgrimages to Jerusalem at the 3 great feasts. Besides this, some also say that those psalms were sung by the Levites at the Feast of Tabernacles while standing upon the 15 steps which were placed in the temple between the court of the men and the court of the women. Some also

=== LEAF p64 ===
[Book Page 40]

call those psalms also the psalms “unto the Lord” (*ta pros Kyrion* [τὰ πρὸς Κύριον]), and that because the first of them begins with this phrase: “unto the Lord”; (η) Psalms 148, 149, and 150 are called the laudatory psalms, or the psalms at the Praises [Lauds] (*hoi ainoi* [οἱ αἶνοι]), from the word *aineite* [αἰνεῖτε] == “praise ye,” which is repeated in the verses of these psalms; (ϑ) Psalm 102 (“Bless the Lord, O my soul” (*Благослови душе моя Господа*)) and Psalm 145 (“Praise the Lord, O my soul” (*Хвалѝ, дꙋшѐ моя̀, Го́спода...*)) are called the Typika psalms (*hoi ton typikon psalmoi* [οἱ τῶν τυπικῶν ψαλμοί]), for they proclaim the prefiguration (*typon* [τύπον] == *figuram*) of the Messiah coming into the world. They are stichologized at the Typika [Obyednytsia].

4. **Kathisma** (from the Greek word *kathizo* [κατίζω] == to sit) signifies “a sitting.” The Psalter, as was said, contains 20 kathismata. A kathisma was used in church either by reading or by chanting (*recitando vel cantando*). The reading of the Psalter was called “stichology” (*stichologia* [στηχολογία]), while the chanting was called “psalmody.” — When they only read (*aneu melous* [ἄνευ μέλους]) the Psalter, they divided the kathisma into 3 staseis (*staseis* [στασεῖς] == *stationes*), that is, at three places during the reading they rested. But when they sang (*meta melous* [μετὰ μέλους]), they divided the kathisma into 3 melodies. They read in such a manner that either one read with a single voice, or responsively [in two choirs]. But they chanted the kathisma in such a manner that: (α) either all sang together (*symphonia* [συμφωνία]), or (β) two choirs sang antiphonally (*antiphonia* [ἀνтиφωνία]), or (γ) two sang antiphonally and someone besides this was the leader (*hypophonia* [ὑποφωνία]),[^46] that is, the precentor (*hypoboleus* [ὑποβολεύς]) sang alone, and the others responded to him, either by repeating the final words of the psalm, or with another verse.

5. **Antiphon** (in Greek: *antiphonon* [ἀντιφωνῶν]) signifies nothing other than “responsive word” (*vox opposita, seu ejusdem vocis oppositio vel rectius ejusdem sententiae ad singulos versus psalmi repetitio*), that is: a corresponding and counter melody, or the repetition of a single divine thought throughout the verses of a psalm. Of the psalms, those which were chanted with a corresponding and antiphonal melody are called “antiphonal psalms,” or simply “Antiphons.” An antiphon in a nar-

=== LEAF p65 ===
[Book Page 41]

rower sense consists of 2 or 3 verses of a Psalm with “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) and “Now,” and of one refrain. Antiphonal psalms are the following: (α) Gradual psalms; (β) “Typika psalms” sung at the Typika [Obyednytsia]; (γ) “Liturgical psalms,” and those antiphons are either daily or festal. Daily antiphons are: Psalm 91 (“It is good to give praise unto the Lord”), thereafter Psalm 92 (“The Lord reigns” (*Госпо́дь воцари́ся*)), and Psalm 94 (“Come, let us rejoice in the Lord”). Of feasts, only the Feasts of Pascha, Ascension, Descent of the Holy Spirit, Transfiguration, Exaltation, Nativity of Jesus Christ, Theophany, and Meeting of our Lord in Jerusalem have proper antiphons. On Sunday and on other feasts it would be proper to sing the antiphonal psalms of the Typika, that is, Psalm 102 (“Bless the Lord, O my soul” (*Благослови душе моя Господа*)), Psalm 145 (“Praise the Lord, O my soul” (*Хвалѝ, дꙋшѐ моя̀, Го́спода...*)), and instead of the 3rd “antiphonal psalm” it would be proper to sing stichera composed on the individual verses of the evangelical Beatitudes spoken by Jesus Christ upon the mount in Matthew chapter 5, and therefore those are named “stichera on the Beatitudes.” But among us instead of those, Sunday antiphons are sung on Sundays.

6. **Paroemia** (in Greek: *paroimia* [παροιμία] == *parabola, proverbium*). Paroemias are called readings from the Holy Scripture taken for the most part from the Old Testament and containing within themselves either prophecies concerning the event commemorated on that day, or explaining the event of the occurring feast, or containing praise thereof. But readings taken from the New Testament are called “Epistles” [*Apostles*] or “Gospels.” Paroemias are read: (α) At Great Vespers on Feasts of the Lord, of the Mother of God, and of Great Saints, usually 3; (β) In the Great Fast at Vespers, especially those combined with the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts,[^47] only 2; (γ) At Vespers combined with the Liturgy of St. Basil the Great, for example, on the Eves of the Nativity, of Theophany, and on Great Saturday, more; (δ) Besides this, they are also read at the Royal Hours, that is, on the Eve of the Nativity, of Theophany, and on Great Friday, but there at a single hour only one paroemia is taken.

7. **The Canticles of Moses**. Concerning these canticles it is necessary to note that they are taken from various books of the Holy Scripture of the Old and New Testaments,

=== LEAF p66 ===
[Book Page 42]

namely: (α) Ode 1 is taken from Exodus 15:1–19; this canticle is that which, after passing through the Red Sea, Moses and the children of Israel sang thus: “Let us sing unto the Lord, for He hath been gloriously glorified”; (β) Ode 2 is taken from the Book of Deuteronomy, chapter 32, which Moses cried aloud at the end of his life, when he had led the people through the wilderness, tactics: “Give heed, O heaven, and I will speak”; (γ) Ode 3 is taken from 1 Kings [1 Samuel], chapter 2, and is the canticle of Hannah, mother of the Prophet Samuel, which she thus proclaimed: “My heart is established in the Lord”; (δ) Ode 4 is taken from the prophecy of Habakkuk, chapter 3, who having seen God coming forth from the shaded mountain, cried aloud thus: “Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid”; (ε) Ode 5 is taken from the book of the Prophet Isaiah, chapter 26, who having foretold the birth of Emmanuel from the Virgin, cried aloud thus: “Early in the morning my spirit seeks Thee, O God”; (ϛ) Ode 6 is taken from the prophecy of Jonah, chapter 2:1–7, which he cried aloud when after his three-day stay in the belly of the whale he reached dry land, thus: “I cried in mine affliction unto the Lord God”; (η) Ode 7 is taken from the Book of Daniel, chapter 3, which the Three Youths cried aloud in the Babylonian furnace, saying thus: “Blessed art Thou, O Lord God of our fathers”; (ϑ) Ode 8 is likewise taken thence and contains the thanksgiving canticle of those same youths delivered from the fiery furnace, which they cried aloud thus: “Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord; sing and exalt Him above all forever”; (ι) Ode 9 is taken from the Gospel of St. Luke, chapter 1:68–79, which the Prophet Zechariah, father of John the Baptist, cried aloud when his son was born, thus: “Blessed be the Lord God of Israel,” etc. At the beginning of this 9th Ode is found also the Canticle of the Theotokos in Luke, chapter 1:46, which she, bearing in her womb the Saviour, cried aloud thus: “My soul doth magnify the Lord, and my spirit hath rejoiced in God my Saviour.” — These Canticles of Moses are very important, for they are the foundation for the canons of the Sunday service. — Now they are read before the odes of the canon only in the Great Fast. Besides this, Ode 1 and Ode 8 are also sung on Great Saturday at Vespers between the 15 paroemias with beautiful hypophonia. Furthermore, it is necessary to note that when there was not yet a Sunday canon in the Church of Christ, these canticles were probably used.

8. **A Verse** [*Stikh*] (in Greek: *stichos* [στίχος] == *versiculus*) is a short chant, taken either from the psalms, or from the Holy Scripture of the New or Old Testament, proclaimed before the stichera according to the melody of the

=== LEAF p67 ===
[Book Page 43]

stichera. Verses are either taken, or given [prescribed], or antiphonal. (α) Taken verses are verses from the evening psalms at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) and from the psalms at the Praises [Lauds] at Matins and from the Beatitudes of Jesus Christ;[^48] (β) Given verses are according to the dogmatic significance of the feasts and rites at Vespers, at the aposticha, at Compline, at the Litiya, at Matins at the aposticha, and at the Royal Hours, and on various occasions;[^49] (γ) Besides these there are also antiphonal verses, of which the most renowned are those verses which are sung at Matins after the Polyeleos at the exaltations [megalynaria], where after the verses there is for the most part the refrain “We magnify thee,” furthermore at “My soul doth magnify the Lord,” where “More honorable than the Cherubim” (*Честнѣйшую*) is sung, or at the Liturgy at the antiphons, where after the verses “By the prayers of the Mother of God” or “Save us, O Son of God” is repeated.

9. **The Prokimenon** (in Greek: *prokeimenon distichon* [προκείμενον δίσтиχον] == *praevium distichon*, from the word *prokeimai* [πρόκειμαι] == *propositus sum vel positus sum ante*) is a two-fold verse, which is taken for the most part from the psalms, and is set forth before the reading of Holy Scripture and reminds the faithful of Holy Scripture, or of some extraordinary chant, the subject of which it contains within itself in brief (*complexive*). Prokimena regarding their usage are: (α) Vespers prokimena, which are used at Vespers both great and small; (β) Matins prokimena, which are taken only at great Matins; (γ) Liturgical prokimena, which are used at Liturgies; (δ) Finally, prokimena according to various services and needs. — Both movable and immovable feasts have their proper prokimena. Nevertheless, of the prokimena

=== LEAF p68 ===
[Book Page 44]

prescribed for movable feasts the most renowned are: the daily prokimena used at Vespers throughout the entire week;[^50] furthermore the Sunday prokimena sung at Matins according to the 8 tones, and the Sunday prokimena used at Liturgies according to the 8 tones.[^51] — If in the church books at evening divine services we find this phrase: “Prokimenon of the day” (*prokeimenon tes hemeras* [προκείμενον τῆς ἡμέρας]), then under this we always understand the movable day, that is, Monday, Tuesday, etc., whose prokimenon we take. As regards melody, prokimena are divided into: (α) Great, and (β) Small. Great prokimena are sung with a longer melody, while small ones are sung with a shorter melody.

10. **Stichera** (in Greek: *sticheron* [стиχηρὸν] == *versus*) are strophes of a single poem composed according to dogmatic significance or according to the propriety of the feast, before which a short verse is sung, and that in such wise that the subject of this verse is explained, amplified, or interpreted in the sticheron. Stichera according to liturgical usage are stichera: (α) At Vespers at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) and at the Aposticha; (β) At Compline; (γ) At the Litiya; (δ) At Matins at the Praises [Lauds] and at the Aposticha; (ε) At the Royal Hours; and (ϛ) At the Typika [Obyednytsia]. As regards melody they are sung according to the 8 tones, and the tones of the stichera are designated in the church books. Of stichera according to their dogmatic significance the most renowned are: (α) Resurrectional (*anastasima* [ἀναστάσιμα]), in which the Resurrection of Christ is glorified; (β) Cross-resurrectional (*stauroanastasima* [σταυροαναστάσιма]), in which the Cross and Resurrection of Christ are glorified; (γ) Triadica (*triadika* [тριαδικὰ]), composed in honor of the Holy Trinity; (δ) Theotokia (*theotokia* [θεοτόκια]), composed in honor of the Most Holy Theotokos; (ε) Stavrotheotokia (*staurotheotokia* [σταυροθεοτόκια]), in honor of the compassions [sorrows] of the Mother of God; (ϛ) Martyria (*martyrika* [μαρτυρικὰ]), composed in honor of the holy martyrs; (η) Stichera of Pascha, composed in honor of the Feast of Pascha; (ϑ) Stichera of the departed (*nekrosima* [νεκρώσιма]), wherein supplications are set forth for the deceased; (ι) Eastern stichera (*anatolika* [ἀνατολικὰ]), which it is necessary to chant before the rising of the sun. (κ) Besides these, most especially worthy of note are the so-called “Evangelical Stichera,” of which there are 11 and which are sung at Sunday Matins (at the Praises [Lauds], and that at “Glory” (*Сла́ва*)), and which are composed according to the 11 Matins Gospels, being in union with them as regards their subject matter.

=== LEAF p69 ===
[Book Page 45]

11. **An Automelon [Idiomelon]** (in Greek: *idiomelon* [ἰδιόμελον]) is such a sticheron that was not chanted according to the melody of either a troparion or a heirmos, but had its own proper and solemn melody (*idion echon melon* [ἴδιον ἔχον μέλος]), and therefore was not chanted with a verse, but without it. Of the automela [idiomela] the most renowned are those whose melody in the 8 tones was probably found for the first time in Bulgaria, and therefore they called them “Bulgarian” [*Bolgar*] chants.

12. **Prosomoia** [Model-stichera], that is, stichera (*stichera prosomoia* [στιχηρὰ προσόμοια]), are such original stichera after whose pattern other stichera are metrically composed, and according to whose melody others also are sung.

13. **A Troparion** (in Greek: *troparion* [троπάριον]). Some derive this name from the text, and others from the melody. Of those who explain that name from the text, that is, according to its meaning, there are some who derive this name from the Greek word *tropos* [τρόπος] == a manner of living (*modus vivendi*), for in troparia is contained the manner of life of a saint, or a description of the feast. Others again derive it from the Greek word *tropaios* [τρόπαιος] == a trophy of victory (*signum victoriae*), because in troparia the victory of a saint is glorified, or the triumph of a feast. Others again derive it in a metaphorical sense from the word *tropos* [τρόπος] == an image (*imago, figura*), and that because in troparia a saint is represented under various figures, such as: a ray, a star, etc. And finally, some derive it from the word *trepein* [тρέπειν] == to turn (*vertere*), because a troparion hath the aim to incline the faithful unto such a life as is represented to us in troparia. Those who explain it according to melody likewise derive it from *trepein* [тρέπειν] == to turn, because those poems with respect to melody are turned in various manners, that is, unto the tones, unto prosomoia, and unto other models. Moreover, troparia are either short hymns or strophes of a single long poem which they sang in church without accompaniment of music. And according to this, they are divided into two parts, into “independent” and into “subordinate.” 1) A troparion as independent is a short hymn chanted in church, wherein is expressed the essence of the day, of the feast, or of the intention, or the virtue-filled life of a saint is described. One may also in this sense call a troparion a principal or ruling chant (*canticum principale vel regens*). Independent troparia have 8 melodies (tones), and a single troparion hath only one, and not two melodies, unto which it is necessary each time to turn, as many times as the troparion is sung. Independent troparia

=== LEAF p70 ===
[Book Page 46]

are given both on movable feasts and on immovable feasts, both on Sundays and on weekdays; besides this, diverse intentions also have their proper troparia. Of troparia the most renowned are: the 8 Sunday troparia in the 8 tones chanted throughout 8 weeks. Every single troparion is joined with a Theotokion troparion, because according to the custom accepted in the Greek Church we at all prayers magnify the Mother of God and entrust ourselves unto her mighty intercession. Besides this, independent troparia are joined with a kontakion, an ikos, a synaxarion, and with a hypakoe.
2) Subordinate troparia are parts of a single poem; such are:
(α) Strophes of the odes of a canon, which after the pattern of the first strophe (that is, the heirmos) are metrically composed, and according to the melody of the first strophe (that is, the heirmos) it is necessary to chant them;[^52]
(β) Sessional hymns, or kathisma-troparia, subordinate unto the kathismata, because at the beginning of Matins, between the kathismata, they are chanted or read, and therefore are called sessional troparia (*versus sedendarii*), for when they read a kathisma and chanted those troparia, it was permitted to sit;[^53]
(γ) Exaposteilaria [troparia photagogika], subordinate unto the Sunday Matins Gospels (τροπάρια φωтаγώγικα), of which there are 11, just as there are 11 Gospels and 11 Evangelical Stichera;
(δ) Dismissal Theotokia (τροπάρια ἀπολυτίκια), by which a conclusion is set unto a service or unto some part of divine worship.

14. **A Kontakion** (in Greek: *kontakion* [κοντάкιον] == *breviculum*, from *kontos* [κονтὸς] == *brevis*). It is so called either from the text, for it is a short hymn (*hymnus brevis*), or from the melody (*en konto* [ἐν κονтῷ] == *breviter*), for it was necessary to chant it in a short melody (*brevis modulus*). Kontakia are in union with independent troparia and serve for the fulfillment of the thoughts composed in troparia.

15. **An Ikos** (in Greek: *oikos* [οἶкоς] == *domus vel imago*) is a hymn chanted in church which expresses the glory and significance of a feast or the praise of a saint more extensively than a kontakion and in figures. An ikos is the counterpart of a kontakion, for a kontakion describes a feast for us in brief, while an ikos describes it in its fullness (*amplius et diffusius*). An ikos always follows after a kontakion and is never read without a kontakion.

---

=== LEAF p71 ===
[Book Page 47]

16. **Hypakoe** (in Greek: *hypakoe* [ὑπακοὴ], from the word *hypakouo* [ὑπακούω] == to give heed, to hear, *aures praebere ad audiendum verbum vel respondere*) is a hymn worthy of wonder (in Greek: *oidion thaumaston* [ᾠδιον θαυμαστὸν]), which when the deacon chanted as an idiomelon (lit. 'samohlasno'), all the people listened unto it with God-fearing quietude; yet others say also that the deacon chanted only half, and the people half. The Sundays according to the 8 tones, and furthermore also certain feasts, have their proper hypakoe, which now among us is only read at Matins after the sessional hymns and at Little Compline. The hypakoe, although it is appointed in the place of the sessional hymns, when it is permitted to sit, nevertheless is to be sung and heard while standing.

17. **Canon** (from the Greek word *kanon* [κανών] == *regula*) in general is the longest church poem composed according to a determined order, consisting of 9 odes. A canon consists of 9 odes for this reason, namely, that by them men may be likened unto the 9 angelic choirs, who unceasingly glorify God. The canon is divided by little ektenias and other chants into three chief sections in honor of the three chief orders[^54] of angels, and that in such wise that after the 3rd Ode a “sessional hymn” is taken, after the 6th a “kontakion” and “ikos,” and after the 9th the “Exaposteilarion.” Canons according to their dogmatic significance are: (α) Resurrectional (*anastasimoi* [ἀναστάσιμοι]), (β) Cross (*staurosimoi* [σταυρόσιμοι]), (γ) Cross-resurrectional (*stauroanastasimoi* [σταυροαναστάσιμοι]), (δ) Triadica (*triadikoi* [τριάδικοι]), (ε) Theotokia (*theotokioi* [θεοτόκιοι]), (ϛ) Cross-Theotokia (*staurotheotokioi* [σταυροθεοτόκιοι]), (η) Dogmatic (*dogmatikoi* [δογμάτικοι]), (ϑ) Of the departed (*nekrosimoi* [νекρόσιμοι]), (ι) Supplicatory (*hiketerioi* [ἱκετήριοι]), and diverse ones according to Feasts of the Lord, of the Mother of God, and of Saints.

18. **An Ode** (in Greek: *oide* [ᾠδὴ]) is a part of a canon and is a longer poem consisting of several troparia similar to one another (that is, strophes) and chanted with a living voice, and not by means of any musical instruments. The first troparion of an ode is called the “Heirmos,” and before it a refrain is not sung; before the 2nd and 3rd a refrain is sung, etc.; before the penultimate troparion “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) is sung, and before the last “Now and.” The concluding troparion is called the “Katavasia”; and before it likewise a refrain is not sung.

19. **The Heirmos** (in Greek: *heirmos* [εἱρμός] == *tractus*, a link or connection) is the first troparion of an ode, which is independent in itself both in metrical composition and in melodic chanting, but with respect unto the other subsequent troparia it hath a connective function.

=== LEAF p72 ===
[Book Page 48]

For it unites, binds, and gathers these troparia into a single ode[^55] with respect to metrical composition and with respect to melodic chanting, as their prototype.

20. **A Refrain** [*Pripiv*] is a verse which is sung before the troparia subordinate unto the heirmos. The refrain is diverse according to the dogmatic significance of the ode. There are refrains: (α) Resurrectional, for example: “Glory, O Lord, to Thy holy resurrection!”; (β) Cross-resurrectional, for example: “Glory, O Lord, to Thy cross and resurrection!”; (γ) Theotokia, for example: “Most Holy Lady Theotokos, save us!”; (δ) Unto the Holy Apostles, for example: “Holy Apostle of Christ, pray unto God for us”; (ε) Unto Holy Hierarchs, for example: “Holy Father and Hierarch, pray unto God for us”; (ϛ) Unto Venerable Fathers, for example: “Holy Venerable Father, pray unto God for us”; (η) Unto Venerable Women, for example: “Venerable Mother, pray unto God for us!”; (ϑ) Unto Martyrs, for example: “Holy Martyr, pray unto God for us!” etc.

21. **Katavasia** (from the Greek word *katabaino* [καταβαίνω] == descent) is the last troparion of an ode, which when they chanted, both choirs came forth into the midst of the church, and there sang this troparion. A katavasia is either: (α) Ordinary, that is, it is the heirmos itself repeated at the end of the ode; or (β) Prescribed, that is, appointed for a certain season, and such are the heirmoi of festal canons. For example, the prescribed katavasia after the Apodosis of the Exaltation of the Precious Cross until November 21 is: “I shall open my mouth” (*Отверзу*); from November 21 until January 1 it is: “Christ is born.”

Note 1. The Sunday canon was composed on the foundation of the 9 Canticles of Moses of the Old Testament by St. John Damascene, namely: (α) The Heirmos of the First Ode was composed on the foundation of the thanksgiving canticle sung by Miriam, sister of Moses, after the passage of the Hebrews through the Red Sea (Exod. 15:1–19); (β) The Heirmos of the Second Ode, which is taken only in days of penance, that is, in the Great Fast, but at other times is omitted, was composed on the foundation of the canticle of Moses at the time of the march of the Hebrews through the wilderness: “Give heed, O heaven, and I will speak” (Deut. 32:1–44);[^56] (γ) The Heirmos of the Third Ode was taken from the canticle of Hannah,

=== LEAF p73 ===
[Book Page 49]

mother of the Prophet Samuel: “My heart is established in the Lord” (1 Kings [1 Samuel] 2); (δ) The Heirmos of the Fourth Ode was taken from the canticle of the Prophet Habakkuk, who having seen God coming forth from the shaded mountain, cried aloud: “Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid” (Hab. 3:2–20); (ε) The Heirmos of the Fifth Ode was taken from the book of the Prophet Isaiah, who foretold the birth of Emmanuel from the Virgin: “Early in the morning my spirit seeks Thee, O God” (Isa. 26:9–20); (ϛ) The Heirmos of the Sixth Ode was taken from the words of the Prophet Jonah, who after his three-day stay in the belly of the whale reached dry land: “I cried in mine affliction unto the Lord God” (Jonah 2:1–7); (η) The Heirmos of the Seventh Ode was taken from the canticle of the Three Youths in the Babylonian furnace sung by them: “Blessed art Thou, O Lord God of our fathers” (Dan. 3:57–72); (ϑ) The Heirmos of the Eighth Ode was taken from the thanksgiving canticle of those same Three Youths which they sang when delivered from the furnace, saying: “Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord; sing and exalt Him above all forever” (Dan. 3:57–72); (ι) The Heirmos of the Ninth Ode derives its thoughts from the thanksgiving canticle of the Prophet Zechariah, father of St. John the Baptist: “Blessed be the Lord God of Israel” (Luke 1:57–79). It is necessary to note that in the canon at the Eighth Ode instead of “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) and instead of “Now and” the Trinitarian refrain is read: “Let us bless the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit, the Lord,” etc.; while before the 8th Katavasia there is sung: “We praise, we bless, we worship the Lord.” After the 8th Katavasia, but before the 9th Heirmos at Matins the Priest proclaims: “The Theotokos and Mother of Light let us magnify in hymns,” whereupon there is sung the Canticle of the Theotokos: “My soul doth magnify the Lord,” or the magnification of the feast. After the canon at Matins on weekdays there is sung: “It is truly right.”

Note 2. Since at Matins Sunday hath three canons, namely: Resurrectional, Cross-resurrectional, and Theotokion; Monday two: Unto the Angels and of Repentance; Tuesday two: Unto the Forerunner and of Repentance; Wednesday and Friday: Unto the Cross and unto the Theotokos; Thursday: Unto the Apostles and Nicholas; and Saturday: Unto All Saints and of the Departed; unto which sometimes a 3rd or even a 4th is added if a celebrated Saint is kept: therefore the Typikon always accurately indicates how many troparia must be taken for the individual odes, and that in these words: the canon on 8, on 12, on 14, or on 16, that is, each single ode consists of 8, 12, 14, or 16 troparia, of which the first troparion is the heirmos, and the last the katavasia. Furthermore, the Typikon will also show how many troparia

=== LEAF p74 ===
[Book Page 50]

are taken from one, how many from another, how many from the third, and how many from the fourth canon out of the multiple canons falling upon a single day; for example, the Sunday canon on 6, the Cross-resurrectional is omitted, the Theotokion on 4, and unto the Saint on 4 — all together it is taken on 14; which rubric only signifies that from the odes of the Resurrectional canon it is necessary to take 6, from the Theotokion 4 troparia, and from the canon of the Saint likewise 4; the heirmos will be taken of Sunday, and the katavasia the prescribed one: and all the troparia will be 14.

22. **The Synaxarion** (in Greek: *synaxarion* [συναξάριον]) is a short compendium of the history of a feast or of the life of a saint. The synaxarion is read at Matins in the canon after the 6th Ode following the kontakion and ikos. It originates from the Greek word *synaxis* [συνάξις] == assembly.

23. At Matins in the canon after the 9th Ode the **Exaposteilarion** (lit. 'Svetilen') or “Poslatelen” (in Greek: *exapostilarion* [ἐξаποστειλάριον], from *exapostello* [ἐξαποστέλλω] == I send forth) is taken.

Some say that the exaposteilarion is the very same thing as the hymn of light (lit. 'Svetilen'), that is, it is a hymn containing within itself a supplication for the sending down of heavenly light, or a supplication for the enlightenment of our mind through heavenly light.

Others, however, assert that the exaposteilarion and the hymn of light (lit. 'Svetilen') are not one and the same, for in the exaposteilarion the sending forth of the disciples and Apostles of Christ unto the Gentiles is glorified, while in the hymns of light (lit. 'Svetilen') the presence of heavenly light and that the intelligible light may shine forth is entreated. Besides this, there are also those who derive this designation from the circumstance that in ancient times for the singing of these hymns they sent forth the reader into the midst of the church.

24. **Magnification** [*Velichaniye*] is a laudatory chant (*canticum laudativum*) consisting of a single refrain, which in chanting is for the most part repeatedly sung after certain verses of a psalm. Magnifications are taken: (α) At Matins after the Polyeleos; (β) At Matins in the canon before the 9th Ode; (γ) At the Liturgy instead of “It is truly right” (*Достойно есть*) (and for this reason they are also called “Zadostoinyky”); and (δ) At dismissals instead of “More honorable.” — As regards the Magnifications sung after the Polyeleos, concerning these it is necessary to note that they consist of a single given refrain which is repeated after the verses, and at the end at “Alleluia” the “Bulgarian refrain” is taken if there be one, but if there be no Bulgarian refrain, then the given refrain is repeated. — In the Matins canon before the 9th Ode there is usually taken the ordinary Magnification “More honorable than the Cherubim,” in which this refrain is repeated after the verses of the Canticle of the Theotokos: “My soul doth magnify the Lord” (*Вели́читъ дꙋша̀ моя̀ Го́спода...*); but this ordinary magnification nevertheless is omitted on Feasts of the Lord

=== LEAF p75 ===
[Book Page 51]

and of the Mother of God, for then at the exclamation of the Priest: “The Theotokos and Mother of Light,” there is sung the magnification of the feast, consisting of a single refrain with the heirmos of the ninth ode. — This given magnification is taken also at the Liturgy instead of “It is truly right.”[^57]

25. **The Polyeleos** (in Greek: *polyeleos* [πολύελεος], from *polys* [πολύς] and *eleos* [ἔλεος]) is a chant consisting of selected verses taken from two psalms of the 19th Kathisma (“Praise ye the name of the Lord” and “O give thanks unto the Lord”),[^58] in which chant the word “mercy” (*eleos* [ἔλεος]) is frequently repeated throughout those verses. Others derive this name from *polys* [πολύς] == many and *elaion* [ἔλαιον] == oil, and that because in ancient times when they chanted this canticle in church, they lighted many lamps filled with oil.[^59] — The Polyeleos is ordinarily taken at Great Matins on Feasts of the Lord, of the Mother of God, and of Great Saints, and that instead of the 3rd Kathisma, when it is joined with the magnification. — On Sunday it is not taken, except from September 22 until December 20, and furthermore from January 14 until Cheesefare Sunday, when there are usually appended also the Bulgarian refrains in the Heirmologion: “Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit: Glory to Thee, O Holy Trinity. Now and: Rejoice, thou who art full of grace. Alleluia (thrice), glory to Thee, O God: Most Pure Theotokos Mary,” and immediately “The Angelic Council” (*Аггельскій соборъ*); while on Cheesefare Sunday, Meatfare Sunday, and the Sunday of the Prodigal Son, unto the two psalms of the 19th Kathisma there is appended also a 3rd psalm, that is, Psalm 136: “By the rivers of Babylon,” which is sung with a solemn Alleluia, while the Bulgarian refrains are omitted.

=== LEAF p76 ===
[Book Page 52]

26. The Sunday troparia: “The Angelic Council,” with the refrain: “Blessed art Thou, O Lord: teach me Thy statutes,” are called “troparia after the Blameless,” and that because they are sung only at Sunday Matins instead of the 3rd sessional hymn, and that after the 3rd Kathisma (that is, the 17th), or better said, after Psalm 118 (“Blessed are the undefiled”), which psalm is called the “blameless” psalm.[^60]

27. **Graduals** (lit. 'Stepenna', Slavonic: *Stepenny*) are stichera composed by St. John Damascene on the basis of the gradual psalms (119–133)[^61] in the 8 tones. Each single tone hath its own gradual stichera, which are chanted not with verses, but without verses in antiphony, and according to this they are also divided into 3 antiphons. Each antiphon hath three stichera, and the third sticheron of each antiphon is composed in honor of the Holy Trinity. (Among us they are only read, except on feast days with a Litiya, when from the graduals of Tone 4 the 1st antiphon is sung: “From my youth.”)

28. **Theotokion** (in Greek: *Theotokion* [Θеοτόκιον]) is a poem composed in honor of the Most Holy Theotokos and chanted with a living voice in church. Theotokia are divided into: (α) Theotokia-troparia, of which some are “independent” (such are the troparia composed for Feasts of the Mother of God); while others are “subordinate” (such are all troparia on Sunday and on other days

=== LEAF p77 ===
[Book Page 53]

chanted at “Now” with the resurrectional troparion or with the heirmos.[^62] (β) Theotokia-stichera, which are taken at Vespers at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) at “Now,” at the aposticha at “Now,” at the Litiya at “Now,” at Matins at the Praises [Lauds] and at the aposticha at “Now.” Of these Theotokia-stichera the most renowned are those Sunday ones which are sung at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) at “Now” and at the aposticha at “Now,” for of these the former are called “Dogmata,” and the latter “Dogmatika,” because they always contain within themselves the dogma relating unto the incarnation of the Son of God.[^63] (γ) There are Theotokion-canons, which are sung on Feasts of the Mother of God at Matins; and besides this at Compline there is a canon of the Theotokos, while at Sunday Matins the third canon likewise is in honor of the Theotokos.

29. **Ektenia** [Litany] (from the Greek word *ektenes* [ἐκτενὴς] and *euche* [εὐχὴ] == *oratio prolixa, protensa, sine intermissione fusa*) is a series of joined supplications or petitions (*concatenata precum series*), upon the conclusion of which, for the fulfillment of these petitions, we entreat intercession from the Most Holy Virgin and from the Saints; and at the end, as a seal of the suppliant petitions, the Priest loudly utters the exclamation in honor of the Holy Trinity, firmly confident that all this shall be unto the honor, glory, and worship of God. An ektenia is: (α) The Peace Ektenia [Great Litany] (“In peace let us pray unto the Lord”); (β) The Insistent [Augmented Litany] (“Have mercy on us, O God” (*Помилуй насъ Боже*)); (γ) The Supplicatory [Litany of Fervent Supplication] (“Let us complete our prayer unto the Lord” (*И҆спо́лнимъ моли́твꙋ на́шꙋ...*)); (δ) The Little Ektenia (“Again and again in peace let us pray unto the Lord”); (ε) The Litiya Ektenia (“O God, save Thy people”); (ϛ) At the Midnight Office (“Furthermore we pray that this city... may be preserved”); (η) For the Departed; (ϑ) Ektenia of the Catechumens (“Pray, ye catechumens”); (ι) Ektenia for the Enlightened in the Great Fast at the Liturgy of the Presanctified; (κ) And ektenias for diverse needs, as for every petition, over the sick, etc., etc.

30. **A Bow** (in Greek: *metanoia* [μετάνοια] == *poenitentia*) is an outward sign of repentance, reverence, or greeting. A bow in church is twofold: small and great. (α) A small bow (*metanoia mikra* [μετάνοια μικρὰ]) is such a sign whereby a man inclines only the head and torso, but not the knees (*choris gonyklisias* [χωрὶς γονυκλισίας]). A great bow (*metanoia megale* [μετάνοια μεγάλη]) is such a sign wherein a man inclines not only the head and torso, but also the knees, and touches the earth with his hands and head. Great bows occur on Wednesday and on Friday of Cheesefare Week, furthermore on Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, and Friday of all weeks of the Great Fast, and cease on Great Wednesday at the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.[^64]

=== LEAF p78 ===
[Book Page 54]

31. **Litiya** signifies either a procession (*processio*), or a prayer in procession (*supplicatio in processione*). In the ancient times of the Church such processions (that is, litiyas) were in use in Constantinople, where on determined days the Patriarch and the Emperor went forth annually with a procession into the public square (*forum*) and there offered supplication for the salvation of the entire city. But besides these determined days, such processions occurred also upon the appearance of any calamity, for example, an earthquake, hail, famine.... Now, however, by a litiya we understand a prayer ordinarily concluded in procession, which takes place at Vespers in the narthex, or before the ambo, and wherein we entreat God that He may deliver us from famine, pestilence, etc., that He may bless us and multiply for us the fruits of the earth; and it is joined with the blessing of loaves, wheat, wine, and oil. — Litiya is joined with Vespers,[^65] while with Great Compline it is concluded only on the Nativity of Christ, on Theophany, and on the Annunciation, yet only then if this feast falls on a fast day in the Great Fast.

32. **The Akathist** (in Greek: *hymnos akathistos* [ὕμνος ἀκάθιστος], from the word *a* [ἀ] and *kathizo* [καθίζω]; *a* is privative, *kathizo* == to sit, or it is a hymn lacking sitting, without sitting) is a thanksgiving poem composed in honor of our Lord Jesus Christ, the Most Holy Theotokos, or of some Saint, consisting of

=== LEAF p79 ===
[Book Page 55]

24 shorter or longer strophes with an antecedent initial troparion, which poem they sang not sitting, but standing. The shorter troparia ending always with “Alleluia” are called kontakia, while the longer ones ending always with “Rejoice, O Bride unwedded” or otherwise are called “ikoi.” Moreover, the entire poem is divided into four staseis (*staseis* [στάσεις]). Of the akathists the most renowned are: (α) The Akathist of the Annunciation, which is taken at Matins on the 5th Saturday of the Holy Great Fast; and (β) The Akathist of the Dormition, which among us is celebrated on the eve of the Forefeast of the Dormition in the evening.

33. **The Paraklesis** (in Greek: *paraklesis* [παράκλησις], from the word *parakaleo* [παρακαλέω] == *evoco auxilii gratia*, I call upon for help) is a service composed unto the Most Holy Theotokos, whereby the people have recourse unto the Most Holy Theotokos, asking her help (*officium impetratorium*), — consisting after the likeness of Matins of a canon, Gospel, and other laudatory stichera.

34. **The Parastas** (from the Greek word *parastasis* [παράστασις] == *intercessio, commendatio*) is a service joined with kolyvo, whereby we intercede for the departed. Kolyvo, which we present in remembrance and instead of the departed person himself, is prepared from boiled wheat, sweetened with honey, and adorned with diverse sweet fruits, and that because: wheat signifies the body of the deceased, which although it hath died and is buried in the earth, nevertheless in truth shall rise from the grave at the Second Coming of Christ. The sweetness of honey signifies that although the souls of the Saints already now enjoy divine joys, yet after the resurrection of the body they shall perfectly enjoy the sweetness of divine gladness. The adornment of diverse fruits signifies the radiant splendor of the beauty of the holy bodies. The parastas consists of a canon, which psalms precede, while a Little Panakhyda follows.

35. **The Dismissal** (in Greek: *apolysis* [ἀπόλυσις] == *dimissio*) is the concluding prayer of any rite (*completi officii conclusio*). In dismissals we call upon the Most Holy Virgin and the Saints (*invocatio B. Virginis et Sanctorum*) that they may intercede for us before God (*intercessio B. Virginis et Sanctorum*), so that God through the prayers of the Most Holy Virgin and the Saints may bless and save us. A dismissal with respect to its composition is threefold: great, small, and least. (α) The Great Dismissal is performed thus: “Wisdom. Bless. He Who is blessed, Christ our God, is and was and shall be, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Most Holy Theotokos, save us,” etc.

=== LEAF p80 ===
[Book Page 56]

(β) The Small Dismissal: “Wisdom. More honorable than the Cherubim... Glory to Thee, O Christ God, our hope, glory to Thee,” etc. (γ) The Least Dismissal begins only from “Glory to Thee, O Christ our God.” The Great Dismissal is taken at Vespers and at Matins, and that then if at Vespers there is an entrance, and at Matins the Great Doxology. The Small Dismissal is taken at Vespers and at Matins then if at Vespers there is no entrance, and at Matins there is no Great Doxology. The Least Dismissal is used at the celebration of the Holy Mysteries and at other rites. With respect to prescription, dismissals are: daily, festal, and for diverse intentions. — (α) Of the daily dismissals the most renowned is first: the Sunday dismissal, which begins thus: “May He Who rose from the dead, Christ our true God...” etc., in which are also the dismissals prescribed for the other days of the week,[^66] which all begin thus: “May Christ our true God...” (β) Of festal dismissals the most renowned are: the dismissals given for Feasts of the Lord, which begin thus, for example: “May He Who was born in a cave in Bethlehem of Judea and lay in a manger for our salvation, Christ our true God...” etc., or: “May He Who deigned to be baptized in the Jordan for our salvation, Christ our true God...” etc., etc., — or: “May He Who deigned to recline in the arms of righteous Simeon for our salvation, Christ our true God...” etc. The dismissal of the Feast of Pascha is called the “Dismissal with the Cross.” It is taken in Bright Week, on the Sundays of the Paschal season, on Mid-Pentecost Wednesday, and on the Apodosis of Pascha at the Liturgy, — and is concluded thus: The Priest: “Wisdom”; the Choir: “Bless,” etc.; the Priest: “May Christ Who rose from the dead, by death trampling down death, and to those in the tombs bestowing life, our true God...” etc.; the Choir: “Amen.” After this the Priest, turning unto the people with the Cross, signing them thrice with the sign of the Cross, saith: “Christ is risen!” And the people say: “Indeed He is risen!” and thus the blessing takes place. On Feasts of the Mother of God the daily dismissal is taken, only at the name of the Most Holy Virgin in the dismissal these words are inserted, for example: “whose Entry into the Temple we today celebrate with a bright feast,” or: “whose honorable Dormition we today celebrate with a bright feast,” etc. On Feasts of Saints we likewise take the daily dismissal, and the Saint is only commemorated or...

---

=== LEAF p81 ===
[Book Page 57]

thus: “whose solemn feast we brightly celebrate,” or “whose feast we today accomplish,” or “whose memory we today venerate,” etc., according as the Saint whose day we celebrate is either a great, or middle, or little feast. (γ) Of the dismissals for diverse intentions, however, the most renowned is that for the departed, which begins thus: “May He Who hath dominion over the quick and the dead, Christ our true God...” etc., and in which after the invocation of the Saints the names of the departed are also commemorated. Finally concerning dismissals it is also necessary to note that therein that Saint, male or female, unto whom the church is dedicated, is always commemorated by these words: “whose all-honorable temple this is.” — Within the afterfeast, when the Oktoechos is not taken, the festal dismissal is taken, except on Sunday. Within the feast of Pascha, however, the “Dismissal without the Cross” is taken, thus: The Priest: “Wisdom.” The Choir: “Shine, shine,” etc. The Priest: “May He Who rose from the dead...” etc., and thus: “Blessing.” If, however, the Apodosis of a feast falls on Sunday, we say the Sunday dismissal.

§. 23.

What Is Further Necessary to Note Concerning These Designations?

(α) The evening chant “Gladsome Light” (*Свѣте тихій*) is called the “Lucernarium Hymn” (*hymnos epilychnios* [ὕμνος ἐπιλύχνιος] == *hymnus lucernarius*). This hymn, as St. Basil saith,[^67] the Holy Martyr Athenogenes († 169) composed; nevertheless it is customarily attributed unto St. Sophronius, Patriarch of Jerusalem, probably for this reason, that he introduced it into church use in his province around the year 634.
(β) A twofold doxology (*doxologia* [δοξολογία]) do we have: little and great. The Little Doxology is: “Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit,” etc.; the Great, however, is “Glory to God in the highest.” This hymn is called also the Angelic Hymn (*hymnus angelicus*). — Here, nevertheless, it is also necessary to note that in church books, when the hymn “Glory to God in the highest” (*Слава во вышнихъ Богу*) is only to be read, then this is called the “Little Doxology,” whereas the singing of this hymn is called the “Great Doxology.”
(γ) The hymn “Holy God” (*Святы́й Бо́же*) is called the “Trisagion” (*hymnos trisagios* [ὕμνος τρισάγιος]). It came into church use during the time of

=== LEAF p82 ===
[Book Page 58]

St. Proclus, Patriarch of Constantinople († 446), and Emperor Theodosius II (408–450).[^68]
(δ) The hymn “Let us who mystically represent the Cherubim” is called the “Cherubic Hymn” (*hymnus Cherubicus*).[^69]
(ε) “Holy, Holy, Holy” is called the “Triumphal Hymn” (*hymnos epinikios* [ὕμνος ἐπινίκιος] == *hymnus victoriae et triumphi*).
(ϛ) Antidoron (in Greek: *antidoron* [ἀντίδωρον], from *anti* and *hagia dora* [ἀντὶ et ἅγια δῶρα]) are particles of the blessed prosphora wherefrom the Lamb was taken for the sacred action, which instead of the holy gifts, instead of Holy Communion, it is necessary to give unto those who did not participate in the Holy Mysteries.[^70] Pope St. Pius I, Universal Pontiff, instituted the distribution of the antidoron.[^71]

## Part II
### Concerning Church Services in Particular

Since public services, as was said above, are divided into three parts: (α) into pre-liturgical services, (β) into liturgical services, and (γ) into services following the Divine Liturgy; therefore, conformably unto this division, we divide this Part II into three sections: and in Section I we will speak in particular concerning pre-liturgical services; in Section II concerning liturgical services; and in Section III concerning the celebration of the Holy Mysteries and other sacred actions.

=== LEAF p83 ===
[Book Page 59]

### Section I

§. 24.

Concerning Pre-Liturgical Services

The pre-liturgical services are the following: (α) Vespers;[^72] (β) Compline; (γ) Midnight Office; (δ) Matins; (ε) The First, Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours;[^73] and (ϛ) Typika.[^74] The aggregation of all these services is called the “Church Rule” (*pensum canonicum*). According to present practice, each service among the adduced divine services is not celebrated separately, but they are rather combined, so that the entire church rule is celebrated at three intervals; and in that manner each day hath a threefold service: evening, morning, and daytime. (α) The evening service consists of the Ninth Hour, Vespers, and Compline. (β) The morning service consists of the Midnight Office, Matins, and the First Hour. (γ) The daytime service consists of the Third and Sixth Hours, the Divine Liturgy, or the Typika. In this section we will speak first concerning the individual parts of the church rule, and secondly concerning the church rule according to diverse occasions.

#### Chapter I
Parts of the Church Rule

##### Article I

§. 25.

Concerning Vespers

Vespers according to their compositions are divided in general: (1) into ordinary and (2) extraordinary. (1) Unto ordinary Vespers belong: (α) Little Vespers; (β) Daily Vespers;

=== LEAF p84 ===
[Book Page 60]

and (γ) Great Vespers, which ends either without Litiya or with Litiya. Of extraordinary Vespers, however, we adduce only the most renowned, and these are: (α) Vespers for the departed; (β) Vespers in the Great Fast; (γ) Vespers with Liturgy; (δ) Vespers of Holy and Great Friday with procession; (ε) Vespers with the Liturgy of St. Basil the Great and the blessing of loaves on Holy and Great Saturday; (ϛ) Vespers of Bright Week; (η) Vespers at the Descent of the Holy Spirit; (ϑ) Order of Vespers on the Eve of the Nativity, if this falls on Saturday or on Sunday.

##### 1) Ordinary Vespers

§. 26.

Order of Little Vespers

The Priest, having come into the church and taken upon himself the epitrachelion, bowing before the royal doors makes the usual beginning, that is: “Blessed is our God,” “Heavenly King,” the Trisagion, “Most Holy Trinity,” “Our Father” (*Отче нашъ*) with the Exclamation, “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (12 times), “Come, let us worship” (*Прїиди́те поклони́мся*) (thrice). (Thus far the usual beginning). And immediately the reader in a quiet voice reads the evening psalm: “Bless the Lord, O my soul.” After the psalm: “Glory,” and “Now and,” “Alleluia” (thrice) without chanting. There is no Litany of Peace, but the Priest enters into the sanctuary, and the choir instead of it saith: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (12 times), and immediately chants: “Lord, I have cried.” Stichera on 4, that is, from the verse: “From the morning watch.” At the conclusion of the stichera: “Glory,” and “Now and,” Theotokion. After this the Priest at his place in the sanctuary proclaims: “Wisdom! Stand upright!” (*Премꙋ́дрость про́сти!*) and the choir sings: “Gladsome Light.” At “Let us attend! Peace be to all! Wisdom, let us attend!” the choir sings the Daily Prokimenon, after which the reader reads: “Vouchsafe, O Lord.” After “Vouchsafe” (*Сподо́би*) there is no litany “Let us complete our evening prayer,” but instead of this the choir saith: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (12 times), and immediately sings the aposticha stichera (3). After “Glory,” and “Now and” follows “Now lettest Thou,” then: Trisagion, “Our Father” (*Отче нашъ*) with the Exclamation, Troparion of the feast or troparion unto the Saint, “Glory,” and “Now and,” Theotokion. After these the Priest comes forth from the sanctuary and chants the litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” with the Least Dismissal: from “Glory to Thee, O Christ God, our hope, glory to Thee.”

Little Vespers is celebrated on Feasts of the Lord (except the Nativity and Theophany), Feasts of the Mother of God, and of Solemn Saints always in the evening, namely, when on the morrow Great Vespers with Litiya and the blessing of loaves takes place.

=== LEAF p85 ===
[Book Page 61]

§. 27.

Order of Daily Vespers without Entrance

The Priest comes forth in the epitrachelion before the royal doors, and having made bows, makes the usual beginning as at Little Vespers, that is: “Blessed is our God,” “Heavenly King,” the Trisagion, “Most Holy Trinity,” “Our Father” (*Отче нашъ*) with the Exclamation, “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (12 times), and “Come, let us worship” (*Прїиди́те поклони́мся*) (thrice). After this, Psalm 103: “Bless the Lord, O my soul.” Upon the conclusion of this, “Glory,” and “Now and,” “Alleluia” (thrice), “Glory to Thee, O God” with singing; and immediately the Priest loudly proclaims the Litany of Peace. After this litany the Priest goes into the sanctuary, and the chanter reads the kathisma of the day with three antiphons, but before it: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (thrice), “Glory,” and “Now and.” Upon the conclusion of the kathisma: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (twice), “To Thee, O Lord,” “Amen.” And immediately from Psalm 140 the first verse “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) and the second verse “Let my prayer be set forth” (*Да исправится*) are sung, while the remaining verses of the psalm are read. Psalm 141, “With my voice unto the Lord,” is also read, likewise also Psalm 129, “Out of the depths,” even unto the verse “If Thou, Lord, shouldest mark iniquities”; for here we set 6 verses and chant them unto the stichera appointed at “Lord, I have cried.” After “Glory,” and “Now and,” the Priest loudly proclaims: “Wisdom! Stand upright!” (*Премꙋ́дрость про́сти!*) and the choir sings: “Gladsome Light.” After this the Priest proclaims: “Let us attend! Peace be to all! Wisdom, let us attend!” whereunto the choir sings the Daily Prokimenon which is in the Horologion, and afterwards there is read: “Vouchsafe, O Lord.” Thereafter the Priest comes forth from the sanctuary before the royal doors and loudly proclaims the litany: “Let us complete.” Having finished this litany and with the second exclamation: “Blessed and glorified be the majesty,” he goes into the holy sanctuary. After that, the aposticha stichera (3), then “Now lettest Thou.” Trisagion. “Our Father” (*Отче нашъ*) with the Exclamation. Troparion unto the Saint who falls on the morning of the day. “Glory,” and “Now and,” Dismissal Theotokion according to the tone and day. During the singing of the troparia the Priest comes forth, and after the singing saith the litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” and the Exclamation. After this the Small Dismissal from “Wisdom.”

This Vespers is celebrated on all weekdays, if on the morning of the day there be no Great Saint.

§. 28.

Order of Great Vespers with Entrance

(α) Great Vespers without Litiya. — The Priest, having put on the epitrachelion and bowed before the royal doors unto the holy sanctuary, pro-

=== LEAF p86 ===
[Book Page 62]

claims: “Blessed is our God,” and the choir saith: “Amen.” And immediately: “Heavenly King,” Trisagion, “Most Holy Trinity,” “Our Father” (*Отче нашъ*) with the Exclamation, “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (12 times), “Come, let us worship” (*Прїиди́те поклони́мся*) (thrice). After this usual beginning the reader or chanter reads the “Introductory Psalm” [Psalm 103]: “Bless the Lord, O my soul.” During this the Priest, standing before the royal doors, recites the Lucernarium prayers. When the psalm hath been read, the chanter saith: “Glory,” and “Now and,” “Alleluia” (thrice, with singing). The Priest loudly proclaims the litany: “In peace let us pray unto the Lord.” After the Exclamation the Priest goes into the sanctuary, and the chanter reads the kathisma of the Psalter with antiphons, and that in such wise that before it he saith: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (thrice), “Glory,” and “Now and”; and after it he saith: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (twice), “To Thee, O Lord,” “Amen.” After this from “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) in the appointed tone the first two verses are sung. Having read the remaining verses of Psalms 140 and 141, the chanter sets 10 verses (“Bring my soul out of prison,” etc.) or 8 (“Out of the depths”), and sings the stichera appointed at “Lord, I have cried.” When the verse “For His mercy is confirmed” is said, then the Priest vests in the phelonion. When they begin to sing: “Glory,” and “Now and,” the Priest puts incense before the Holy Table into the censer, and going forth around the holy table, by the northern side makes the “entrance” with the censer, saying the prayer: “O good and man-befriending King, Who hast blessed all things: we diligently pray Thee, with contrite heart and humble spirit, bless our entrances and exits, O Christ our true God, for Thy coming, and ascension, and life with men is blessed, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.”[^75] Having taken his stand before the royal doors, the Priest blesses the censer and censes the holy sanctuary, the icon of Christ and of the Mother of God, and awaits the conclusion of the Theotokion. After this he opens the royal doors, raises the censer, and traces with it the sign of the cross, proclaiming: “Wisdom! Stand upright!” (*Премꙋ́дрость про́сти!*) and the choir sings: “Gladsome Light.” Afterwards the Priest, having entered into the holy sanctuary, censes cruciformly around the holy table, and standing in the royal doors, turned toward the people, censes the icon of Christ, of the Mother of God, and thus the people. After this the Priest goes by the right side unto the high place. When the Priest is already seated upon the high place, upon the conclusion of the singing of “Gladsome Light,” the Priest proclaims: “Let us attend! Peace be to all! Wisdom, let us attend!” Unto this the choir sings either the Great Prokimenon (4 times), and then there are no paremias; or the Little Prokimenon of the day (twice), and then after the prokimenon on Polyeleos feasts

=== LEAF p87 ===
[Book Page 63]

there occur 3 readings;[^76] and that in such wise that the Priest before each reading proclaims: “Wisdom!” (*Премꙋ́дрость!*) and the reader: “The reading from the Prophecy of Isaiah,” or “The reading from the Proverbs,” or “The reading from the Catholic Epistle of Peter”; and again the Priest: “Let us attend!” (*Во́ньмемъ*) and the reader reads the paremia. During the reading of the 3rd paremia the Priest goes by the left side unto the holy table. When the reader hath finished the paremia, the Priest shuts the royal doors, and the litany “Let us all say” (*Рцѣмъ вси*) is sung. After this there is read “Vouchsafe,” and immediately the litany “Let us complete” (*Исполнимъ*) is sung, and the Exclamation from the Priest: “For a good God and lover of mankind Thou art,” etc., “Peace be to all,” and again the Exclamation: “Blessed and glorified be the majesty of Thy kingdom.” Then they begin to sing the aposticha stichera (3), “Glory,” and “Now and,” Theotokion. After this “Now lettest Thou.” Trisagion. “Our Father” (*Отче нашъ*) with the Exclamation from the Priest. After the Exclamation the choir sings the troparia according to custom, as the Typikon prescribes. Upon the conclusion of the troparia the Great Dismissal takes place, in which at “Wisdom” the royal doors are opened, and after the blessing they are shut. This Vespers is celebrated among us on Saturday evening and on other middle feasts on which at Matins there is a Polyeleos.

(β) Order of Great Vespers with Litiya. — The Priest, having entered into the church and made the usual bows before the royal doors, enters into the holy sanctuary, and here puts on the epitrachelion, having kissed the cross upon it, and vests in the phelonion. And vested he comes unto the Holy Table, where, bowing low, he opens the royal doors. Thereafter he blesses incense with the prayer: “Incense we offer unto Thee, O Christ our God, for an odor of spiritual sweetness; which do Thou receive upon Thy heavenly altar, and send down upon us the divine grace and the gift of Thy Most Holy Spirit” — and censes around the holy table, cruciformly, and the prothesis. Afterwards he comes forth through the royal doors onto the ambo, where he censes the icons and the entire church. He returns again unto the royal doors, censes the Holy Table, and unto the choir saying: “Command!” he proclaims: “O Lord, bless!” “Glory to the holy, consubstantial...” etc. After this Exclamation he enters through the royal doors into the holy sanctuary, hands over the censer, shuts the royal doors, and having divested of the phelonion, in the epitrachelion comes forth before the royal doors, where, having bowed, he recites the evening prayers. The choir during this time, beginning from “Come, let us worship,” reads the Introductory Psalm: “Bless the Lord, O my soul.” When the psalm is finished and the choir hath sung: “Alleluia” (thrice), “Glory to Thee, O God” — the Priest before

=== LEAF p88 ===
[Book Page 64]

the royal doors loudly proclaims the litany: “In peace let us pray unto the Lord.” After the Exclamation “For unto Thee is due,” the choir reads the kathisma, saying beforehand: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (thrice), “Glory,” and “Now and”; while the Priest departs unto his place in the sanctuary, and after the kathisma hath been read, secretly recites the litany: “Again and again” with the Exclamation “For Thine is the majesty,” and the choir sings: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (twice), “To Thee, O Lord,” “Amen.” After this the choir sings: “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) and the proper stichera on 8 or on 10, and that in the occurring tone.
