At Great Vespers: a) At “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) (in epitrachelion).[^326] b) At the Great Entrance. c) Before the beginning of the All-Night Vigil in phelonion and with open doors. d) At the Litiya according to custom, for the Typikon itself doth not prescribe censing the Holy Table, nor the Iconostasis, but only the vestibule and the people there. e) At the blessing of loaves, only the offered gifts from four sides. f) At Great Compline on the Nativity of Christ, Theophany, and the Annunciation, before the beginning, with open Royal Doors — in phelonion.
Note: At Small Vespers only once, at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) (but this is omitted among us).
2. At Great Matins: At the beginning, when there is a vigil, we do not cense; but we cense: a) At the beginning of Matins in

=== LEAF p307 ===
[Book Page 283]

(p. 283)

epitrachelion at the time of the very beginning: “Blessed is our God,” without opening the Royal Doors, but going forth through the northern doors — we cense the whole church and the vestibule (but among us this doth not take place). b) At the time of singing: “Let every breath praise the Lord” (*Вся́кое дыха́нїе да хва́литъ Го́спода*) before the Gospel. c) After the 8th Ode, when they sing: “My soul doth magnify the Lord” (*Вели́читъ дꙋша̀ моя̀ Го́спода...*) or the refrains of the feast.[^327]
Note: At weekday Matins it is prescribed to cense the Sanctuary and the whole church only at the very beginning and at the 9th Ode in epitrachelion with closed Royal Doors (but that doth not take place among us).
3. At the Divine Liturgies: a) At the Proskomide we cense each veil and the gifts already covered, with the prayer: “Blessed is our God Who art thus well-pleased.” b) Before the beginning of the Liturgy. c) At the time of the reading of the Apostle, the Table of Oblation, the entire Sanctuary, and from the Royal Doors the people are censed. d) At the Cherubic Hymn. e) After the consecration of the Holy Gifts at “Especially.” f) At the end, if there be an offering, that is, *kolyvo*, then before the prayer we cense it. g) If at the Liturgy for the departed there is also a Panakhyda, then we cense at “With the spirits of the righteous” the whole church (excepting within the Sanctuary); further in the Litany for the departed at each petition three times the Sanctuary; and at “In blessed dormition” likewise.
4. At funerals and parastases censing takes place: a) at the beginning; b) after each litany for the departed; and c) at “With the spirits of the righteous.”
5. Further, we use the censer also at Great Blessings of Water, at the beginning or before the Gospel, and besides this also at many church sanctifications and blessings, but for this see the Trebnik.

§. 219.

What is Necessary to Note Concerning the Color of Church Vestments?

In the Western-Latin Church the color of church vestments is strictly regulated, and it is fivefold: white, red [crimson], green, violet, and black;[^328] further, vestments of diverse colors may be used freely only if one of these five colors is the fundamental color; moreover, it is most precisely

=== LEAF p308 ===
[Book Page 284]

(p. 284)

determined also which color of vestments it behoves to use on individual days and at various divine services.
Among us in the Eastern-Greek Church, however, regarding the color of church vestments there is no precise arrangement. The ecclesiastical books speak of bright, red [crimson], and black vestments.
Bright vestments[^329] are used on festive days, red [crimson] on fasting days, and black on days of commemoration for the departed.[^330]
Furthermore, the color of vestments hath its symbolic significance: for white vestments are a symbol of innocence, sanctity, and joy; red vestments are a symbol of sorrow and repentance;[^331] while black vestments are a sign of grief. And on this account it is not fitting to use vestments of diverse color, or of such color as is not accepted by the Church or doth not correspond with the mystery of the feast. For if the fatherland hath its colors, then let the Church also have its colors, as is evident.

§. 220.

What is Necessary to Know Concerning the Ecclesiastical Language?

The language of divine services is twofold: $\alpha$) symbolic (*képnyelv* [symbolic language]), and $\beta$) verbal (*szóbeli nyelv* [spoken language]).[^332]
In order that a man may understand the symbolic language of divine services, he must first learn what essence or what symbolic meaning each individual service hath according to its theological sense, and therein each individual liturgical action.
As for the verbal language, concerning it we indicate this: that it must be, according to the common law of each church (*juxta jus commune*), more or less distinct from the common and ordinarily spoken tongue (*köznyelv* [vernacular]), and unchangeable, that is, a dead language (*holt nyelv* [dead language]).

=== LEAF p309 ===
[Book Page 285]

(p. 285)

The difference between the Eastern and Western Churches — regarding the discipline of the ecclesiastical language — is this: that the Western Church, according to its particular law (*juxta jus privatum*), uses only one[^333] dead language in the Divine Liturgy, whereas the Eastern Church according to its particular law (*juxta jus privatum*) uses more.[^334]
That an ecclesiastical language properly established is not permitted to be altered or uprooted, and that in the Eastern Churches it must also be a dead language, is attested by Benedict XIV of blessed memory, who thus writes: “Constantem, firmamque esse disciplinam, ne Missae idioma mutetur, et si mutet lingua vernacula; sed eo sermone Missa celebretur, quo celebrata est ab initio, etiam si illa lingua exoleverit apud vulgus, ejusque peritiam viri docti dumtaxat habeant.”[^335]
More especially doth Benedict XIV of blessed memory forbid that in the Eastern Churches an already consecrated and dead language should be cast out and uprooted on the ground that the people no longer understand it because they now speak another living language; for thus he writes: “Sed non in eo controversiae cardo vertitur. Illud quaeritur, an si, quae vernacula fuit, vulgari alio sermone in populum invecto doctorum dumtaxat virorum facta fuerit lingua, divina Officia et Missa in novum vulgi sermonem convertenda sint. Id vero est, a quo semper abhorruit et abhorret Catholica Ecclesia.”[^336]

§. 221.

Brief Explanation Concerning the Paschalion.[^337]

According to the statute of the Ecumenical Council of Nicaea (held in the year 325), the Feast of Pascha may occur on the days from March 22 unto April 25; for this Council decided “to celebrate the Pascha of Christ on the first Sunday following the full moon which occurs either on the very day of the vernal equinox (March 21), or the first after it.” — If this full moon should occur on the very day of the vern-

=== LEAF p310 ===
[Book Page 286]

(p. 286)

al equinox (March 21) on a Saturday, then the Pascha of Christ may be celebrated on the following Sunday (March 22), for the Jews will celebrate their Passover on Saturday. — But if this full moon should occur on the very day of the vernal equinox on a Sunday, then, in order not to celebrate Pascha together with the Jews on that same Sunday, the Pascha of Christ is postponed unto the following Sunday. — The latest possible day of the Pascha of Christ is the 25th day of April, because the March full moon may occur before the equinox, which according to our Julian Menologion is March 20; on this account one must await the full moon after March 21 after the passage of 28 days; yet even then, if this 28th day should occur on a Sunday, then on account of the Jewish Passover the Christian Pascha is to be celebrated on the following Sunday after the passage of 7 days, and thus after the passage of 35 days, that is, the 25th of April.
These 35 paschal days — between March 22 and April 25 — are designated with the letters of the Cyrillic alphabet with the addition of the letter “Ѫ”[^338] and they are called the “paschal letters” or “boundary letters.” By means of these 35 letters the Paschalion is composed.[^339] And therefore if we wish to know when in any given year the Pascha of Christ and the movable seasons fall, we must first know what the “boundary key,” that is, the “paschal letter” is in that year; and knowing this letter, everything that we wish to know will be unlocked by this key, if only we look into the table. (See Table I.) For example: in the year 1891 the boundary key will be “ь”, therefore the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee will be February 10; Pascha: April 21; the Descent of the Holy Spirit: June 9.
Besides this table, there are also other tables in which without any labor we can find the boundary key, for in these it is already given. (See Table II.) For example: for the year 1891 the boundary key is “ь”, for the year 1893: “ж”.
The question arises: how can this boundary key be found?

---

=== LEAF p311 ===
[Book Page 287]

(p. 287)

then we take already the 14th day following after this month. For since this rule commands to celebrate Pascha after the vernal equinox, therefore the vernal full moon can be only between March 21 and April 18; and since the first Sunday after this full moon can be already on the following day, but can also be after 7 days, Pascha can occur at the earliest on March 22, and at the latest on April 25 (that is, between April 4 and May 8 according to the new calendar). From this it is evident that Pascha is celebrated within a span of 35 days, which days from March 22 unto April 25 inclusively are called the “paschal days.”

For calculating the day of Pascha and of all movable feasts and seasons, the so-called “Paschalion” serves, which was composed on the basis of the following three calculations:

**1.** Calculation according to the moon. It is known that the lunar (monthly) year (12 lunar months of 28 days each) hath only 354 days and about 9 hours, and is consequently 11 days shorter than the solar year. These 11 days are called the “Epact” (*Epactae*). Since this difference is multiplied annually by 11 days, after 19 years a difference of 209 days will result. These 209 days make exactly 7 lunar months (7 × 28 = 209 days, 19 hours), after which the lunar year coincides again with the solar year on one and the same day. Hence it is that every 19 years the phases of the moon fall upon the very same days of the solar year. This 19-year period is called the “Lunar Cycle.”[^340]

**2.** Calculation according to the sun. In the church order the 7 days of the week are designated by 7 letters (А. В. Г. Д. Є. Ѕ. З.), which letters are called *vrucilito*. Since a common year hath 365 days, that is, 52 weeks and 1 day, the days of the week advance by one day to the following day, and after 4 years, when a leap year hath 366 days, by 2 days. Hence it follows that the order of the days of the week can repeat itself only after 28 years (4 × 7 = 28). This 28-year period is called the “Solar Cycle.”[^341]

**3.** Calculation according to the Indiction. Since the period of the moon contains within itself 19 years, and the period of the sun 28 years, both these periods will coincide in one and the same order after 532 years (19 × 28 = 532). This 532-year period is called the “Indiction” or the “Great Cycle.”[^342]

=== LEAF p312 ===
[Book Page 288]

(p. 288)

Since the last 532-year period (Indiction) began in the year 1408, it will end in the year 1940 (1408 + 532 = 1940), and in the year 1941 a new 532-year period will begin.[^343]

Strictly speaking, the “Perpetual Paschalion”[^344] was composed on the basis of these three calculations. It presents a table which hath 28 rows (according to the number of the solar cycle) and 19 columns (according to the number of the lunar cycle); consequently, 532 cells are contained therein (28 × 19 = 532), in each of which is found one boundary letter. (See Table III.)

In order to find from this table the boundary letter for any given year, the following rule is used:

From that year for which we wish to find the boundary letter, we subtract 1408 (the beginning of the present Indiction). The remainder we divide by 19 (the lunar cycle). The quotient resulting from the division (*osztalék*) shows in which row (*sor*) from top to bottom it is necessary to seek the letter; while the remainder from the division (*maradék*) shows in which column (across) the sought-for boundary letter (*betü*) is found.[^345]

=== LEAF p313 ===
[Book Page 289]

(p. 289)

For example: We wish to find the boundary letter for the year 1872.

$$1872 - 1408 = 464$$
$$464 \div 19 = 24	ext{, remainder } 8$$

$$egin{array}{r@{\quad}l}
464 : 19 = 24 \
=84 & \
76 & \
\hline
=8 &
\end{array}$$

The number 24 signifies how many rows it is necessary to count off; of these there are 24, and in the 25th row, according to the remaining number 8 from the division, it is necessary in that row to take the 8th letter, and this is the paschal letter, that is, “ч”; that is, for the year 1872 the boundary letter is “ч”. But if in the division nothing remains, then in the row that hath been found the last letter is the paschal letter.

Now if we have found the “paschal letter,” then we shall find also the day of Pascha. — We have already said[^346] that the “boundary letters” signify numbers; here let us only recall that if the number of the paschal letter is 10, or less than 10, 21 is added, and the day of Pascha in March will be obtained; but if that number is greater than 10, 10 is subtracted from it, and the day of Pascha in April is obtained. — For example: for the year 1874 the paschal letter is “и” = 10; 10 + 21 = 31 is added; on this account Pascha of that year fell on March 31. For the year 1872 the paschal letter was “ч” = 26; from this number is subtracted: 26 − 10 = 16; Pascha was on April 16.

Notes:

**1.** The day of Pascha, that is, the Resurrection, is repeated throughout the Sundays. There are 52 ordinary Sundays. The festal Sundays are the following: a) Sunday of the Holy Forefathers; b) Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the Old Testament; c) Sunday of the Holy God-Bearers; d) Sunday before the Theophany; e) Sunday after the Theophany; f) Sunday before the Exaltation of the Precious Cross; g) Sunday after the Exaltation of the Precious Cross; h) Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the Six Councils; i) Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the 7th Council.

**2.** The Polyeleos from September 22 unto December 20 is chanted on Sundays for this reason: because this period is a “period of preparation” for the feast of the Nativity of Jesus Christ; likewise also on the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee, of the Prodigal Son, Meatfare, and Cheesefare it is chanted for this reason: because these Sundays are “preparatory” (= pre-festal) unto the Great Fast.

**3.** In the first centuries of Christianity the faithful on Sunday did not bend their knees, but prayed standing in token of joy and

=== LEAF p314 ===
[Book Page 290]

(p. 290)

in representation of the Resurrection of Christ from the tomb and the resurrection of the faithful unto a new life. An ancient church writer (Pseudo-Justin, *Quaest. et resp. quaest.* 115) writes thus: “The bending of the knees during the six days of the week represents and signifies our fall through sin; but that on Sunday and during the Pentecostarion season we do not bend the knees, that signifies the resurrection, which by the grace of Christ hath freed us from sins and from the death that was rooted in them.” Nevertheless, the class of the penitents was bound during prayer and during the above-designated time to bend the knees. (Conc. Carth. IV, c. 28.)

**Glory to Thee, Holy Trinity, Glory to Thee!**

Note: In this table the days are designated thus: И = Sunday, П = Monday, В = Tuesday, Р = Wednesday, Ч = Thursday, Т = Friday, С = Saturday. Wednesday and Friday are designated not by their initial letters, but by other letters, for the reason that by the letters “С” and “П” other days of the week are already designated.

=== LEAF p319 ===
[Book Page Note on Sunday Letters]

### Note on Dominical and Ferial Letters in the Menologion

Note: In our Prayer Books in the Menologion, for every single day these letters are found:

$$egin{array}{ccccccc}
	ext{А} & 	ext{В} & 	ext{Г} & 	ext{Д} & 	ext{Є} & 	ext{Ѕ} & 	ext{З} \
	ext{И} & 	ext{П} & 	ext{В} & 	ext{Р} & 	ext{Ч} & 	ext{Т} & 	ext{С}
\end{array}$$

Concerning these letters we appoint the following: In the first row stand the dominical letters, in the second row the ferial letters. The dominical letters everywhere, for every single day, are inscribed uniformly, while the ferial letters vary. These letters serve for this purpose: that if I know the dominical letter, I immediately say upon which day one or another feast will occur, if only I look at that ferial letter which stands beneath the dominical letter. For example, if the dominical letter is А, then the day of the Indiction will occur on Sunday; but if the dominical letter is З, then on Saturday, since those letters stood beneath the dominical letters.

# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: Julian Pelesh, *Pastoral Theology* [Богословіе Пастырское], Vienna, 1885, p. 278.

[^2]: In these cases, there is also discussion concerning “Liturgies of Intention” (*Liturgiyakh namireniya* [Votive Liturgies]) according to various occasions.

[^3]: The ecclesiastical day, according to the Eastern custom and the utterance of the God-seer Moses (Gen. 1:5), begins from the evening; thus daily divine service also has its beginning from Vespers.

[^4]: Pelesh, *Pastoral Theology* [Богословіе Пастырское], Vienna, 1885, p. 267.

[^5]: Cf. Nilles, *Calendarium utriusque Ecclesiae Orientalis et Occidentalis*, Oeniponte [Innsbruck], 1879, tom. I, p. 32.

[^6]: We call the Indiction the 532-year key cycle of the Paschalia, which upon its conclusion begins anew and proceeds in that very same order.

[^7]: *“Gregorium Palamam, non solum uti Sanctum coli, aut festo die celebrari, verum etiam eum in nostra Ecclesia nominari deinceps prohibet Sancta Synodus sub poenis contra Schismaticos praescriptis”* [“The Holy Synod forbids Gregory Palamas not only to be venerated as a Saint or celebrated on a feast day, but even to be mentioned henceforth in our Church under the penalties prescribed against Schismatics”]. Cf. Nilles, *op. cit.*, t. II, p. 416.

[^8]: Sundays are twofold: ordinary (*riadovi*) and festal. Ordinary Sundays follow one after another in order from the Sunday after the Descent of the Holy Spirit unto the Sunday “of Zacchaeus.” The first ordinary Sunday is the “Sunday of All Saints”; the last, the 32nd, is “of Zacchaeus.” Sunday is the first day of the week, and it is dedicated to the Resurrection of Jesus Christ; Monday to the Angels; Tuesday to the Forerunner; Wednesday and Friday to the Precious Cross; Thursday to the Apostles and St. Nicholas; Saturday to All Saints. The period comprising eight full weeks, or 8 Tones, is called a pillar (*stolp*). There are six pillars in a year, which always begin with Sunday and Tone 1. Each pillar indicates which Tone and which Matins Gospel will fall on which Sunday. In the Paschalia, moreover, under the boundary key, it is indicated when the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, etc., pillar begins. Festal Sundays, however, which have their own proper Epistles, Gospels, Prokimena, and Communion Hymns, are 7 in number, namely: (α) The “Sunday of the Holy Forefathers,” the second before the Nativity of Christ; (β) The “Sunday of the Holy Fathers,” that is, the first before the Nativity of Christ; (γ) The “Sunday of the Holy Ancestors of God,” the first after the Nativity of Christ; (δ) The Sunday before Theophany; (ε) The Sunday after Theophany; (ϛ) The Sunday before the Exaltation of the Precious Cross; (η) The Sunday after the Exaltation of the Precious Cross.

[^9]: If the 11th falls on a Sunday, the service of the holy Fathers is chanted on that same day; if it falls on a Monday, Tuesday, or Wednesday, on the preceding Sunday; but if on a Thursday, Friday, or Saturday, on the following Sunday.

[^10]: Between the Sunday of All Saints and the Sunday of Zacchaeus there are at times more and at times fewer than 32 Sundays, but the ordinary liturgical Gospels are constantly only 32 in number; whence it follows that at times the liturgical Gospels do not suffice, and at times they are left over. — If there are more Sundays than ordinary Gospels, then those Gospels are interrupted and are not taken on festal Sundays, but are taken only from the Sunday after Theophany; and if even so they do not suffice, they are then repeated after the festal Sundays. — But if there are fewer Sundays than ordinary Gospels, and Gospels are left over, then they must be read together on festal Sundays with the festal Gospels, and some are omitted altogether.

[^11]: Pelesh, *Pastoral Theology* [Богословіе Пастырское], Vienna, 1885, p. 630.

[^12]: That is, the Annunciation of the Most Holy Virgin.

[^13]: That is, the Meeting (*Sriteniye*).

[^14]: The Immaculate Conception of the Most Holy Virgin in the whole Catholic world is a decretal feast; so it ought to be also among us.

[^15]: See *Enchiridion Juris Eccl. Orient.*, M.-Varadini, 1862, p. 500.

[^16]: February 8.

[^17]: February 17.

[^18]: April 30.

[^19]: May 2.

[^20]: May 8.

[^21]: July 22.

[^22]: To these church feasts, by determination of His Holiness Leo XIII, Universal Pontiff, the feast of SS. Cyril and Methodius is also to be reckoned. For the Universal Pontiff in the Bull *Grande munus* (Sept. 30, 1880) thus commands: *“Itaque praecipimus, ut rato die 5-o mensis Julii, quem felicis recordationis Pius IX. constituit, in Calendarium Romanae atque Universalis Ecclesiae inseratur, agaturque quotannis festum SS. Cyrilli et Methodii cum ritus duplicis minoris Officio et Missa propria, quae S. Consilium legitimis ritibus cognoscendis approbavit”* [“Therefore We command that, confirmed on the 5th day of the month of July, which Pius IX of happy memory established, it be inserted into the Calendar of the Roman and Universal Church, and that each year the feast of SS. Cyril and Methodius be celebrated with the Office and proper Mass of a lesser double rite, which the Sacred Congregation for Recognizing Legitimate Rites hath approved”].

[^23]: The Immaculate Conception and the day of St. Demetrius are inscribed by the late Bishop Papp-Szilágyi among the decretal feasts.

[^24]: The church spoken of here was erected on Golgotha, and its renewal, that is, its consecration, occurred under Constantine the Great in the year 335.

[^25]: This day is a decretal feast.

[^26]: And this too is a decretal feast.

[^27]: Lesser feasts have no forefeast, with the exception of the feast of the Procession of the Precious Cross (August 1), which hath one day of forefeast.

[^28]: Saints, regarding their liturgical celebration, as we shall see later, are divided into 3 parts: into Great, Middle, and Lesser Saints. Great Saints have Great Vespers on 8 with an entrance, and Matins with a Polyeleos and Doxology. — Middle Saints have Vespers on 6 without an entrance, and Matins without a Polyeleos, but a canon on 6; a Great Doxology is present for some, but not for others. — Lesser Saints have Vespers on 3, and a canon at Matins on 4. — Great Saints belong to middle feasts; Middle Saints to lesser feasts; and the days of Lesser Saints are called non-festal days.

[^29]: Great Saturday, as the Eve of the Resurrection of Christ, is a fast. — Fasting is observed besides this on the Eve of the Nativity and on the Eve of Theophany, but if these Eves fall on a Saturday or a Sunday, the fast is transferred to the preceding Friday, since the forefeast of these feasts consists—as was said—of several days.

[^30]: See Nilles, *Calendarium Manuale Utriusque Ecclesiae*, Innsbruck [Oeniponte], 1881, Vol. II, p. 87.

[^31]: Likewise, when on a Wednesday or Friday there falls some great Feast of the Lord, or Marian Feast, or the feast of a Solemn Saint, on those Wednesdays and Fridays, although meat is not permitted, nevertheless the strictness of the fast is reduced to abstinence. — Dukhnovych, *Liturgical Catechism* [Литургическій Катихизъ], Lviv, 1855, p. 144. If, however, the Nativity of Christ or Theophany happens to fall on a Wednesday or a Friday: then there is no fast and the eating of meat is permitted; — but the eating of meat is not permitted if the Feast of the Dormition or of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul falls on a Wednesday and Friday, for then there is abstinence.

[^32]: See Papp-Szilágyi, *op. cit.*, p. 366.

[^33]: The Sunday Matins of the Sunday of Pascha is set forth here in its completeness.

[^34]: A Great Saint is also called "Solemn" (*Narochyty*).

[^35]: In the 1901 Slavonic text, an orphaned asterisk marker (***) appears after the word *Four-Ode Canon* (*Chetyrepesnets*), but the typesetter omitted the corresponding footnote text at the bottom of page 26.

[^36]: Ordinarily, Monday hath in the morning canon Odes 1, 8, and 9; Tuesday Odes 2, 8, and 9; Wednesday Odes 3, 8, and 9; Thursday Odes 4, 8, and 9; Friday Odes 5, 8, and 9; Saturday Odes 6, 7, 8, and 9; but Sunday a complete canon.

[^37]: The word *Menaia* [Μηναῖα] signifies either: (α) “Monthly services” (*officia*) prescribed for immovable feasts, so that the Menaion would be: *biblion periechon ta menaia* [βιβλίον περιέχον τὰ μηναῖα]; or (β) “12 books” (*libri 12*) according to the 12 months, and thus it is *Menaia ta dodeka biblia* [Μηναῖα τὰ δώδεка βιβλία].

[^38]: Among the Greeks there is found a book called *Anagnosis* [᾿Αναγνωσὶς] or *Anagnosmata* [᾿Αναγνώσματα], termed in Latin *Lectionarium*, containing within itself all readings taken from the Holy Scripture of the Old Testament. — It might be called a *Parameynik*. — See Tompa, *Institutiones de SS. Ritibus*, Bratislava [Posonii], 1785, Part I, p. 40.

[^39]: That is, the Zadostoiniki [Hymns in place of *It is truly meet*]. At the Sunday of Pascha are found also prayers for the blessing of the artos and various meats, and at Pentecost the kneeling prayers.

[^40]: The Typikon used at our theological seminary was published in Przemyśl in 1852 by Yakiv Doskovsky, co-worker of the Cathedral Church of Przemyśl, and corrected by Archpriest Gregory Shashkevych of the same church in 1870.

[^41]: Pelesz, *Pastoral Theology* [Бог. Паст.], Vienna, 1885, p. 440.

[^42]: See Papp-Szilágyi, *op. cit.*, p. 366.

[^43]: Many writers doubt this.

[^44]: Simon Wangnereck, S.J., *Pietas Mariana Graecorum*, Munich [Monachii], 1647, p. 25.

[^45]: Pelesz, *Pastoral Theology* [Бог. Пастырское], Vienna, 1885, p. 428.

[^46]: “Symphonia” probably originates from the Apostles themselves. — “Antiphonia” was established by St. Ignatius the God-Bearer. Cf. Joseph Bingham, *Origines Ecclesiasticae*, Book XIII, Vol. V, § 5. “Hypophonia” probably originates from St. Athanasius. Cf. Fessler, *Patrologia*, Innsbruck [Oeniponte], 1850, Vol. I, p. 482. Moreover, in the East St. Basil, and in the West St. Ambrose, were those Hierarchs who first established and propagated psalmody.

[^47]: At the Presanctified Gifts two paroemias are read: one from the Book of Genesis, and the other from the Book of Proverbs of Solomon (with the exception of when the Eve of a celebrated Saint should occur, for then his 3 paroemias are also read), — and they are so arranged that in the course of the Great Fast until the Friday of Palms both of these books are read through entirely at Vespers.

[^48]: If the stichology at Vespers at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) is taken on 6 stichera, the verses are taken beginning from the verse “If Thou shouldst mark iniquities”; if on 8 stichera, then from the verse “Out of the depths have I cried”; if on 10 stichera, then from the verse “Bring my soul out of prison”; — in a word, the counting begins from the last verse “For His mercy is confirmed” and proceeds backwards. — At the beginning, 2 verses are sung from the Psalm “Lord, I have cried,” while the other verses are read until that verse which is already combined with a sticheron. — In like manner it is counted also at the Praises [Lauds], only there sometimes between “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) and between the taken verses given [prescribed] verses are intercalated. Thus on Sunday, when it is taken on 8, at the beginning 2 verses are sung from Psalm 148, and the rest are read until the verse “This glory shall be to all His saints”; — thereafter follows the stichology on 8, namely: 6 verses are taken from the Psalm, and 2 given verses are printed in the Octoechos.

[^49]: On weekdays at Vespers at the aposticha the verses taken are: “Unto Thee have I lifted up mine eyes” and “Have mercy on us, O Lord, have mercy on us: for we are utterly filled with contempt” [text: *Naypache napolnytsia dusha nasha*]. See *Molitvoslov*, Budapest, 1878, p. 231. At Matins at the aposticha the verses are: “Look upon Thy servants” and “Let the brightness of the Lord our God be upon us.” See *Molitvoslov*, p. 147.

[^50]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 221.

[^51]: *Leitourgikon*, Lviv, 1866, p. 692.

[^52]: Among us only the first and last strophe, that is, the heirmos and the katavasia, are sung; the troparia are only read.

[^53]: It is necessary to know that in the Eastern Church during divine worship it is not permitted to sit, but it is necessary to stand with reverence. See Goar, *Euchologion Graecorum*, Venice, 1730, p. 37.

[^54]: The Angels are divided into 9 choirs; 3 choirs constitute one hierarchy.

[^55]: Just as in canons the initial troparia are called heirmoi, so in stichera they are called prosomoia [model melodies], for in the likeness of these the subsequent ones are sung.

[^56]: *“Omittitur autem canticum 2-um, quia non est hymnus ad Deum, sed compellatio Judaeorum, et comminatio et nequitiae eorum increpatio; malorumque, quae iis superventura sunt, praedictio”* [“The second canticle is omitted, however, because it is not a hymn to God, but an address to the Jews, and a threatening and rebuke of their wickedness, and a prediction of the evils that are about to come upon them”]. Goar, *Euchologion*, loc. cit., p. 352.

[^57]: Among us only the refrain of the 9th Ode is taken, while the heirmos for the most part is omitted also in dismissals.

[^58]: The 8 verses are these: (α) “Praise ye the name of the Lord”; (β) “Ye that stand in the temple”; (γ) “O Lord, Thy name endures forever”; (δ) “Blessed be the Lord out of Zion”; (ε) “O give thanks unto the Lord”; (ϛ) “To Him that established the earth”; (η) “With a strong hand”; (ϑ) “O give thanks unto the God of heaven.” And there is appended unto each verse: “Alleluia.” It is sung 9 times in token of the chanting of the nine ranks of Angels.

[^59]: In Galicia even now, when on great feasts at Matins they chant the Polyeleos: a tetrapod [analogion] is placed in the midst of the church, and upon it is set the festal icon of the Lord, or of the Mother of God, or of the Saint of the day, and all candles are lighted and distributed unto the people. The Priest then vests in phelonion, opens the holy doors, and comes out before the tetrapod [analogion], where he censes the icon of the feast placed upon the tetrapod [analogion], and thereafter the altar and the people. Upon the conclusion of the Polyeleos, standing before the tetrapod [analogion], he begins the magnification there, singing the first verse, and the people continue. Upon the completion of the magnification, the Priest enters into the altar, and the people continue Matins. Pelesz, *Pastoral Theology* [Богослов. Пастырское], p. 617; *Typikon*, p. 19.

[^60]: The troparia after the Blameless are sung also on Great Saturday and on Lazarus Saturday at Matins.

[^61]: These psalms constitute the 18th Kathisma. (α) The Graduals of Tone 1: the first antiphon is taken from verses 1 and 4 of Psalm 119; the second antiphon from verses 1 and 5 of Psalm 120; and the third antiphon from verses 1 and 5 of Psalm 121. (β) The Graduals of Tone 2: the first antiphon is from verses 1 and 3 of Psalm 122; the second antiphon from verses 1 and 6 of Psalm 123; and the third antiphon from verses 1 and 3 of Psalm 124. (γ) The Graduals of Tone 3: the first antiphon is from verses 1 and 5 of Psalm 125; the second antiphon from verses 1 and 3 of Psalm 126; and the third antiphon from verses 1 and 4 of Psalm 127. (δ) The Graduals of Tone 4: the first antiphon is from verses 1 and 5 of Psalm 128; the second antiphon from verses 1 and 4 of Psalm 129; and the third antiphon from verses 1 and 2 of Psalm 130. (ε) The Graduals of Tone 5: derive their thoughts anew in all three antiphons from those same psalms as the graduals of Tone 1. (ϛ) The Graduals of Tone 6: derive their thoughts in the same manner as the graduals of Tone 2. (η) The Graduals of Tone 7: in the same manner as the graduals of Tone 3. (ι) The Graduals of Tone 8 have 4 antiphons: the first antiphon is taken from verses 1 and 5 of Psalm 128; the second antiphon from verses 1 and 4 of Psalm 129; the third antiphon from verses 1 and 2 of Psalm 130; and the fourth antiphon from Psalm 132.

[^62]: Of Theotokia-troparia the most renowned are: (α) The dismissal Sunday Theotokia in the 8 tones sung after the Sunday troparia at Vespers and at Matins. (β) The dismissal Theotokia for weekdays in the 8 tones sung at Vespers after the Troparia, and at Matins at “God is the Lord” (*Богъ Господь*) and at the end.

[^63]: Among the stichera sung at “Lord, I have cried,” the sticheron at “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) is called the “Doxastikon” [Slavonic: *slavnyk*]; for it hath as its subject the magnification of the occurring feast or Saint.

[^64]: Of great bows the most renowned are those 15 bows which it is necessary to use at the daily Midnight Office in the Great Fast. Those bows formerly (according to the Typikon of Irene Augusta [*Juxta Typikon Irene Augustae*]) they performed thus: The first 3 bows the sinful man made in such wise that when standing, he outstretched his arms and said three times these words: “O God, be merciful unto me a sinner” (*Бо́же, ми́лостивъ бꙋ́ди мнѣ̀ грѣ́шномꙋ*); thereafter bending the knees and head, he said again three times: “Countless times have I sinned, O Lord, forgive me!” — The other 12 he performed likewise in this manner, but these prayers, both when he stood and when he knelt, he said only once. Cf. Nilles, *Kalendarium Manuale Utriusque Ecclesiae*, Innsbruck [Oeniponte], 1879, Vol. I, p. 119.

[^65]: On those feasts on which a Litiya is celebrated, Compline and the Midnight Office are omitted.

[^66]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 151.

[^67]: Cf. Goar, *Euchologion Graecorum*, loc. cit., p. 25.

[^68]: Certain of the Eastern heretics sing it thus: “Sanctus Deus, Sanctus fortis, Sanctus immortalis, qui crucifixus est pro nobis” [“Holy God, Holy Mighty, Holy Immortal, Who wast crucified for us”].

[^69]: “Ejus cantandi primam institutionem ad Justini tempora revocat Cedrenus scribens: ‘Eo imperante ut Cherubicus hymnus decantaretur, sancitum est’” [“Cedrenus refers the first institution of singing it to the time of Justin, writing: ‘In his reign it was decreed that the Cherubic Hymn should be chanted’”]. Goar, loc. cit., p. 113.

[^70]: *Benedictus panis sacrae Communionis vicarius* [“Blessed bread as vicar of Holy Communion”].

[^71]: See Goar, loc. cit., p. 132.

[^72]: The All-Night Vigil is a service which consists of Great Vespers with Litiya and of Matins with the First Hour.

[^73]: The Inter-Hours (in Greek: *mesorion* [μεσώριον]) are 4 services which in monasteries are celebrated between the Hours, and constitute as it were a continuation of the Hours. Their composition is such as that of the Hours.

[^74]: The Typika is called also the Service of the Typika (*akolouthia ton typikon* [ἀκολουθία τῶν τυπικῶν] == *officium typicorum*), for this reason, namely, that it represents the Divine Liturgy and at times is celebrated instead of the Divine Liturgy.

[^75]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 220.

[^76]: Sometimes it happens that both the Great Prokimenon is sung and paremias are read.

[^77]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 226.

[^78]: For this reason only with a single sign of the cross are the loaves, wheat, wine, and oil blessed: because if it had been necessary to use signs of the cross multiple times, then this prayer would sound thus: “Bless these loaves, bless this wheat, bless this wine, bless this oil.”

[^79]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 328.

[^80]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 336.

[^81]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 355.

[^82]: In the dismissal of these days that Saint also whose memory we accomplish is commemorated.

[^83]: In the Great Fast on Saturdays (that is, in the evening) ordinary Vespers is celebrated.

[^84]: On Cheesefare Sunday, and on the 2nd and 4th Sundays of the Fast, “Turn not away Thy face” (*Не отврати лица*) is taken; on the 1st, 3rd, and 5th Sundays of the Fast: “Thou hast given an inheritance.” See *Molitvoslov*, p. 899.

[^85]: These penitential stichera are printed at the end of the Lenten Triodion as an appendix.

[^86]: Only 2 stichera are given in the book, but the first is repeated.

[^87]: In the Fast at the aposticha, “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) is omitted for minor Saints.

[^88]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 41.

[^89]: In these Vespers we do not say the litany “Let us all say.”

[^90]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 183.

[^91]: On Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday there is no kathisma.

[^92]: This is taken from the Typika.

[^93]: It is also celebrated on Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday of Passion Week.

[^94]: But since the penitential stichera are printed at the end of the Triodion, we may say: 6 stichera from the Triodion and 4 of the Saint.

[^95]: For example, on the Paramony of the Nativity and on the Paramony of Theophany, when between the paremias there are also troparia with verses.

[^96]: On the Paramony of the Nativity and on the Paramony of Theophany, the Royal Hours conclude before Vespers.

[^97]: Alleluia is not taken.

[^98]: “Heavenly King” (*Царю̀ небе́сный*) is not said until the Descent of the Holy Spirit.

[^99]: From Thomas Sunday, services are also sung for Saints throughout the entire Pentecostarion.

[^100]: On Great Friday at Compline we sing the canon: “Traversing the deep on foot.”

[^102]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 48: “Let us pray for our most holy Hierarch...” The choir answers: “Lord, have mercy,” etc.
