[^108]: *Typikon*, p. 212.

[^109]: *Molitvoslov*, p. 288.

[^110]: It must be noted that on Cheesefare Tuesday and Thursday “O Lord of hosts” is not taken, but instead the troparion of the day and of the temple and “O God of our Fathers” are taken, as at Small Compline.

[^111]: In the Typikon we read that on Fridays of the Holy Forty Days we usually sing Small Compline. Great Compline is taken extraordinarily. *Typikon*, p. 216.

[^112]: Likewise “O Lord of hosts” is not taken: (α) also on the Forefeast of the Annunciation, if it should occur on Friday of the 3rd or 4th week, but the kontakion of the Forefeast is taken; (β) furthermore, if on weekdays of the Holy Great Fast there falls the Finding of the Head or the 40 Martyrs, then instead of “O Lord of hosts” the kontakion of the Saint or Saints is taken; (γ) and on Monday and Tuesday of Passion Week the kontakion of the Triodion and the troparion “O Lord of hosts” are taken, but without verses.

[^113]: Some priests are accustomed at the conclusion of the Litiya, after “Blessed be the name of the Lord,” to bless the people secretly, while others omit this, which seems unto us to be more suitable.

[^114]: *Cfr.* [*cf.*] Pelesh, *Pastoral Theology*, p. 605; *Typikon*, p. 46.

[^115]: Dr. Julian Pelesh states that after “Our Father” (*Отче нашъ*) the Hypakoe of the tone is sung. *Pastoral Theology*, p. 606.

[^116]: There are Typika wherein, if the Midnight Office is said in private, instead of the Troparia: “Have mercy on us, O Lord,” and instead of the litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” it is prescribed: “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (12 times), and immediately the Dismissal. See Doskovsky, *Typikon*, p. 46.

[^117]: If, however, the Midnight Office was celebrated in church, then after “Blessed is our God” (*Благословенъ Богъ нашъ*) there immediately follows: “Come, let us worship” (*Прїиди́те поклони́мся*) (thrice).

[^118]: If the Saint hath stichera at the Praises (*Ainoi*), then two verses of Psalm 148 are sung in the usual manner: “Let every breath praise the Lord; praise the Lord from the heavens,” and the 2nd verse; the remaining verses are read, and the stichera are sung unto the final verses of Psalm 150.

[^119]: On Saturday the canon of the Saint takes precedence, and after it that of the temple of the Saint, and then in the Octoechos the martyr canon. (The canon for the departed is sung on Friday at Compline.) At the Praises on Saturday we do not sing the stichera in the Octoechos, but we sing them at the Aposticha: 3 martyr stichera with the usual refrains. The Saturday aposticha stichera are omitted, and they are sung only on All Souls Saturdays with their refrains for the departed. (This exchange of stichera at the Aposticha takes place also on Friday evening at Vespers.) *Typikon*, p. 53.

[^120]: If there be the Small Doxology, then there is the small Dismissal: “Wisdom”; “More honorable,” etc.

[^121]: If there be the Great Doxology, then there is the great Dismissal: “Wisdom”; “Bless.”

[^122]: On Lazarus Saturday at Matins (which is Matins with the Great Doxology) the troparia are sung: “The angelic host”; small litany, and the Priest before the royal doors saith: “Having seen the Resurrection of Christ” (*Воскресеніе Христово*) once, and thus Psalm 50 is read. After the canon, small litany and “Holy is the Lord” (thrice).

[^123]: Before the Kathisma and after the Kathisma all is said in the manner stated above.

[^124]: If it be a feast of the Lord, at “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) the sticheron of the feast (“All things today are filled with joy,” etc.); at “Now and,” the same; and unto the refrain “Have mercy on me, O God,” etc., the sticheron of the feast. — If, however, it be a feast of the Mother of God, at “Glory” (*Сла́ва*): “Through the prayers of the Theotokos,” etc.; at “Now and,” the same; and unto the refrain “Have mercy on me, O God,” the sticheron of the feast. — If, however, it be the feast of the holy Apostles Peter and Paul, at “Glory” (*Сла́ва*): “Through the prayers of the holy Apostles Peter and Paul, O Merciful One, cleanse,” etc.; at “Now and”: “Through the prayers of the Theotokos” (*Моли́твами Богоро́дицы...*); and unto the refrain “Have mercy on me, O God,” the sticheron to the holy Apostles.

[^125]: See *Molitvoslov*, pp. 308–311.

[^126]: Among us the deacon censes the holy sanctuary and the entire church.

[^127]: When the Polyeleos is sung on a Sunday, of this mention will be made in the “Appendix” at the end of this section.

[^128]: In the Great Fast on Sunday at “Glory” (*Сла́ва*): “Open unto us the doors of repentance”; at “Now and”: “Guide me in the pathways of salvation”; and unto the refrain “Have mercy on me, O God” (*Поми́луй мя́, Бо́же*): “The multitude of my deeds.”

[^129]: Unto this is added also the canon of the Saint from the Menaion.

[^130]: The deacon censes, but among us the Priest doth not cense.

[^131]: Among us the Priest proclaims aloud only once the litany for the departed before the royal doors, and that: after the stases; but the second time he saith it only quietly.

[^132]: Among us the Panakhyda is sung only after the Liturgy.

[^133]: If, however, Matins is combined with the Midnight Office, then after “Blessed is our God” (*Благословенъ Богъ нашъ*) there immediately follows the Trisagion. (“O Heavenly King” (*Царю̀ небе́сный*) is omitted.)

[^134]: They are: (α) On Monday: “By the intercession of Thy Bodiless Powers, have mercy on us”; (β) On Tuesday: “Through the prayers of the Forerunner, have mercy on us”; (γ) On Wednesday and Friday: “By the power of Thy Cross preserve us”; (δ) On Thursday: “Through the prayers of the holy Apostles and of Hierarch Nicholas, have mercy on us.” — (The 2nd and 3rd Triadica conclude alike.)

[^135]: On Monday from the 9 Canticles of Moses the 1st, 8th, and 9th canticles are taken, and we recite them before the 1st, 8th, and 9th odes of the canon. On Tuesday the 2nd, 8th, and 9th canticle of Moses, and it is recited before the 2nd, 8th, and 9th odes of the canon. On Wednesday the 3rd, 8th, and 9th canticle of Moses, which is recited before the 3rd, 8th, and 9th odes of the canon. On Thursday the 4th, 8th, and 9th canticle of Moses is taken, and it is recited before the 4th, 8th, and 9th odes of the canon. On Friday the 6th, 8th, and 9th canticle of Moses is taken, and it is recited before the 5th, 8th, and 9th odes of the canon. Finally on Saturday the 6th, 7th, 8th, and 9th canticle of Moses is taken, and it is recited before the 6th, 7th, 8th, and 9th odes of the canon.

[^136]: If there occur on weekdays 2 Saints (excepting Saturdays and Sundays): then from the Octoechos the penitential canon is sung with the Heirmos on 6, and to the Saints both canons on 8; — but if a Triodion canon occurs, the canon in the Octoechos is omitted, and both canons to the Saints are sung with the Heirmos on 6, and from the Triodion on 8. The Katavasia will be the Heirmos of the Triodion canon.

[^137]: If there occur 2 Saints on weekdays (excepting Saturdays and Sundays), we sing to the one 4 stichera at “Lord, I have cried” (*Господи воззвахъ*) at Vespers, and to the other at the Praises (*Ainoi*) on 4, repeating the 1st sticheron.

[^138]: If there be a Saint, then at the aposticha “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) to the Saint.

[^139]: The Triodion canon at the 4th, 8th, and 9th odes is only read with the refrains: “Holy Apostles, pray unto God for us” — and that without bows; neither do we sing the heirmoi of the Triodion canon, nor do we say “Glory” (*Сла́ва*) and “Now and.”

[^140]: This Matins is celebrated among us on Great Thursday evening at the 6th hour.

[^141]: Refrains: 1st “We magnify Thee, O Christ Giver of Life,” etc.; 2nd “It is meet”; and 3rd “Every generation [offers] a hymn,” etc.

[^142]: So it is in the ancient Typika. See Doskovsky, p. 241.

[^143]: See *Molitvoslov*, p. 478.

[^144]: If the Third and Sixth Hours are read together without the First Hour, they have the usual beginning.

[^145]: The Ninth Hour is usually celebrated before Vespers, and then begins from: “Blessed is our God,” “O Heavenly King.”

[^146]: When it is proper to say the verse: “O God, be bountiful unto us” and when “Through the prayers of the holy Fathers”—let us note this: At the Midnight Office after the prayer: “Thou Who at all times” (*Иже на всякое время*): “O God, be bountiful unto us.” And at the First Hour likewise: “O God, be bountiful unto us.” At the Third and Sixth Hours: “Through the prayers of the holy Fathers”; likewise also at the Ninth Hour, if the Hours are sung together. But if the Ninth Hour is not sung together with the Third and Sixth, but before Vespers, then: “O God, be bountiful unto us.” At Great Compline after the 1st “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (40 times): “Through the prayers of the holy Fathers”; after the 2nd “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (40 times): “Lord Jesus Christ our God, have mercy on us.” After the 3rd “Lord, have mercy” (*Господи помилуй*) (40 times): “O God, be bountiful unto us.”

[^147]: If, however, the Typika is combined with the Ninth Hour, then after the prayer of the Ninth Hour immediately the psalm: “Bless the Lord, O my soul.”

[^148]: If, however, it is celebrated without Matins, then the Priest in the epitrachelion before the royal doors saith: “Blessed is our God.” — “O Heavenly King,” etc.

[^149]: It is proper to know that on all Mondays of the Holy Forty Days we do not say a kathisma at the 1st Hour. Likewise also on Friday the 1st and 9th Hours are without a kathisma.

[^150]: On Monday, Wednesday, and Friday of the Week of the Veneration of the Cross, instead of “Order my steps,” there is sung “To Thy Cross” with bows. This takes place in such wise that the Priest vests in the full priestly vestment, opens the royal doors, and having come forth unto the Tetrapod, begins “To Thy Cross”; after the singing he shuts the royal doors.

[^151]: It is needful to note that on Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday, at the 1st and 9th Hours we do not say a kathisma. On these 3 days at the Hours there are readings from all four Evangelists—and that at the 1st Hour the Gospel is not read, but at the 3rd, 6th, and 9th Hours it is read.—See *Typikon*, p. 231. (Among us, however, the Gospel of the 9th Hour is taken at the Presanctified Liturgy.)

[^152]: There are Typika in which it is required to say this prayer not at the Typika [*Obidnytsia*], but in its own place, that is, at the 9th Hour.

[^153]: Instead of the 3rd Kathisma, from the 1st Sunday after the Leave-taking of the Feast of the Exaltation of the Precious Cross until December 20, and from January 14 until “Cheesefare Sunday,” the Polyeleos is taken: “Praise ye the name of the Lord.”

[^154]: Among us the deacon comes forth before the royal doors and there proclaims: “Holy is the Lord our God.”

[^155]: The Pochaiv Typikon [*Ustav*] commands to sing at 6, but all the ancient ones at 4.

[^156]: That is: The Nativity of the Theotokos, the Entry into the Temple, the Conception, the Dormition, the Deposition of the Robe and of the Precious Cincture [Sash], and the Encounter [Meeting of the Lord] before the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee.

[^157]: If a Feast of the Lord occurs on a Monday, we sing the 1st antiphon from the 1st Kathisma; on other days the kathisma is not sung.

[^158]: It is needful to note that on all great Feasts of the Lord in the evening at Vespers there is an entrance and the great prokimenon with its verses.

[^159]: In this case among us the small Saint, except at Compline, is entirely omitted.

[^160]: It may happen that on Sunday and within a feast there fall more Saints having offices; then only one of them is taken; all the others are omitted and commemorated only in the Dismissals.

[^161]: That is, if it be Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, or Friday.

[^162]: On Wednesday and Friday, the Stavrotheotokion.

[^163]: On Tuesday and on Thursday evening at Compline, if it be a temple of Christ, then the troparion of the temple is omitted, because the troparion of the day is said: “Save, O Lord,” etc. On Wednesday evening at Compline, however, we say the troparion of the day: “Holy Apostles,” and the troparion of Saint Nicholas: “Rule of faith,” etc.

[^164]: On the Praises the singer first singeth: “Let everything that hath breath praise the Lord; praise the Lord from the heavens, praise Him in the highest; to Thee is due praise, O God.” And the 2nd verse of Psalm 148 [Ps. 148:1–2] with this ending; the remaining verses are read.

[^165]: On Tuesday and Thursday evening, when there is no Polyeleos to the Saint, instead of Theotokia, Stavrotheotokia are sung.

[^166]: If a Feast of the Lord fall on Saturday: then on Friday evening the prokimenon of the day is omitted and the Great Prokimenon is sung with its verses; on Saturday evening, that is, on the very feast, we sing the prokimenon “The Lord reigns” (*Госпо́дь воцари́ся*) [Ps. 92:1]. But if a Feast of the Lord fall on Sunday, on Saturday evening we sing the prokimenon: “The Lord reigns” (*Госпо́дь воцари́ся*) [Ps. 92:1], and on Sunday evening we sing the Great Prokimenon with its verses.

[^167]: Some say also this, that in this case, although the small Saint is omitted, nevertheless it is so that at the end of Vespers, as also at the beginning and at the end of Matins, the troparion of the small Saint is also taken. That is, thus: Troparion of the feast and troparion of the 1st Saint; “Glory” (*Сла́ва*): Troparion of the small Saint; “Now and”: of the feast. At the Hours, however, the troparion and kontakion of the middle Saint are taken at the 3rd, and of the small Saint at the 9th Hour.

[^168]: On the Praises there are stichera and they are called “for the Departed” [*Pokoyni*], but they are not sung except on the 2nd, 3rd, and 4th Saturday of the Fast, or when “Alleluia” is sung.

[^169]: Some say also this, that on Saturday a Great Dismissal ought to be taken for the sake of the apodosis of the tone, and that after the troparion at “God is the Lord,” at the end of Matins the great Resurrection Theotokion is taken.

[^170]: If a feast or a Polyeleos Saint occur on Sunday, then only two kathismata are taken, and that: the 2nd and the 3rd; instead of the 17th, the Polyeleos is taken.

[^171]: If a festal celebration occur on Sunday, then we sing: “Blessed is the man” (*Блаженъ мужъ*) [Ps. 1], the 1st Antiphon.

[^172]: Whenever, however, a festal celebration occur on these days in which 3 kathismata are said at Matins: then we say 2 kathismata at Matins, and the 3rd daily kathisma at Vespers.

[^173]: On Meatfare and Cheesefare Sunday we chant the stichology of two kathismata at Matins, and the 3rd daily kathisma at Vespers.

[^174]: On Thomas Sunday at Matins: Kathismata II and III, and the Polyeleos.

[^175]: It is to be noted that among us two Gospels are never read at Matins.

[^176]: See the Tables at the end of this booklet.

[^177]: With the 5th Sunday of the Great Fast the cycle of tones and Matins Gospels ends, even unto the Sunday of All Saints.

[^178]: “Holy is the Lord” is taken 3 times also on Lazarus Saturday and on Great Saturday at Matins.

[^179]: Throughout the entire Forty Days there is no Epistle and Gospel, except on Saturdays and Sundays, but if anyone wishes to celebrate the Liturgy, he may according to his need, either for the departed, or to a Saint in general, or to the Cross, or to the Theotokos, and so forth. See *Sluzhebnik*, Lviv, 1866, p. 274.

[^180]: But if the Feast of the Annunciation occur on Holy and Great Friday, then on this day also the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom is celebrated. See Yakiv Doskovskyi’s *Typikon*, Peremyshl, 1852, p. 184.

[^181]: In ancient *Sluzhebnyky* there is a rubric note that the priest entering into the holy Sanctuary bows unto both kliroi, which is meant to signify that he then asks forgiveness from all the faithful for all his offenses. But in the *Sluzhebnik* published in Rome by order of Benedict XIV, there is no mention of this. — See Pelesz, *Pastoral Theology*, Vienna, 1885, p. 472.

[^182]: That one may not err as to which side is right and which is left, according to ecclesiastical custom it is necessary to picture Christ seated upon the throne with His face turned toward the people.

[^183]: According to ancient custom, 5 prosphoras were used for each Liturgy, that is to say: from the first prosphora they cut out the “Lamb,” from the second the particle in honor of the Theotokos, from the third the particle in honor of the Saints, from the fourth in remembrance of the living, and from the fifth in remembrance of the departed. Now only one prosphora is used, from which are cut out the “Lamb” and the other particles, and the remainder is distributed as “Antidoron.”

[^184]: The priest must carefully distinguish here between “intention” and “commemoration” (*intentio et commemoratio*); for these are distinct matters.

[^185]: Clement XIV preconized the Vicariate of Mukachevo into the Eparchy of Mukachevo.

[^186]: In the Proskomedia there is symbolically represented the quiet and secret Incarnation and Nativity of Jesus Christ in this world for all men, Who is placed here upon the Diskos in the form of the Lamb; this Diskos representeth the manger in which the Child Jesus was laid, and the Diskos resteth upon the Table of Preparation, which here figureth the cave of Bethlehem where Christ was born. After His Nativity the star appeared unto the Magi in the East, and showing them the way, stood over the cave in which Jesus was born; therefore the priest taketh the Asterisk and setteth it over the Diskos, saying: “And the star came and stood over where the young Child was” (Matt. 2:9), and covereth the Diskos and Chalice with veils, figuring those swaddling cloths with which Jesus Christ was wrapped. — Finally, the priest boweth down before the Child and with the Magi offereth gifts unto Him: for gold, the Chalice; for myrrh, the censer; and for frankincense, the incense with which he censeth the prepared gifts.

[^187]: Vespers begins with “Blessed is our God,” etc., Matins with “Glory to the holy, consubstantial, and life-creating Trinity,” etc., and the Liturgy with “Blessed is the kingdom of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit,” etc.; and that because Vespers and Matins symbolically figure the Old Testament, at the beginning of which the mystery of the Holy Trinity was not revealed, while toward the end of this Old Testament the people already knew something concerning It, but not clearly; clearly this mystery is revealed only in the New Testament, which the Liturgy figures.

[^188]: Amen is a Hebrew word taken from the Old Testament, and signifies only: “so be it,” or “let it be,” or “in truth it is so.” Saint Jerome calls it the seal of prayers.

[^189]: This entrance signifieth the manifestation of Jesus Christ at the Jordan River; the deacon or server carrying a lighted candle representeth Saint John the Baptist going before, and the priest clothed in holy vestments representeth Jesus Christ clothed in the flesh and found in fashion as a man.

[^190]: Stand upright [*Prosti*] = *orthoi* [ὄρθοι] = *recti* = with a pure conscience.

[^191]: Let us attend [*Vonmim*] = let us be attentive, that is, with a quiet and silent mind let us give heed.

[^192]: If the Typikon prescribes more than one Epistle to be read, then only the title of the first Epistle is proclaimed at the beginning; the second is merely appended thereto with the words: “Brethren,” or “In those days,” or “Child Timothy,” etc.

[^193]: Alleluia is a Hebrew word, signifying only: “God is coming, praise Him” or “Praise the Lord.”

[^194]: See the Services for Diverse Intentions in the *Sluzhebnik* [*Liturgikon*].

[^195]: After the “Litany of the Catechumens,” “Wisdom” is pronounced twice: when it is pronounced the first time, all the faithful are thereby aroused to show themselves worthy, for from this time begins the accomplishment of the Mystery; but when “Wisdom” is pronounced the second time, those who desire to receive communion of the Holy Mysteries are thereby aroused unto a pure conscience.

[^196]: The Cherubic Hymn was composed probably by Saint Justin Martyr.

[^197]: *Dorinosiat* [Дориносятъ] = that is, they bear aloft upon spears. This expression is taken from the times of the Roman emperors, and hath this meaning: when among the Romans a new emperor was chosen, they set him upon a shield, and the soldiers raised him aloft on that shield upon spears and bore him about among all the legions, that all might recognize him and render him homage: thus do the Angels bear Christ upon shields, whom here the priest with the deacon represent, carrying the Holy Gifts among the people unto the holy Altar-Table.

[^198]: It is customary that the faithful kneel at the Great Entrance. The Synod of Zamość forbids this, saying: “The Holy Synod forbids kneeling or bowing the head when the bread of oblation is transferred from the Table of Preparation unto the Altar-Table at the time of the Great Entrance, and commands pastors to instruct the people concerning this, that they be not exposed to the danger of idolatry” (Sess. III, tit. III, § 4, p. 90). See Pelesz, *Pastoral Theology*, p. 493.

[^199]: The Great Entrance symbolically signifies the entry of Jesus Christ from Bethany into Jerusalem unto His passion and death; but since Jesus Christ before His death foretold both His passion and His resurrection: therefore also at His entrance His taking down from the cross and His laying in the tomb are figuratively depicted.

[^200]: If there are several concelebrants at the Divine Liturgy, then after manifesting love toward God they manifest also their mutual love, kissing one another upon the shoulder, whereat the superior saith: “Christ is in our midst!” and the concelebrants answer: “He is and shall be!” See Pelesz, *Pastoral Theology*, p. 494.

[^201]: The deacon’s exclamation: “The doors! The doors!” etc. had in ancient times this meaning, that by these words the deacon summoned the ostiarii [door-keepers] standing at the doors unto vigilance, lest any unbelievers should enter into the church at that time when the faithful confess their faith, and when the holy sacrifice is about to be accomplished.

[^202]: Some say that the words: “singing, shouting, crying aloud, and saying” signify the four quarters of the earth, that is, east, west, south, and north, in which the Word of God is proclaimed; but Saint Germanus saith that by these expressions are understood those living creatures that were present unto the four Evangelists, for he saith: “The singing one is the eagle, the shouting one the ox, the crying one the lion, and the saying one the man” (See the Prophecy of Ezekiel 1:10).

[^203]: During the singing of “Holy, Holy, Holy,” as also during the words that consecrate and transubstantiate the bread into the Body and the wine into the Blood of Jesus Christ, and at the exclamation “Thine own of Thine own,” the church bell is rung, and the people fall upon their knees and render homage unto Jesus Christ, Who hath come to give Himself unto us as food under the appearances of bread and wine.

[^204]: *Haec oratio vocatur „ἐπίκλησις“ seu „invocatio Spiritus Sancti“, in qua oratione Sacerdos implorat Spiritum Sanctum, non simpliciter, ut faciat panem corpus, et quod est in calice sanguinem, sed ut fiat nobis ad fructum. Mutata enim substantia panis et vini in Corpus et Sanguinem Christi per verba consecratoria et confecto vero corpore Christi, necesse est, ut etiam nos, quos accidentia panis et vini repraesentant, per Spiritum Sanctum in mysticum corpus Christi coadunemur juxta dicta S. Pauli: „Unum corpus multi sumus, omnes, qui de uno pane participamus“* (I. Corinth. X. 17) [“This prayer is termed the ‘epiklesis’ or ‘invocation of the Holy Spirit,’ in which prayer the priest imploreth the Holy Spirit, not simply that He make the bread the Body and that which is in the chalice the Blood, but that it may be for us unto fruit. For the substance of the bread and wine having been changed into the Body and Blood of Christ through the consecratory words, and the true Body of Christ having been confected, it is necessary that we also, whom the accidents of bread and wine represent, should through the Holy Spirit be united together into the mystical Body of Christ according to the words of St. Paul: ‘For we, being many, are one bread and one body, all of us who partake of that one bread’” (1 Cor. 10:17)].

[^205]: Here the priest first of all commemorates those (living or departed) unto whom he desires to apply the fruits of the offered sacrifice (*fructus sacrificii*), and only thereafter the others whom he wishes. In ancient times the deacon read aloud the commemorations for the living and departed from the book called the “Diptychs” (*liber diptychorum*). Diptychs were two tablets folded together, in which on one side the names of the living were written, and on the other side the names of the departed, which were necessary to be commemorated at the sacred action.

[^206]: The core of the Divine Liturgy is termed the *canon Missae* or *anaphora* (ἀναφορά = *actio, secretum*).

[^207]: Saint Cyril of Jerusalem thus interprets these words: “The holy things are the gifts set forth, having received the descent of the Holy Spirit; and ye are holy, having been accounted worthy of the Holy Spirit. Holy things therefore are proper for the holy.”

[^208]: “The fullness of the Holy Spirit” (*И҆сполне́нїе Дꙋ́ха свята́гѡ*) represents the union of the soul and Body of Christ, or His most glorious Resurrection.

[^209]: If, however, several priests concelebrate, then in the first place one after another they approach the holy Altar-Table in an orderly manner, where the presiding priest distributes unto them particles of the Holy Body, saying: “Christ is in our midst!” and after this all together say the prayer: “I believe, O Lord, and I confess...” etc.; likewise in turn they receive communion also from the chalice. But if a Bishop celebrates, he saith: “Priests, draw near”; and the priests, drawing near, say the prayer: “Lo, I draw near unto the Immortal King; forgive me, Holy Master, and bless, and impart unto me the precious, holy, and most pure Body (and for the Blood: the precious, holy, and life-creating Blood) of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ, for the remission of all my sins and unto life everlasting. Amen.”

[^210]: The Communion Hymn is called in Greek (*koinonikon* [κοινωνικόν]), unto which “Alleluia” is appended.

[^211]: With us the priest takes the chalice and turns with it, while the deacon only exclaims: “With the fear of God.”

[^212]: When the priest turns toward the people, saying: “With the fear of God,” this symbolically signifies the appearance of Jesus Christ unto His disciples, whom He blesses. But when he turns for the last time, blessing the people with the holy chalice, this signifies His Ascension from the Mount of Olives.

[^213]: Table of Preparation (*Mensa propositionis* = Πρόθεσις = *Zhertvennyk*).

[^214]: The Prayer Behind the Ambo signifies: a prayer read behind the ambo, because in ancient times the ambo stood in the midst of the church, and the priest read this prayer behind the ambo.

[^215]: The prayer: “O Lord, Who blessest them that bless Thee...” ought to be read with great contrition of heart and not rapidly.

[^216]: Antidoron (ἀντίδωρον) signifies a reciprocal gift. This bread was distributed among the ancient Christians after the common banquet (agape = feast of love) unto the poor faithful. For in ancient times those gifts which remained from the loaves of oblation at the holy Proskomedia were distributed unto the faithful *in memoriam agaparum* [in memory of the love-feasts], so that even those who did not receive communion might be partakers of the blessing.

[^217]: The hymn: “Only-begotten Son” (*Е҆диноро́дный Сы́не...*) was composed by Emperor Justinian in the 6th century (about the year 528).

[^218]: If several priests concelebrate, then the serving priest at the words: “and all the priestly order” turns toward the concelebrating priests and bows unto them.

[^219]: For these words pertain unto all priests, both the superior and the concelebrants.

[^220]: The words of consecration in the Liturgy of St. Basil the Great are these: “He gave it unto His holy disciples and Apostles, saying: Take, eat: this is My Body, which is broken for you for the remission of sins.” The choir: “Amen.” “He gave it unto His holy disciples and Apostles, saying: Drink of it, all of you: this is My Blood of the New Testament, which is shed for you and for many for the remission of sins.” The choir: “Amen.”

[^221]: On the day of Great Thursday, because at this Liturgy the institution of the Most Holy Eucharist is celebrated, there is sung: “Of Thy mystical supper, O Son of God, receive me today as a communicant; for I will not speak of the mystery unto Thine enemies, nor give Thee a kiss like Judas, but like the thief do I confess Thee: Remember me, O Lord, in Thy kingdom.”

[^222]: When the Feast of the Annunciation falls upon Great Thursday or Great Saturday, the Liturgy of St. Basil the Great is celebrated with Vespers.

[^223]: A full Liturgy is not served on weekdays of Great Lent, because the full Liturgy is a solemn and joyful commemoration of the entire mystery of human salvation, while the days of Great Lent are days of weeping, contrition, and repentance.

[^224]: The Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is attributed to St. Gregory the Dialogist (Pope of Rome), but the order of this Liturgy already existed earlier, and St. Gregory only gathered and arranged it. The Synod of Laodicea (in the 4th century) by Canon 49 decreed: “It is not fitting during the Forty Days to offer bread, except on Saturday and Sunday alone”; and the Synod in Trullo (in the 7th century) by Canon 52 determined: “On all days of the fast of the holy Forty Days, except Saturday and Sunday and the holy day of the Annunciation, let no other Liturgy take place but that of the Presanctified.”

[^225]: As many Lambs are prepared as there are Presanctified Liturgies to be celebrated during the week.

[^226]: Some priests, before the immersion into the holy chalice of the particle from the first Lamb, dip the particles of the other Lambs into the Holy Blood, and then place them upon the Diskos, and set them into the Tabernacle [Kyvot].

[^227]: On Wednesday and Friday of the first week of Great Lent the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is not celebrated according to the custom of our eparchy, but Vespers alone with prostrations; on other days, however, it is celebrated.

[^228]: On weekdays of Great Lent the dismissal of Vespers is as follows: “May Christ our true God, through the prayers of His most pure Mother, of the holy, glorious, and all-praised Apostles, of our holy Father Nicholas, Archbishop of Myra in Lycia, the Wonderworker (and of the saint whose temple it is, and whose day it is), of the holy and righteous Ancestors of God Joachim and Anna, and of all the saints, have mercy on us and save us, for He is good and loves mankind.”

[^229]: In Galicia these verses are sung thus: 1. The choir sings: “Let my prayer arise like incense before Thee, the lifting up of my hands as an evening sacrifice.” 2. The reader in the midst of the church (or the deacon upon the ambo): “Lord, I have cried unto Thee, hear me; attend to the voice of my supplication, when I cry unto Thee.” The choir: “Let my prayer arise...” 3. The reader: “Set a guard, O Lord, upon my mouth, and a door of enclosure about my lips.” The choir: “Let my prayer arise...” 4. The reader: “Incline not my heart unto words of malice, to devise excuses for sins.” The choir: “Let my prayer arise...” 5. The reader: “Let my prayer arise.” The choir finishes singing: “Like incense before Thee, the lifting up of my hands as an evening sacrifice.” During this singing the priest, standing before the holy Altar-Table, censes it, and thereafter the Table of Preparation [Prothesis], while the people and the singers kneel; after this the priest makes 3 prostrations with the prayer of St. Ephrem.

[^230]: If at the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts there should be people who wish to receive communion of the Holy Mysteries, the priest then puts particles of the Body of Christ into the holy chalice, and imparts communion unto them with the holy spoon in the customary manner. (See the Sluzhebnik [Liturgikon] at the Presanctified Liturgy.)

[^231]: For Tone 1: the kontakion of the Meeting of the Lord; for Tone 2: the kontakion of the Dormition; for Tone 3: the kontakion of the Protection; for Tone 4: the kontakion of the Nativity of the Mother of God; for Tones 5, 6, and 7: “O Protection of Christians” (*Заступнице*); for Tone 8: the kontakion of the Annunciation.

[^232]: Where the temple is of Christ or of the Mother of God, there in every case unconditionally at “Now and forever” the kontakion of the temple is sung, except those cases when there is an afterfeast of a Feast of the Lord or of the Mother of God, for then we cover it with the kontakion of the occurring feast.

[^233]: Where, however, the temple is of a Saint, there on feasts of Saints at “Now and forever” “O Protection of Christians” (*Заступнице*) is taken; but on Sundays and on weekdays it is concluded with the above-mentioned kontakia, except the above-mentioned afterfeasts.

[^234]: Festal antiphons are sung only on the very first day of the feast; during the afterfeasts, however, the typical antiphons are sung, but the Prokimenon, Heirmos, Communion Hymn, and dismissal of the feast are always taken during afterfeasts even until the Apodosis.

[^235]: If the patronal feast of a male or female Saint should occur on a Sunday, at the Liturgy we sing the entire service unchanged as for a Great Saint that hath occurred on a Sunday. (See Case III.)

[^236]: If the patronal feast of a male or female Saint should occur within a feast on a Sunday, at the Liturgy the service of the Sunday and of the temple is taken, and that of the afterfeast is omitted.

[^237]: For if a Polyeleos or Great Saint should occur on a weekday, then the service of the Oktoechos is entirely omitted, and at the Liturgy of that day the entire service is unto the Polyeleos Saint. The weekday service of the Oktoechos is likewise omitted during the forefeasts and afterfeasts (until the Apodosis) of Feasts of the Lord and of the Mother of God; likewise from Lazarus Saturday until the Sunday of All Saints, and then also at weekday Liturgies the weekday service unto the Angels, the Forerunner, the Cross, etc., is omitted.

[^238]: The daily antiphons are taken at weekday Liturgies only when a Minor or Middle Saint occurs on a weekday; but if any of these hath [stichera] at the Beatitudes, then the typical antiphons are taken.

[^239]: On Thursday: the troparion unto the Apostles and unto St. Nicholas; the kontakion unto the Apostles; “Glory” (*Сла́ва*): the kontakion unto Nicholas; “Now and forever”: as above.

[^240]: On Thursday we omit the troparion and kontakion unto St. Nicholas; but wherever there is a temple of St. Nicholas, we omit the troparion and kontakion unto the holy Apostles, and take them unto St. Nicholas and of intention.

[^241]: On Saturday the Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn are first of the Saint, then of the day. If there is an intention: the Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Saint, of the day, and so of the petition.

[^242]: According to the decree of the Przemyśl Typikon, the Epistle and Gospel in course are transferred only if the Great Saint hath an All-Night Vigil; otherwise not.

[^243]: According to this rubric the Liturgy is celebrated if the patronal feast of a male or female Saint falls on a weekday.

[^244]: See Vrabel, *Ustav* (Ungvár [Uzhhorod], 1890), p. III.

[^245]: In the week of the Myrrh-bearers all festal troparia are taken in alternation.

[^246]: [Epigraphic note: The typesetter inverted footnotes ** and ***. Note ** glosses the Paschal Communion Hymn, while note *** glosses the Paschal Kontakion.] “The Body of Christ” is the Communion Hymn of Pascha and is sung only on Sundays.

[^247]: “Though Thou didst descend into the grave” (*А҆́ще и҆ во гро́бъ*) is the kontakion of Pascha, which is taken only on Sundays, but not always.

[^248]: Among the Saints, “Fools-for-Christ” (*Yurodivii*) are called those who renounced all the goods of this world, renounced even the visible manifestation of their reason and intellect, and led a wandering life, enduring hunger and thirst, cold and heat with astonishing patience. *Izbornik*, Lviv, 1873, p. 479. In Greek a “Fool-for-Christ” is called *salos* [σαλὸς], which signifieth as much as *simplex* [simple], *stultus* [foolish], *fatuus* [silly]. *“Sub affectato stultitiae velo coelestem sapientiam suam occultasse leguntur in Actis Sanctorum S. Theodorus Salus, S. Andreas Salus.”* [They are read in the Acts of the Saints to have hidden their heavenly wisdom beneath an assumed veil of foolishness: St. Theodorus Salus, St. Andreas Salus.] Cf. Nilles, *Calendarium Manuale Utriusque Ecclesiae*, Innsbruck, 1879, Vol. I, p. 219.

[^249]: In a manner similar to this the holy Liturgy is celebrated if the patronal feast of a male or female Saint falls within a feast on a weekday.

[^250]: [Hungarian:] *“Halottakért való mise”* [Mass for the dead] and *“Halottak miséje”* [Mass of the dead].

[^251]: The Epistle and Gospel for each day are different.

[^252]: *Pannychis* [Παννύχις] = *vigilia* [vigil] = *officium pernox* [all-night office]; *pannychides* [παννυχίδες] = *vigiliae, officia nocturna* [vigils, night offices]; *pannychis agrypnete* [πανυχὶς ἀγρυπνητὴ] = *officium nocturnum* [night office] (= All-Night Vigil); which is performed throughout the whole night with watchful solicitude, attentively and devoutly. Cf. Nilles, *Calendarium Manuale Utriusque Ecclesiae*, Innsbruck, 1879, Vol. I, p. 55.

[^253]: And that in this manner: After “Blessed be the name of the Lord,” the priest exclaims: “Blessed is our God” (*Благословенъ Богъ нашъ*); “Holy God” (*Святы́й Бо́же*) is sung 3 times, and after this the cantor: “O Most-Holy Trinity,” etc., “Our Father,” and the exclamation. After the exclamation the priest censes, and the cantor sings: “With the spirits of the righteous.” After this the litany “Have mercy on us, O God” (*Помилуй насъ Боже*) (for the departed). From Thomas Sunday until the Apodosis of Pascha: in place of “Holy God” (*Святы́й Бо́же*) and “Eternal Memory,” we sing: “Christ is risen,” etc. The dismissal of the Liturgy of the departed, the blessing, and at the end the priest exclaims: “In blessed repose,” etc., unto which the cantor: “Eternal memory.”

[^254]: We have already stated that there is a twofold beginning: (α) ordinary and (β) extraordinary. The ordinary occurs before Vespers and Matins, both daily and Sunday, if they are served alone, but never before the holy Liturgy. The extraordinary occurs: a) Before the beginning of an All-Night Vigil; b) In Bright Week; and c) On all days of Pentecost, both at Vespers and at Matins. An All-Night Vigil hath an extraordinary beginning (“Glory to the holy,” etc. and “Come, let us worship” (*Прїиди́те поклони́мся*)) only if Small Vespers is served beforehand; if not, it hath the ordinary beginning. In Bright Week at Vespers the priest, vested in all vestments (sticharion and phelonion), censes and exclaims: “Blessed is our God,” “Christ is risen” (*Христосъ воскресе*) with the verses, and thus the Litany of Peace. At Matins, vested in like manner, he begins with the censer: “Glory to the holy,” “Christ is risen” (*Христосъ воскресе*) with the verses. On the remaining days of Pentecost (from Thomas Sunday until the Apodosis of Pascha) at Vespers: “Blessed is our God,” “Christ is risen” (*Христосъ воскресе*) (without verses), and straightway the Trisagion; at Matins: “Glory to the holy,” “Christ is risen” (*Христосъ воскресе*) (without verses), and straightway “Glory to God in the highest.”

[^255]: The antiphons of Pascha we sing also throughout the entire Bright Week, even unto New Sunday.

[^256]: At the daily antiphons, at the first antiphon there is the refrain: “By the prayers of the Mother of God, O Savior, save us”; at the second antiphon: “By the prayers of Thy saints, O Savior, save us”; and at the third antiphon: “Save us, O Son of God, Who wondrous art Thou in Thy saints, who sing unto Thee: Alleluia.”

[^257]: Among us, however, such a custom prevails in many places, that on all days the daily antiphons are taken, and on Sundays and in Pentecost the antiphons of Pascha: “Shout with jubilation unto the Lord,” etc., “God be bountiful unto us,” etc., and “Come, let us rejoice.”

[^258]: Certain Typika state that “Before Thy Cross” (*Кресту Твоему*) is sung only one day, that is, on the very feast itself, but in the afterfeast no longer.

[^259]: In the Week of the Veneration of the Cross on Monday, Wednesday, and Friday at the First Hour, in place of “Order my steps,” “Before Thy Cross” (*Кресту Твоему*) is taken.

[^260]: Among us on the very apodosis of the Exaltation of the Precious Cross we do not sing “Before Thy Cross,” but the “Trisagion.”

[^261]: In Galicia: on the feast of the “Procession of the Precious Wood of the Honorable and Life-Creating Cross” at Matins after the Great Doxology, when the cantor sings the Trisagion, the priest vested in full priestly vestments comes from the ambo unto the holy Altar-Table, and censes the holy Altar-Table and the precious Cross. After this he takes the precious Cross upon his head and goes around the holy Altar-Table through the northern doors out of the Sanctuary, preceded by candle-bearers and the deacon censing. And having come directly before the Royal Doors, he stands awaiting the end of the Trisagion. And as the Trisagion is being concluded, the priest exclaims: “Wisdom! Stand upright!” (*Премꙋ́дрость про́сти!*), unto which the cantor sings “Save, O Lord, Thy people.” And the priest comes unto the analogion, which is decorated, places upon it the precious Cross, and sings “Before Thy Cross we bow down in worship” 3 times, and he and the others kiss the holy Cross. During the kissing the stichera “Come, O ye faithful” are sung. After this follow two litanies: “Have mercy on us, O God” (*Помилуй насъ Боже*) and “Let us complete,” and the dismissal. After the Liturgy there is the blessing of water. And in the evening on that very day, after the dismissal of Vespers, the precious Cross is carried in by the priest vested in phelonion with candles and censer, while they sing: “Save, O Lord” or “Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross,” and it is placed upon the Altar-Table. (Among us: the holy Cross is not set out, but the blessing of water takes place.)

[^262]: If the Annunciation should occur on the feast of Pascha, then first the Gospel of Pascha is read by sections, during which they also ring the bells, and afterward the Gospel of the Annunciation as usual.

[^263]: *“Amen est vox hebraica, et interdum est affirmantis, quandoque est desiderantis, aliquando est etiam assentientis.”* [Amen is a Hebrew word, and at times is of one affirming, sometimes of one desiring, at other times even of one assenting.] Cf. Benedict XIV, *De SS. Missae Sacrificio*, Venice, 1767, p. 38. St. Isidore (*De Originibus*, Bk. 6, ch. *De Officiis*) saith that two words, “Amen” and “Alleluia,” were not translated into other languages on account of their sacredness: *“Tanto enim sacra sunt nomina, ut etiam Joannes in apocalypsi referat se spiritu revelante vidisse et audivisse vocem aquarum multarum et tonitruum validorum dicentium ‘Amen’ et ‘Alleluja’ ac per hoc sic oportet in terris utrumque dici, sicut in coelo resonant.”* [For so sacred are the names that even John in the Apocalypse relateth that he, by the Spirit revealing it, saw and heard the voice of many waters and mighty thunders saying ‘Amen’ and ‘Alleluia,’ and therefore it is fitting that both be spoken on earth just as they resound in heaven.] Cf. Benedict XIV, *loc. cit.*, p. 40.

[^264]: This custom (that is, the distribution of the blessed bread and anointing with the blessed oil) was instituted by the Church in place of the ancient love-feast (*in memoriam agaparum*), which took place after the night vigil following the Liturgy and concluded with the holy kiss (*philema hagion* [φίλημα ἅγιον]), according to the words of the holy Apostle Paul: “Salute one another with a holy kiss” (I Cor. 16:20).

[^265]: Among us the custom is that the cantor during the Myrovanije sings various festal songs, for example: “Rejoice, O Queen,” “O Who, Who Loves Nicholas,” or “The Son of God This Day”—which to collect and print in a single booklet would be a very necessary endeavor; for the people love these songs very much.

[^266]: In Bright Week even burials themselves are celebrated in white vestments.

[^267]: If the priest celebrates the “Liturgy of the departed” on All Souls’ Saturdays: he also completes the church rule for the departed; otherwise it is permitted to complete the daily rule, and celebrate the “Liturgy of the departed.”

[^268]: The Forefeast of the Nativity of Christ begins from the 20th of December.

[^269]: If the Nativity of Christ occurs on a Sunday: then between the Nativity of Christ and Theophany there is only one Sunday, on which at the same time the Circumcision of our Lord Jesus Christ is celebrated. In this case the Sunday after the Nativity is celebrated on Monday, that is, on the 26th day of December, when at the Liturgy we read the Epistle and Gospel of the Sunday after the Nativity, and thus of the Synaxis of the Most-Holy Virgin; and on the 1st day of January there is read the Prokimenon, Epistle, and Gospel of the Sunday before the Enlightenment [Theophany], and thus of the Circumcision.

[^270]: The Forefeast of Theophany begins from the 2nd of January.

[^271]: This typikon [order] was written by Bishop Basil Popovics of blessed memory, and circulated in our Eparchy on January 24, 1855, under No. 306.

[^272]: The procession around the church takes place thrice, with the exception of Great Friday (when the Plashchanytsia is carried around) and of Sunday Matins on Easter Sunday at midnight; for in these two cases it is necessary to encompass the church only once.

[^273]: Processions around the church take place from the right side unto the left, that is to say, after the manner of processions around the holy Altar-Table.

[^274]: At the Resurrection of Christ at midnight, when the circling around the church is performed only once, the Gospels are not read, but during the procession there is sung without fail the hymn: “Thy resurrection, O Christ Savior,” etc.

[^275]: The priest when administering the Holy Mysteries must keep before his eyes the following general rules: (α) At any time of day and night, if he is summoned to minister a Holy Mystery, he is to celebrate the Mystery without any delay, especially if necessity urgeth. — (β) If it should happen that he is in mortal sin, let him not celebrate the Mystery, but let him first confess his sins. If, however, there is no presbyter at that time, let him confess his sins before God in contrition of heart and thus celebrate the Mystery, and after this as soon as possible confess his sins. — (γ) When he ministereth the Holy Things, let him not minister without intention. The prayer of intention before ministering the Holy Mysteries he reciteth thus: “O God, Who of Thine unsearchable goodness hast established the Holy Mysteries, whereby man might restore unto himself the grace lost through sin, and of a child of the devil make himself a child of God and an heir of the kingdom of heaven: do Thou Thyself, the Worker of Thy works, perform through me this Mystery, which I desire to minister with such intention as Thy holy Church doth minister, and this in the name of the eternal Father, of the Son begotten of the Father before the ages, and of the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father and the Son. Amen.” — (δ) For the celebration of each Mystery the priest is to vest in epitrachelion and phelonion, except only the Mystery of Repentance, which in necessity may even be performed without an epitrachelion. — (ε) Let him read all the words whereby the Holy Mystery is effected, and the other prayers aloud for all to hear, and not silently in the mind, from the Trebnik, keeping before his eyes the words of St. John Chrysostom who saith: “Let there be in his heart contrition, in his lips confession, and in his deed all humility.” — (ϛ) Finally, let him minister no Mystery in private places, but only in church, except in case of necessity or incidental requirement, or by permission received from the Hierarch. See *Trebnik*, Lviv, 1873, p. 2.

[^276]: Even before Baptism, when a child is born, the Church intends to impart a blessing immediately unto the woman who hath given birth, and therefore doth not even await her departure from the house and arrival into the temple of the Lord; but thither unto the place of birth comes the priest, and glorifies God that a human being hath been born, and soothes by prayers the pains endured by her in childbirth, blessing her and the child born of her; and these prayers whereby this is done are called “molitvanky” [birth-prayers], but among us these are for the most part not in use.

[^277]: The sponsors are to be only two, that is to say: a man and a woman. Let them not be unbelievers, heretics (Calvinists, Lutherans, or schismatics), burdened with an excommunication or suspension, nor fornicators, drunkards, open evil-doers, not possessing sufficient reason, understanding the Symbol of Faith [Creed], nor monks or nuns.

[^278]: If it is necessary to baptize conditionally, the formula is spoken thus: “If he is not baptized, the servant of God is baptized,” etc.; or: “If this person is capable, he is baptized,” etc.; or: “If thou art alive, he is baptized,” etc.

[^279]: If an infant on account of necessity was baptized by a lay person: then the Priest, if he findeth any doubt concerning the form or concerning the matter at the baptism, must repeat the form conditionally, and perform all the rites; — but when there is no doubt concerning the matter and concerning the form: then he performeth only the rites themselves from the beginning unto the end. — On the contrary, however, Bishop Papp-Szilágyi of blessed memory stateth that the exorcisms are omitted and only the concluding prayers after baptism are performed: *“Dum in necessitate collatus est Baptismus absque exorcismis, si infans mortuus non est, exorcismi suppleri non debent, sed solum, quae post Baptismum juxta Rituale praescripta sunt, absolvantur”* [*“When in necessity Baptism hath been conferred without exorcisms, if the infant is not dead, the exorcisms ought not to be supplied, but only those things which are prescribed after Baptism according to the Ritual are to be fulfilled”*]. *Enchir. Juris Eccl. Orient.*, M. Varad. [Nagyvárad / Oradea], 1862, p. 86.

[^280]: The Bishop consecrates the Holy Myron [Chrism] on Great Thursday, and it is not permitted to use it for more than one year, and for a second year only by permission of the Ecumenical Roman Pontiff. When the pastor receives the Myron from the Bishop through the dean, he must immediately burn the remains of the old Myron upon a clean stone, and throw the ashes into the place designated for that purpose. (*Syn. Zamosc.* tit. III, §. 2, p. 83.)

[^281]: *“Ex hisce unctionibus solummodo unctio in fronte est essentialis, ceterae solummodo ad integritatem Sacramenti pertinent”* [*“Of these anointings only the anointing on the forehead is essential, the rest pertain only unto the integrity of the Sacrament”*]. Cf. Papp-Szilágyi, *op. cit.*, p. 383.

[^282]: If there are many communicants and the Blood of our Lord Jesus Christ is not sufficient: then among us the Chalice on the holy Altar-Table is replenished with wine and thus communion is increased. — This wine is not transubstantiated into the Blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, but is sanctified only through contact; for thus we read concerning this question: *“Contactu tamen illo vinum sanctitatem acquirit, et in alium superiorem statum sustollitur; — ut separatum a Corpore Christi, si id fieret, veneratione omni, non tamen ea, quae Corpus Christi colitur, dignum sit, quousque species illas vini, quibuscum in Corpus Christi infusum est, conservaverit. — Quod si quis in vinum majorem molem immiserit, et copia alterius vini species illae singulares evanuerint, sanctitas quoque illa et veneratio evanescet”* [*“Yet by that contact the wine acquires holiness, and is raised unto another higher state; — so that if it were separated from the Body of Christ, it would be worthy of all veneration, yet not of that wherewith the Body of Christ is worshiped, as long as it hath preserved those species of wine with which it was poured upon the Body of Christ. — But if anyone hath poured a greater volume into the wine, and by the abundance of the other wine those particular species have disappeared, that holiness also and veneration will disappear”*]. Leo Allatius, *Dissertatio de Missa Praesanctificatorum apud Graecos*, Cologne [Coloniae Agrippinae], 1548, ch. XVIII, p. 1590.

[^283]: The sick who are not in mortal danger must be communicated before all eating and drinking, and it is not permitted for them even to take medicine before eating; but for those who are in danger of death it is fitting to communicate them even after eating. — Unto those, however, who on account of continual or frequent vomiting are unable to retain anything of what is consumed, it is not possible to impart the Holy Eucharist lest dishonor occur; for such who are unable, — if they have truly cleansed their soul by holy confession, — a heartfelt desire for Holy Communion is sufficient. — Likewise it is not permitted to impart Holy Communion unto the insane, lest any indignity be brought upon the Holy Mysteries.

[^284]: From unfamiliar and strange persons one ought not to inquire thus: “Hast thou a husband, or a wife?” but thus: “Hast thou a sworn wife or husband?” For those living in adultery do not even consider this sin as a sin, — and will conceal it.

[^285]: During a jubilee, absolution is plenary, for at that time one may also absolve from reserved sins (*casus reservati* [*reserved cases*]).

[^286]: See this prayer in this booklet on page 195.

[^287]: See: *The Order of Consecration unto a Bishop* by Dr. Julian Pelesz, Lviv, 1885.

[^288]: The eagle-rug [*orletz*] = *szőnyeg rá varrt sassal* [*a rug with an eagle sewn onto it*].

[^289]: Nuptial crownings and marital blessings are forbidden: α) from November 14 unto the Feast of Theophany; β) from Meatfare Sunday unto Thomas Sunday; γ) from the Sunday of All Saints unto the Feast of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul; δ) from August 1 unto the Dormition of the Mother of God; and likewise on all fast days.

[^290]: Here let us note that certain priests so perform this rite that they do not place crowns upon the heads of the second-married.

[^291]: “Our help” with the verse: “Hear us, O Lord,” is taken after each prayer.

[^292]: *“Quinque sensuum unctiones esse habendas veluti essentiales, pedum vero et renum tantum integrales. Apud nos honestatis causa ‘unctio renum’ omittitur et ungitur pectus. Una unctio (in fronte) nonnisi in necessitate et sub conditione adhiberi debet cum obligatione repetendi unctiones in singulis sensibus, si moribundus postea respiret”* [*“The unctions of the five senses are to be regarded as essential, but those of the feet and loins only as integral. Among us, for reasons of modesty, the ‘anointing of the loins’ is omitted and the breast is anointed. A single anointing (upon the forehead) ought not to be administered except in case of necessity and conditionally, under the obligation of repeating the anointings upon the individual senses if the dying person should later revive”*]. Cf. Müller, *Theologia Moralis*, Vienna 1876, Tom. III, p. 417.

[^293]: According to the prescription of the Trebnik, even if there be several priests, nevertheless only one (and that: the presiding one) speaks the form and performs all 7 anointings; the remaining priests neither speak the form nor anoint. — Nevertheless in the older Trebniks it is so prescribed, that if there be several priests, each one anoints, but let each one anoint only one sense, and while anointing let him speak the corresponding form.

[^294]: *“Benedictiones consecrativae seu constitutivae (= Освященія) sunt tales, quibus aut personae aut res ad cultum divinum deputantur seu in esse sacro permanenter constituuntur, et nunquam ad usus profanos adhiberi possunt. V. g. paramenta sacerdotalia, calix. — Benedictiones invocativae vero seu Benedictiones simpliciter (= Благословенія) sunt tales, quibus benignitas Dei invocatur, ut personis vel rebus benedicendis aliquid boni tribuat, quin tamen statum mutent et stricte sacrae fiant, propterea ad usus profanos, honestos tamen converti possunt. V. g. benedictio domus, esculentorum, agrorum”* [*“Consecrative or constitutive blessings (= Consecrations) are such whereby either persons or things are deputed unto divine worship or permanently constituted in a sacred state, and can never be employed for profane uses. E. g. priestly vestments, the chalice. — Invocative blessings, however, or blessings simply so called (= Blessings) are such whereby the bounty of God is invoked, that He may bestow some good upon the persons or things to be blessed, without however changing their status or becoming strictly sacred; therefore they can be converted unto profane, yet honorable, uses. E. g. the blessing of a home, of edible foodstuffs, of fields”*]. Cf. Müller, *Theologia Moralis*, loc. cit., p. 505.

[^295]: Unto this prayer, if the child be alive, he adds also this addition: “And bless the child born of her,” etc.; but if the child be not found among the living, then he omits this.

[^296]: But if the child hath died, then the actions and prayers belonging unto it are completely omitted.

[^297]: During this singing the Priest censes.

[^298]: In the Trebnik there is also the Trisagion, etc., but among us it is omitted.

[^299]: In the Trebnik, after “Blessed is our God,” there are also the Trisagion, etc., Psalm 90, and the Litany of Peace.

[^300]: Here takes place the Little Litany.

[^301]: According to the rubric of the Trebnik, after the stichera of John of Damascus there are also the Little Litany, the Kontakion, the Ikos, and the “Bulgarian Beatitudes.”

[^302]: The Trebnik prescribes to read after the exclamation: “Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ,” etc., but among us that doth not take place.

[^303]: In Bright Week through Saturday inclusive there is no other dismissal except the Sunday dismissal, and that in all cases without exception, even at the Holy Mysteries.

[^304]: At a burial from Thomas Sunday unto Ascension the usual rite is taken; only instead of the Trisagion there is sung: “Christ is risen” (*Христосъ воскресе*); instead of: “It is truly meet” (*Досто́йно є҆́сть*) and “More honorable,” “The Angel cried” (*Ангелъ вопіяше*) and “Shine, shine.” Instead of: “Memory eternal,” “Christ is risen.”

[^305]: Between a “Panikhida” and a “Parastas” there is such a difference, that a Panikhida is a “night office of the departed” (*officium nocturnum Defunctorum* [*night office of the Dead*]), while a Parastas is a “commemorative office of the departed” (*officium commemorativum Defunctorum* [*commemorative office of the Dead*]). In the early centuries of the Church, Christians held vigils either in church, awaiting a great feast, or by the remains of the dead. And corresponding unto this, all these services were called *παννυχίδες* == *vigiliae* == *officium pernox* [*all-night service*]. Now only the vigil by the departed is called by the Greek word “*παννυχίς*,” while the vigil in church on great feasts is called by the simple word “All-Night Vigil”; among the Greeks, however, it is called: “*παννυχὶς ἀγρυπνητή*” or “*ἀγρυпνία ὁλονύκτιος*” [*all-night vigil*].

[^306]: According to the typikon of the Eastern Church, the commemoration of the departed takes place on all Saturdays of the entire year, especially however on All Souls Saturdays. — Besides this, among us there are also the “third-day” [*tretyny*], that is, commemoration on the third day after departure in memory of the three-day Resurrection of Christ; there are also the “ninth-day” [*devyatyny*] and “fortieth-day” [*sorochyny*], that is, commemoration on the ninth and fortieth day, even as the Israelites did in the wilderness after the departure of Moses.

[^307]: Commemorations for the departed do not take place: 1) from the Nativity of Christ unto Theophany; 2) in the first week of the Great Fast; 3) in Bright Week; 4) on Sundays and on great feasts. Trebnik, p. 539.

[^308]: This Sanctification of Water is celebrated also on the Eve of Theophany, namely: at the Liturgy or at Vespers, as was stated on page 252.

[^309]: “Blessed be the name of the Lord” (*Буди имя Господне*) is sung at the conclusion of the Sanctification of Water.

[^310]: Procession [*Proiskhozhdeniye*] == bringing forth [*vynos*] == circuit [*obkhod*] == *processio* [*procession*] == *пρόοδος* [*procession*]; — that is, this feast was so celebrated in Constantinople, that they carried the holy Cross forth from the imperial palace unto the Great Church of Hagia Sophia, and at it celebrated the sanctification of water; then they made a circuit with that Cross during 15 days through the streets of the city, and afterwards carried it back again unto the imperial palace. That rite was celebrated in order to entreat God for help for the infirm, whose number in this month was great.

[^311]: On the way the Gospels are read. See page 260.

[^312]: Among us this solemn blessing of candles is not celebrated.

[^313]: The Tetrapod is placed before the erected Cross or the trench excavated for it.

[^314]: At the laying of the stone into the trench, some are accustomed to say this: “This church is founded unto the glory of the great God,” etc., and likewise they are accustomed to pour oil upon the stone with the words: “Blessed and marked be this place unto a house of prayer,” etc.

[^315]: There is a distinction between the Blessing and the Consecration of a new church. The Blessing of a new church is performed by a Priest, whereas the Consecration is performed by the Bishop himself. — For this latter rite see the Trebnik, p. 533.

[^316]: But if there be the Holy Liturgy, then after the internal sprinkling of the church the Priest, having returned from the church doors unto the Altar-Table, immediately proclaims: “Again and again, with compunction having bowed our knees, let us pray to the Lord.”

[^317]: Priestly vestments are blessed either all together or all individually one by one. For these latter see the Trebnik.

[^318]: The blessing of houses is performed among us annually on the Eve of Theophany after the Blessing of Water.

[^319]: In some places the custom is to bore holes in the four walls in the midst of or beneath the depictions of the cross, into which the Priest places depictions of the four Evangelists [printed erratically as *Ецангелистовъ*] written on paper, and having anointed them with blessed oil, drives them firmly into the walls with wooden pegs.

[^320]: The week from Pascha Sunday unto the Sunday of All Saints usually begins with Sunday; from here they begin from Monday.

[^321]: See Popel, *Liturgika*, Lviv, 1863, p. 334.

[^322]: It is celebrated among us at the 6th hour of the evening on the Eve of the Feast.

[^323]: The Priest concludes the kontakia with "Alleluia" (excepting the 1st and the last); and the ikoi with "Rejoice, thou who art full of grace, who in thy dormition leavest us not." These endings the cantors likewise always sing repetitively. In some places after the 6th ikos they sing the troparion of the feast.

[^324]: See *Sobranie Akafistov* [Collection of Akathists], Lviv, 1864.

[^325]: See *Krestnyi Misiatsoslov* [Cross Menologion], 1890, "Yet Something Concerning the Typikon," p. XXXVI.

[^326]: Among us at "Lord, I have cried" (*Господи воззвахъ*) censing takes place only if a deacon is present; otherwise it is omitted.

[^327]: But even at "The Theotokos and Mother of Light" (*Богоро́дицу и҆ Ма́терь Свѣ́та*) censing is performed only if there is a deacon; otherwise not.

[^328]: The colors yellow (*sárga*) and blue (*kék*) are excluded. These do not belong unto ecclesiastical vestment colors. Sacred Congregation of Rites, 23 February 1839; 17 March 1837.

[^329]: Bright vestments can also be of variegated color (*tarka*); but nevertheless upon them the bright, that is, white color must be primary and predominant.

[^330]: In Galicia at the Descent of the Holy Spirit green vestments are used. See Pelesz, *Pastoral Theology*, p. 387.

[^331]: Symeon of Thessalonica unto this question: *"Quid sibi volunt alba Pontificis ornamenta et quid rubra per Quadragesimam denotant?"* [What do the white ornaments of the Bishop mean, and what do the red denote throughout Lent?] — thus answered: *"Alba sunt illa propter gratiae puritatem et lucem, saepius autem jejuniorum tempore assumuntur purpurea, eo quod peccatores oporteat lugere et propter occisum pro nobis Jesum Christum"* [They are white on account of the purity and light of grace, but more often in time of fasts purple vestments are assumed, because it behoves sinners to mourn, and on account of Jesus Christ slain for us]. Goar, *Euchologion Graecorum*, loc. cit., p. 59.

[^332]: Cf. Lonovics, *Egyh. Archaeologia* [Ecclesiastical Archaeology], Vienna, 1857, Vol. I, p. 1.

[^333]: That is, Latin.

[^334]: That is, Greek, Syro-Chaldean, Armenian, Coptic, Ethiopian, Slavonic, and Romanian.

[^335]: *De Missae Sacrificio*, Book II, ch. 14, Venice, 1767, p. 30. The Latin text cited in the body reads: *"Constantem, firmamque esse disciplinam, ne Missae idioma mutetur, et si mutet lingua vernacula; sed eo sermone Missa celebretur, quo celebrata est ab initio, etiam si illa lingua exoleverit apud vulgus, ejusque peritiam viri docti dumtaxat habeant"* [It is constant and firm discipline that the language of the Mass be not changed, even if the vernacular tongue change; but the Mass be celebrated in that speech in which it was celebrated from the beginning, even if that tongue hath fallen into disuse among the common people, and only learned men possess knowledge of it].

[^336]: *De Missae Sacrificio*, Book II, ch. 5, loc. cit., p. 28. The Latin text cited in the body reads: *"Sed non in eo controversiae cardo vertitur. Illud quaeritur, an si, quae vernacula fuit, vulgari alio sermone in populum invecto doctorum dumtaxat virorum facta fuerit lingua, divina Officia et Missa in novum vulgi sermonem convertenda sint. Id vero est, a quo semper abhorruit et abhorret Catholica Ecclesia"* [But the hinge of controversy turns not upon that. The question is whether, if that which was vernacular hath become the language of learned men alone after another common speech hath been introduced among the people, the divine Offices and Mass ought to be converted into the new speech of the common people. That indeed is something from which the Catholic Church hath always shrunk and shrinks].

[^337]: See *Psalter*, Lviv, 1871, p. 150.

[^338]: Instead of this letter [the big yus, Ѫ], we use the letter "э".

[^339]: The "boundary letter" signifies how many days in that year Pascha is distant from March 21. For these letters signify these numbers thus: а=1, б=2, в=3, г=4, д=5, е=6, ж=7, ѕ=8, з=9, и=10, ї=11, к=12, л=13, м=14, н=15, о=16, п=17, р=18, с=19, т=20, ꙋ (ѵ)=21, ф=22, х=23, ѿ=24, ц=25, ч=26, ш=27, щ=28, ъ=29, ы=30, ь=31, ѣ=32, ю=33, э=34, ѧ=35. That is, for example: if the boundary letter is "л" [printed as "и" in the original text], then in that year Pascha is 13 days distant from March 21, that is, it will be April 3.

[^340]: "The Lunar Cycle" (*cyclus lunae*) is a revolution of 19 years, after the passage of which the new moons and full moons return unto their original days; that number which shows which year a given year is in the number of a single cycle is called the "golden number" (*numerus aureus*). For example, the year 1891 will have the golden number 8 (и), which signifies only that it is the 8th year in this lunar cycle; the year 1902 will have 19, and 1903 anew 1; — that is, here a second lunar cycle will begin anew.

[^341]: It is customary in the Church to designate the 7 days of the week by 7 letters (А. В. Г. Д. Є. Ѕ. З.), — and this designation proceeds from year to year, so that in one year the turn comes unto one letter, and in another year unto another letter, that by it the Sunday day may be designated. — These letters are called "Sunday letters = *litterae dominicales* = *vrucilito*." If the year had 365 days, this revolution would conclude in seven years. But since after every 3 common years one day is added in a leap year, the order of Sunday letters cannot conclude except after 28 years (4 × 7 = 28). This cycle is called the "solar cycle." The Sunday letter begins from the 1st day in March.

[^342]: This Indiction was composed during the time of Emperor Arcadius (395–408) by an Egyptian monk named Anianus, but it was not immediately received in the West, until in the time of Pope Leo I, when a dispute arose concerning the day of Pascha, Victor of Aquitaine introduced the 532-year cycle of Anianus in the year 457. See Pelesz, *Pastoral Theology*, p. 319.

[^343]: The first Indiction began in the year 344, the second in 876, the third in 1408, and further the fourth begins in the year 1904.

[^344]: It is also called the "Table of Irenaeus," or "Key of Alpha."

[^345]: In our illustration (See Table III), instead of red letters we use capital letters.

[^346]: See Note, page 287.
